Betti Numbers of Gaussian Excursions in the Sparse Regime
Gugan Thoppe t1, m1 label=e1]gugan.thoppe@gmail.com, Sunder Ram Krishnan t1, m2 label=e2]eeksunderram@gmail.com

Abstract
Random field excursions is an increasingly vital topic within data analysis in medicine, cosmology, materials science, etc. This work is the first detailed study of their Betti numbers in the so-called ‘sparse’ regime. Specifically, we consider a piecewise constant Gaussian field whose covariance function is positive and satisfies some local, boundedness, and decay rate conditions. We model its excursion set via a Čech complex. For Betti numbers of this complex, we then prove various limit theorems as the window size and the excursion level together grow to infinity. Our results include asymptotic mean and variance estimates, a vanishing to non-vanishing phase transition with a precise estimate of the transition threshold, and a weak law in the non-vanishing regime. We further obtain a Poisson approximation and a central limit theorem close to the transition threshold. Our proofs combine extreme value theory and combinatorial topology tools.

1
Introduction

Key insights into the behaviour of a random field can be inferred from its excursion set, the sub-domain where the field value exceeds some level. Hence, functionals of excursion sets have generated considerable interest both in theory
[
5
,
8
]
and in practice
[
6
,
29
,
40
]
. In this paper, we provide limit theorems, together with rates of convergence, for Betti numbers
\{\beta_{k}\}
of Gaussian excursions in the so-called ‘sparse’ regime. This work is the first study of the sparse regime behaviour of Betti number of random field excursions of any kind. Intuitively, for a topological space,
\beta_{0}
is the number of components while
\beta_{k},
for
k\geq 1,
is the number of
(k+1)-
dimensional ‘holes’. A
(k+1)-
dimensional hole is loosely the hollow region that opens up when the interior of a solid
(k+1)-
dimensional object is removed. Specifically, in three dimensions,
\beta_{1}
is the number of ‘tunnels’ (regions through which one can poke one’s hand),
\beta_{2}
is the number of ‘voids’ (regions that look like the interior of a tennis ball, doughnut, etc.), while other higher order Betti numbers are all zero. Thus, our results statistically quantify the topology of high-level Gaussian excursions.

The majority of recent work on Gaussian excursions concerns what is known as the ‘thermodynamic’ regime. Even within these, the major focus of most papers (
[
14
,
27
,
31
]
, etc.) has been on a different set of functionals called the Lipschitz-Killing curvatures (LKCs), which include as special cases the Euler-Poincaré characteristic (EPC) and the
d-
dimensional volume. LKCs do provide useful topological information, but not at the level of individual Betti numbers. In relation to Betti numbers, the only paper of which we are aware is
[
35
]
. There, a weak law and a (multivariate) CLT have been shown for a generic class of (quasi-) local statistics of spin models on Cayley graphs. By using a suitable random cubical complex to model Gaussian excursions on
\mathbb{Z}^{d},
one can apply these results to Betti numbers in the thermodynamic regime. As can be seen in Section 2.2.3 there, it is required that the underlying spin model be subcritical and the covariance function decay exponentially. We point out that, unlike this fast decay assumption, the results in
[
14
,
27
,
31
]
and related papers require only that the covariance function be integrable (see also Remark

R
5

).

In
[
3
,
4
]
, existence probabilities of connecting paths and holes in high level excursions of Gaussian fields on the continuum have been studied. However, as noted in those papers, it is difficult to handle such questions in the continuum and those works obtained estimates for these probabilities only at the level of large deviations. Separately, in
[
32
,
36
]
, existence results on topology of level sets of stationary Gaussian fields have been provided. It is not clear whether any of those results can easily be translated to excursion sets.

In contrast, and as highlighted previously, we study Gaussian excursions in the sparse regime (see Remark

R
6

for a formal description of sparse/thermodynamic regime). Our results are explicit and we obtain them under significantly weaker assumptions; in fact, in some cases, these assumptions are tight. We achieve these by focussing on Gaussian fields on discrete parameter spaces and using a novel approach of modelling their excursion sets via simplicial complexes (generalization of graphs). The latter enables use of suitable combinatorial approximators to study the Betti numbers of these excursions, a trick motivated by studies in the random simplicial complex literature
[
28
,
30
,
21
,
22
,
24
]
. We then obtain precise distributional limit theorems for these approximators by employing the Stein-Chen approach
[
18
]
, together with Slepian’s lemma and Savage’s multivariate Gaussian tail estimates
[
37
]
. We finally transfer these results to Betti numbers, building upon the topological ideas from
[
22
,
24
]
. A detailed description of all these notions is given in Section
2
.

Figure 1:
Possible realization of our Gaussian field
X
on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
and its piecewise constant extension to
\mathbb{R}^{2}
(colors are used only to provide a clearer illustration).

Figure 2:
Possible realizations of the excursion set
\mathcal{A}(n;u)
in two and three dimensions with non-trivial first Betti numbers(created using voxelbuilder).

Formally, we consider a piecewise constant random field
X:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
with the below properties.

1.

\{X_{\bar{t}}\}_{\bar{t}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
is a zero mean, stationary,
\|\cdot\|_{1}-
isotropic, discrete parameter Gaussian field with the covariance sequence
\{\rho_{q}\}_{q\geq 0}.
That is,

(a)

\mathbb{E}[X_{\bar{t}}]=0,
for all
\bar{t}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d},
and

(b)

\textnormal{Cov}[X_{\bar{t}},X_{\bar{t}^{\prime}}]=\rho_{{}_{\|\bar{t}-\bar{t}^{\prime}\|_{1}}},
for all
\bar{t},\bar{t}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}.

2.

For
\bar{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\backslash\mathbb{Z}^{d},

X_{\bar{t}}
equals the value of the above discrete field at the lattice point closest to
\bar{t}
in the
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
norm (if there is more than one closest point, we pick the one that is the largest in lexicographical ordering).

Its excursion set of interest to us is

\mathcal{A}(n;u):=\{\bar{t}\in[-n-1/2,n+1/2)^{d}:X_{\bar{t}}\geq u\}

(1.1)

where
n\geq 0
is the window size parameter and
u\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
is the excursion level; here,
\mathbb{R}_{>0}
is the set of positive real numbers. See Figures
1
and
2
for an illustration.

To study the topology of
\mathcal{A}(n;u),
as mentioned above, we model it using a simplicial complex defined as follows. Let
\Gamma_{n}:=\{\bar{t}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}:\|\bar{t}\|_{\infty}\leq n\}
be the discrete window of side length
(2n+1)
and let
\mathscr{B}_{\infty}(\bar{t},r):=\{\bar{t}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\|\bar{t}-\bar{t}^{\prime}\|_{\infty}\leq r\}.

Definition 1.1
.

The random Čech complex
\mathcal{K}(n;u)
on the excursion set
\mathcal{A}(n;u)
is the simplicial complex with vertex set
\mathcal{F}_{0}:=\{\bar{t}\in\Gamma_{n}:X_{\bar{t}}\geq u\},
and
\sigma\subset\mathcal{F}_{0}
is a face of
\mathcal{K}(n;u)
if

\bigcap_{\bar{t}\in\sigma}\mathscr{B}_{\infty}\left(\bar{t},\tfrac{1}{2}\right)\neq\emptyset.

Observe that the vertex set of
\mathcal{K}(n;u)
is random, while its faces are decided using a deterministic rule based on the
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}-
distance between the vertex pairs. This places it in the family of random geometric complexes. It is called a Čech complex since each of its face is chosen based on the mutual intersection of suitable balls centered at the vertices in that face.

An alternative way to model
\mathcal{A}(n;u)
could have been the complex
\hat{\mathcal{K}}(n;u)
defined as follows.

Definition 1.2
.

The random Vietoris-Rips complex
\hat{\mathcal{K}}(n;u)
on the excursion set
\mathcal{A}(n;u)
is the simplicial complex with vertex set
\mathcal{F}_{0}:=\{\bar{t}:\bar{t}\in\Gamma_{n},X_{\bar{t}}\geq u\},
and
\sigma\subset\mathcal{F}_{0}
is a face of
\hat{\mathcal{K}}(n;u)
if

\mathscr{B}_{\infty}\left(\bar{t}_{i},\tfrac{1}{2}\right)\cap\mathscr{B}_{\infty}\left(\bar{t}_{j},\tfrac{1}{2}\right)\neq\emptyset

for every pair
\bar{t}_{i},\bar{t}_{j}\in\sigma.

This is also a geometric complex. It is called the Vietoris-Rips complex because its faces are decided based on pairwise intersections. Usually, a Čech and Vietoris-Rips complex are different as the rules for defining them differ. However, in our setup, using the fact that they are defined on a lattice, it turns out that
\mathcal{K}(n;u)=\hat{\mathcal{K}}(n;u);
see Proposition
3.1
for details. Thus, according to need, we shall view
\mathcal{K}(n;u)
sometimes as a Čech and at other times as a Vietoris-Rips complex. Taking one of the views, let
\beta_{n,k}(u)
be the
k-
th Betti number of
\mathcal{K}(n;u);
Figures
2
and
3
show a few representative examples. With details given in Section
2
, we point out that the coefficients to define these Betti numbers can be from either a field or
\mathbb{Z}.

Figure 3:
Possible subcomplexes of
\mathcal{K}(n;u).
The left image shows how a part of our excursion set may appear (grey color) when
d=2
and the simplicial complex associated with it; because of the top left formation, the first Betti number of this subcomplex is
1.
The right image shows a subcomplex, the second Betti number of which equals
1;
this may appear only when
d\geq 3.
(Source: Wikipedia).

Assumptions for different results.

Our key results can now be summarized as follows. The different assumptions are listed in Table
1
; these are in addition to the conditions on
\{\rho_{q}\}
imposed by the fact that the covariance function of
X
must be positive definite. For
k
such that
1\leq k<d,
define the constants
\tau_{k},

a_{k},
and
b_{k}
using

\tau_{k}:=\frac{\tbinom{d}{k+1}}{(2\pi)^{k+1}}\frac{[1+(2k+1)\rho_{2}]^{2k+\tfrac{1}{2}}}{[1-\rho_{2}]^{k+\tfrac{1}{2}}},\;\;a_{k}:=\frac{k+1}{1+(2k+1)\rho_{2}},\text{ and }b_{k}:=k+1.

(1.2)

Separately, let

\tau_{0}:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}},\;\;a_{0}:=\tfrac{1}{2},\text{ and }b_{0}:=\tfrac{1}{2}.

(1.3)

For
n\geq 0,

k
such that
0\leq k<d,
and
u\in\mathbb{R}_{>0},
define
\lambda_{n,k}(u)
using

\lambda_{n,k}(u):=\tau_{k}\;(2n+1)^{d}u^{-2b_{k}}\exp[-a_{k}u^{2}].

(1.4)

Finally, let
\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{\infty}
be the set of sequences in
\mathbb{R}_{>0}.

Our first result gives a precise asymptotic formula for
\mathbb{E}[\beta_{n,k}(u)].
Here, and elsewhere,
O(\cdot)
denotes the Big-O notation and it characterizes a function’s behaviour as
n\to\infty.

Theorem 1.3

(Asymptotic Mean of
\beta_{n,k}(u_{n})
)
.

Let
k
be such that
0\leq k<d
and
\{u_{n}\}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{\infty}
be such that
\lim_{n\to\infty}u_{n}=\infty.
Suppose
\Cr{a:r0Asm},\Cr{a:r1Asm},\Cr{a:rCAsm},
and
\Cr{a:ComC}(k)
hold. Then,

\left|\frac{\mathbb{E}[\beta_{n,k}(u_{n})]}{\lambda_{n,k}(u_{n})}-1\right|=O\left(\frac{1}{u_{n}^{2}}+\frac{1}{n}\right).

The constants involved in the
O
notation depend on
k.

From (
1.4
), note that the limit of the sequence
\{\lambda_{n,k}(u_{n})\}
can be either zero, a constant, or infinity, depending on the growth rate of
\{u_{n}\}.
An alternative way to view this is to let

u_{n}=\sqrt{\frac{1}{a_{k}}\left[\log[\tau_{k}(2n+1)^{d}]-b_{k}\log\left[\frac{\log[\tau_{k}(2n+1)^{d}]}{a_{k}}\right]+\nu_{n}\right]},

(1.5)

where
\{\nu_{n}\}
is any real sequence that ensures
\{u_{n}\}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{\infty}
and
\lim_{n\to\infty}u_{n}=\infty.
Then,
\{\lambda_{n,k}(u_{n})\}
has the above three limits precisely when
\lim_{n\to\infty}\nu_{n}
is either
\infty,
some constant, or
-\infty.
Our next result gives the asymptotic behaviour of the
k-
th Betti number in these three scenarios.

Theorem 1.4

(Asymptotic Distributional Behaviour of
\beta_{n,k}(u_{n})
)
.

Let
k
be such that
0\leq k<d
and
\{u_{n}\}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{\infty}
be such that
\lim_{n\to\infty}u_{n}=\infty.
In the different regimes dictated by the limit of
\{\nu_{n}\},
or equivalently of
\{\lambda_{n,k}(u_{n})\},
the
k-
th Betti number behaves as follows.

1.

Vanishing regime
(\nu_{n}\to\infty
or
\lambda_{n,k}(u_{n})\to 0
): If
\Cr{a:r0Asm},\Cr{a:r1Asm},\Cr{a:rCAsm},
and
\Cr{a:ComC}(k)
hold, then

1-\mathbb{P}\{\beta_{n,k}(u_{n})=0\}=O\left(e^{-\nu_{n}}\left[\frac{\log n}{\log n+\nu_{n}}\right]^{b_{k}}\right).

2.

Poisson regime
(\nu_{n}\to\log[\tfrac{1}{\lambda}]
or
\lambda_{n,k}(u_{n})\to\lambda,
for some
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
): If
\Cr{a:r0Asm},\Cr{a:r1Asm},\Cr{a:rCAsm},\Cr{a:ComC}(k),
and
\Cr{a:PoiG}
hold, then
\beta_{n,k}(u_{n})\Rightarrow\textnormal{Poi}(\lambda);
in particular, we have

\|\beta_{n,k}(u_{n})-\textnormal{Poi}(\lambda)\|_{\textnormal{TV}}=O\left(\frac{\log n}{n}\sum_{q=1}^{2dn}\rho_{q}+\frac{\log\log n}{\log n}+|e^{-\nu_{n}}-\lambda|\right).

3.

Non-vanishing regime (
\nu_{n}\to-\infty
or
\lambda_{n,k}(u_{n})\to\infty
): If
\Cr{a:r0Asm},\Cr{a:r1Asm},\Cr{a:rCAsm},\Cr{a:ComC}(k),
and
\Cr{a:CLTG}
hold, then

\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\textnormal{Var}[\beta_{n,k}(u_{n})]}{\lambda_{n,k}^{2}(u_{n})}=0,

(1.6)

\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\{\beta_{n,k}(u_{n})=0\}=0,

(1.7)

and

\frac{\beta_{n,k}(u_{n})-\mathbb{E}[\beta_{n,k}(u_{n})]}{\lambda_{n,k}(u_{n})}\overset{i.p.}{\longrightarrow}0.

(1.8)

Additionally, there exists a constant
C^{\nu}_{k}>0,
depending on
k,
such that, if
C\in(0,C^{\nu}_{k})
and
\nu_{n}\geq-C\log(2n+1)^{d},
then

\dfrac{\beta_{n,k}(u_{n})-\mathbb{E}[\beta_{n,k}(u_{n})]}{\sqrt{\lambda_{n,k}(u_{n})}}\Rightarrow N(0,1),

(1.9)

where
N
denotes the Gaussian distribution. The explicit formula for
C^{\nu}_{k}
is given in (
3.20
).

This result proves that the
k-
th Betti number undergoes a vanishing to non-vanishing phase transition as its asymptotic mean changes from zero to infinity. It also shows a weak law in the non-vanishing regime. In addition, close to the transition threshold, the result gives a Poisson approximation and a CLT. Loose rates of convergence for (
1.6
) and (
1.9
) are given in their proofs.

We now provide some remarks on our assumptions, model setup, and results.

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}_{1}}
.

We assume
\|\cdot\|_{1}-
isotropy in our model mainly in order to simplify our estimates and computations. With other forms of isotropy, we strongly believe that results with a similar flavor to ours should hold, but will require some more involved calculations; see Section
7
.

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}_{2}}
.

For all our results, we need the covariance function to be positive. This is because it is unclear at present how to apply our Stein-Chen approach when negative covariances are involved; in particular, we are then unable to establish a result such as Theorem
2.11
. For the specific case of
\beta_{0},
however, this assumption can be relaxed; see
[
18
]
for the outline.

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}_{3}}
.

We also need that
\Cr{a:ComC}(k)
holds. For Gaussian fields on the continuum with covariance functions given by
\exp[-\|\bar{t}-\bar{t}^{\prime}\|_{2}^{q}],

\|\bar{t}-\bar{t}^{\prime}\|_{2}^{-q},
etc. with
q>0,
this condition places a lower bound on the distance at which these fields need to be sampled so that our results hold. However, note that this condition is natural in ARMA models. It may be possible to eliminate this assumption, but the computations would then become involved. See Section
7
for details.

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}_{4}}
.

For the vanishing and Poisson regime results, we require the covariance function to also satisfy
\Cr{a:PoiG},
the so-called ‘Berman’ condition. This condition is known to be tight for Poisson approximation; see
[
18
, Remark 1]
for example. It is worth noting that this condition holds even for Gaussian fields that are long-range dependent and also for Gaussian free fields on
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
with
d\geq 3

[
12
]
.

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}_{5}}
.

The non-vanishing regime results including the CLT need the stronger
\Cr{a:CLTG}
condition, which basically means that covariances should be summable in the domain dimension
d.
This is a common assumption in the Gaussian excursions literature
[
14
,
26
,
27
,
9
]
. However, it is unclear whether it is tight.

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}_{6}}
.

In all the three regimes determined by the limit of
\nu_{n},
the average vertex degree (i.e.,
\mathbb{E}[\mbox{number of edges}]/\mathbb{E}[\mbox{number of vertices}]
) of our Čech complex asymptotically vanishes. In random graphs terminology, this places all our key results in the ‘sparse’ regime. The asymptotics in
[
14
,
27
,
31
,
35
]
, from the perspective of our setup, loosely translates to keeping
u
fixed and letting only
n
increase to infinity. It is easy to see that the average vertex degree would asymptotically then be a constant. This positions the results of these studies concerning LKCs and Betti numbers in what is usually referred to as the thermodynamic regime.

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}_{7}}
.

From Theorem
1.4
and (
1.5
), in terms of
u_{n}^{2},

\frac{1}{a_{k}}\left[\log[\tau_{k}(2n+1)^{d}]-b_{k}\log\left[\tfrac{\log[\tau_{k}(2n+1)^{d}]}{a_{k}}\right]\right]

(1.10)

is the transition threshold for non-triviality of the
k-
th Betti number. The lower order
\log\log
term in the above expression is due to the sharp multivariate Gaussian tail estimates given in
[
37
]
. By using only the large deviation approach, such precision cannot be obtained.

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}_{8}}
.

In
[
41
]
and
[
17
]
, studies of random cubical complexes have been carried out. In
[
17
]
, a strong law and a CLT for Betti numbers have been derived; while the strong law holds for generic distributions, the CLT needs independence. It may be possible to translate these results to Gaussian excursions, but then they would again apply in the thermodynamic regime. As opposed to cubical complexes, we model
\mathcal{A}(n;u)
here using simplicial complexes, since a richer set of results are available for the latter class.

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}_{9}}
.

From the point of view of the random simplicial complexes literature, our work extends recent advances. We introduce a new random simplicial complex model. While being in the family of geometric complexes, it facilitates the study of random field excursions. However, the in-built dependence amongst the faces in our model contrasts it with most existing random complexes such as Linial-Meshulam complexes
[
28
,
30
]
, clique complexes
[
21
,
23
]
, and random geometric complexes
[
11
]
. In all the latter models, independence plays a crucial role in both their definitions and their study.

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}_{10}}
.

Despite the dependence, however, our results share several similarities with the sparse regime phenomena in random geometric complexes studied in
[
22
,
24
]
; the presence of faces in their model is decided based on the Euclidean proximity of the vertices that are generated as an IID sequence, while the asymptotics is in terms of the coupling between the number of vertices and the proximity thresholds. As in these works, the behaviour of our Betti numbers is dictated by the simplest, minimal subcomplexes that generate them. Furthermore, for each Betti number, we sequentially observe a vanishing, Poisson, and non-vanishing behaviour. We also see that the lower order Betti numbers appear earlier than the higher order ones as the growth rate of the excursion levels is lowered. Mathematically, we mean that, as
k
increases from
0
to
d-1,
the phase transition threshold decreases; this follows from Remark

R
7

and the fact that
a_{k}
monotonically increases with
k.
In fact, a consequence of Theorem
1.3
is that

\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\mathbb{E}[\beta_{n,k_{1}}(u_{n})]}{\mathbb{E}[\beta_{n,k_{2}}(u_{n})]}\to\infty,

whenever
k_{1}<k_{2}
and
\{u_{n}\}
is such that
\lim_{n\to\infty}u_{n}=\infty.

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}_{11}}
.

If we assume independence in our model (
\rho_{q}=0\;\forall q\geq 1
), our calculations simplify significantly. However, the basic nature of our results will remain the same, except that the associated constants, and hence regimes, will be different. For a more detailed discussion, see Section
7
.

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}_{12}}
.

As compared to the estimate for variance of Betti numbers in Theorem
1.4
, we conjecture that

\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\textnormal{Var}[\beta_{n,k}(u_{n})]}{\lambda_{n,k}(u_{n})}=1.

The evidence for this follows from Lemma
3.9
.
1
and Remark
5.9
.

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}_{13}}
.

In our CLT result, the additional requirement that
\nu_{n}\geq-C\log(2n+1)^{d}
restricts the validity of the theorem to a regime close to the phase transition threshold. Outside this regime, the CLT for Betti numbers does not follow from that of the approximators that we use throughout this work. To obtain better approximators necessitates, as of now, an equivalent of Theorem
2.11
when negative covariances are also involved. But, presently, it is unclear how to obtain such a result. Some more details about this are given in Section
7
.

We end this section by describing how the rest of the paper is structured.

Structure of the Paper
: The following section provides all the requisite background material concerning the topological and probabilistic aspects of this work. In Section
3
, we outline our proofs for the key results. Specifically, we first state all our major intermediate results and then, assuming them to true, prove our key results. The topological portions of these intermediate results are proved in Section
4
, while the remaining ones are proved in Sections
5
,
6
, and the Appendix. We end with a discussion on future directions in Section
7
.

2
Background

This section begins with a brief overview of relevant notions from simplicial homology with a focus on Betti numbers; this is based on
[
21
]
and the references therein. After that, we give the description of a non-trivial cycle and, in the context of a special simplicial complex called the clique complex, recall some useful results concerning it from
[
21
,
22
]
. These results are crucially used in Section
4
later to obtain the approximators for our Betti numbers, mentioned before. Following all of that, we discuss few pertinent results from probability theory. First, we give Savage’s multivariate Gaussian tail estimates from
[
37
]
. Here, we also provide the Savage condition under which this bound holds. We then describe Slepian’s lemma which relates tail probabilities to covariance relations. We finally state a special case of the Stein-Chen method, which is the same one that was used in
[
18
]
. This method gives a bound on the total variation distance between a sum of indicator random variables and a Poisson random variable having the same mean.

2.1
Topological Background

A key object of study across this work is a simplicial complex—a generalization of a graph to higher dimensions. Specifically, an (abstract) simplicial complex
\mathcal{K}
on a vertex set
V
is a collection of non-empty subsets of
V
such that for any
\sigma\in\mathcal{K},
if
\sigma^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
and
\sigma^{\prime}\subset\sigma,
then
\sigma^{\prime}\in\mathcal{K}
as well. That is,
\mathcal{K}
is closed under the subset operation. The elements of
\mathcal{K}
are called faces and the dimension of a face
\sigma
is
|\sigma|-1,
where
|\cdot|
denotes cardinality. The dimension of
\mathcal{K}
itself is the maximum over the dimension of all its faces. The
k-
skeleton of
\mathcal{K}
is the simplicial complex made up of all the faces of
\mathcal{K}
with dimension
k
or less.

Given a simplicial complex
\mathcal{K},
one way to study its topology (or shape) is via its Betti numbers
\{\beta_{k}(\mathcal{K})\}_{k\geq 0}.
These are described next, first intuitively and then formally.

Intuitive Description of Betti Numbers:
Imagine the
k-
dimensional faces of
\mathcal{K},
or
k-
faces in short, to be solid
k-
dimensional objects. Then, a
k-
cycle in
\mathcal{K}
is a collection of its
k-
faces whose union is ‘topologically equivalent to’ the boundary of a solid
(k+1)-
dimensional object. If it is not the boundary of any subset of
(k+1)-
faces in
\mathcal{K},
then that
k-
cycle, in fact, represents a
(k+1)-
dimensional hole. Finally,
\beta_{0}(\mathcal{K})
is one more than the number of ‘independent’
1-
holes in
\mathcal{K},
while, for
k\geq 1,

\beta_{k}(\mathcal{K})
is exactly the number of ‘independent’
(k+1)-
dimensional holes. In this sense, the simplicial complex in the left image of Figure
3
has two
1-
cycles, but only the top left cycle represents a
2-
hole. This description extends the intuitive picture given at the outset.

Formal Description of Betti Numbers:
Let
\sigma
be a
k-
face made up of the vertices
v_{0},\ldots,v_{k}.
An
orientation
of
\sigma
is an ordering of its vertices and is denoted by
(v_{0},\ldots,v_{k}).
Two orderings induce the same orientation if and only if they differ by an even permutation of the vertices. We shall assume henceforth that each face in
\mathcal{K}
is assigned a specific orientation, i.e., ordering. Let
\mathbb{F}
be
\mathbb{Z}
or some field. Then, a simplicial
k
-chain is a formal sum of oriented
k-
faces, i.e.,
\sum_{i}c_{i}\sigma_{i},
with
c_{i}\in\mathbb{F}.
The
k-
chain group
\mathfrak{C}_{k}(\mathcal{K})
is the free Abelian group generated by all
k
-chains, i.e.,

\mathfrak{C}_{k}(\mathcal{K}):=\left\{\sum_{i}c_{i}\sigma_{i}:c_{i}\in\mathbb{F},\sigma_{i}\in\mathcal{F}_{k}(\mathcal{K})\right\},

where
\mathcal{F}_{k}(\mathcal{K})
is the set of all
k-
faces in
\mathcal{K}.
Clearly, if
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{Z},
then
\mathfrak{C}_{k}(\mathcal{K})
is a
\mathbb{Z}
-module, and if
\mathbb{F}
is a field, then
\mathfrak{C}_{k}(\mathcal{K})
is a
\mathbb{F}
-vector space. Separately, set
\mathfrak{C}_{-1}=\mathbb{F}.
Now, for
k\geq 0,
define the boundary operator
\partial_{k}:\mathfrak{C}_{k}\to\mathfrak{C}_{k-1}
first on each
k-
simplex using

\partial_{k}\left((v_{0},\ldots,v_{k})\right)=\sum_{i=0}^{k}(-1)^{i}(v_{0},\ldots,\hat{v}_{i},\ldots,v_{k})

and then extend it linearly on
\mathfrak{C}_{k}.
Here,
\hat{v}_{i}
implies that the vertex
v_{i}
is to be omitted. It is easy see that
\partial_{k-1}\circ\partial_{k}=0
for all
k\geq 1,
i.e., boundary of a boundary is zero. The
k-
th
boundary space
, denoted by
\mathcal{B}_{k},
is the image of
\partial_{k+1}
and the
k-
th
cycle space

\mathcal{Z}_{k}
is the kernel of
\partial_{k}
. The elements of
\mathcal{Z}_{k}
are called
k-
cycles
, while the elements of
\mathcal{B}_{k}
are called
k-
boundaries
. The
k
-th homology group
\mathcal{H}_{k}
is defined to be the quotient group
\mathcal{H}_{k}=\mathcal{Z}_{k}/\mathcal{B}_{k}.
Clearly,
\mathcal{H}_{k}
is also a
\mathbb{F}
-module or a
\mathbb{F}
-vector space depending on whether
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{Z}
or
\mathbb{F}
is a field. Finally,
\beta_{0}(\mathcal{K})=\textnormal{rank}(\mathcal{H}_{0})+1,
while
\beta_{k}(\mathcal{K})=\textnormal{rank}(\mathcal{H}_{k})
when
k\geq 1.

We now give a couple of definitions including that of a non-trivial cycle (NTC) and then state two of its useful properties from
[
21
,
22
]
in the context of what is known as a clique complex.

Continuing with the above notions, for a chain
\gamma\in\mathfrak{C}_{k},
let
[\gamma]:=\{\gamma^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{C}_{k}:\gamma^{\prime}-\gamma\in\mathcal{B}_{k}\}
be its equivalence class with respect to
\mathcal{B}_{k}.
Then, it is easy to see that
\mathcal{H}_{k}=\{[\gamma]:\gamma\in\mathcal{Z}_{k}\}.

For
\sigma\in\mathcal{K},
let
\textnormal{vsupp}(\sigma)
be its vertex support. For
\gamma=\sum_{i}c_{i}\sigma_{i},
let
\textnormal{vsupp}(\gamma):=\bigcup_{i:c_{i}\neq 0}\textnormal{vsupp}(\sigma_{i}).
A chain
\gamma
is a
k-
NTC if
\gamma\in\mathcal{Z}_{k}\setminus\mathcal{B}_{k},
and it has minimal vertex support if
|\textnormal{vsupp}(\gamma)|\leq|\textnormal{vsupp}(\gamma^{\prime})|
for each
\gamma^{\prime}\in[\gamma].
Clearly,
\beta_{0}(\mathcal{K})
is one more than the maximal number of independent
0-
NTCs, while, for
k\geq 1,

\beta_{k}(\mathcal{K})
is the maximal number of independent
k-
NTCs. Based on this, given a set
\{\mathcal{C}\}
of isolated induced subcomplexes of
\mathcal{K},
one can show that
\beta_{k}(\mathcal{K})=\sum_{\mathcal{C}}\beta_{k}(\mathcal{C}).

Separately, for a vertex
v\in\mathcal{K},
its link
\textnormal{lk}(v):=\{\sigma\in\mathcal{K}:v\notin\sigma,\text{ but }\sigma\cup\{v\}\in\mathcal{K}\},
while its star, denoted
\textnormal{st}(v),
is the smallest simplicial complex containing
\{\sigma\in\mathcal{K}:v\in\sigma\}.
Clearly, both the link and star of a vertex are themselves simplicial complexes.

The
(k+1)-
dimensional cross-polytope is the convex hull of the
2k+2
points
\{\pm e_{1},\ldots,\pm e_{k+1}\},
where
\bar{e}_{1},\bar{e}_{2}
etc. are the standard basis vectors. The boundary of this polytope can be represented using a
k
-dimensional simplicial complex. Let
O_{k}
denote the
1-
skeleton of this complex. This is the graph on the above
2k+2
points where an edge is present between a pair if and only if the
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}-
distance between them is
1.

Given a graph
G\equiv(V,E),
a set of vertices
v_{1},\ldots,v_{k}\in V
is said to form a clique if
\{v_{i},v_{j}\}\in E
for all
v_{i},v_{j}.
The associated clique complex
\mathscr{X}(G)
is the simplicial complex made up of all the subsets of
V
that form a clique in
G.

Lemma 2.1
.

[
22
, Lemma 3.4]

Let
\mathscr{X}(G)
be the clique complex associated with a graph
G.
Let
k\geq 0
and
\gamma
be a
k-
NTC of
\mathscr{X}(G).
Then,
|\textnormal{vsupp}(\gamma)|\geq 2(k+1).
If
\textnormal{vsupp}(\gamma)=2(k+1),
then the
1-
skeleton of the induced subcomplex of
\mathscr{X}(G)
restricted to
\textnormal{vsupp}(\gamma)
is isomorphic to
O_{k}.

Lemma 2.2
.

[
21
, Lemma 5.2]

Let
\mathcal{K}
be a simplicial complex. For
k\geq 1
and
F
being some set of
k-
faces, let
\gamma=\sum_{\sigma\in F}c_{\sigma}\sigma
be a
k-
NTC in
\mathcal{K}
with minimal vertex support and
c_{\sigma}\neq 0
for all
\sigma\in F.
Then, for any
v\in\textnormal{vsupp}(\gamma),

\gamma\cap\textnormal{lk}(v):=\sum_{\sigma\in F\cap\textnormal{st}(v)}c_{\sigma}\partial_{k}(\sigma)
is a
(k-1)-
NTC in
\textnormal{lk}(v)\cap\{\sigma\in\mathcal{K}:\sigma\subseteq\textnormal{vsupp}(\gamma)\}.

Remark 2.3
.

Consider the setup as in Lemma
2.2
. For
v\in\textnormal{vsupp}(\gamma),
define
\gamma\cap\textnormal{st}(v)
to be
\sum_{\sigma\in F:v\in F}c_{\sigma}\sigma.
Then, it is easy to see that
\gamma\cap\textnormal{lk}(v)=\partial_{k}(\gamma\cap\textnormal{st}(v)).
Also, since
\gamma
is a
k-
cycle, it follows that
v\notin\textnormal{vsupp}(\gamma\cap\textnormal{lk}(v)).

2.2
Probabilistic Background

Another key element across our computations is the tail probability of a multivariate Gaussian random vector. A tight estimate for this has been given in
[
37
]
. We state this result below after introducing some relevant notations.

Henceforth, we use the bar notation for vectors such as
\bar{Y},\bar{t},
etc. To refer to their
l
-th coordinate we use
\bar{Y}(l),\bar{t}(l),
etc. All our vectors are row vectors and we denote their transpose using
\top.
All vector inequalities mean that they hold coordinate wise. Specifically, for any random vector
\bar{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{i}
and
\bar{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{i},
by
\{\bar{X}\geq\bar{u}\},
we mean the event
\{\bar{X}(1)\geq\bar{u}(1),\ldots,\bar{X}(i)\geq\bar{u}(i)\}.
The determinant of a matrix
M
is denoted by
|M|,
while
|r|
denotes the absolute value for any
r\in\mathbb{R}.
Lastly, we use
\mathds{1}
to denote both the indicator random variable as well as the indicator function.

Lemma 2.4
.

[
37
, (I), (II)]

Let
i\geq 1
and let
\bar{Y}\in\mathbb{R}^{i}
be a zero mean multivariate Gaussian random vector with positive definite covariance matrix
M.
Then, for each
\bar{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{i}
such that
\bar{\Delta}:=\bar{u}M^{-1}>0
holds, the so called ‘Savage condition’, we have

1-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j,\ell=1}^{i}\frac{M_{j\ell}[1+\delta_{j\ell}]}{\bar{\Delta}(j)\;\bar{\Delta}(\ell)}\leq\dfrac{\mathbb{P}\{\bar{Y}\geq\bar{u}\}}{(2\pi)^{-i/2}|M|^{-1/2}\exp[-[\bar{u}M^{-1}\bar{u}^{\top}]/2]\left[\prod_{j=1}^{i}\bar{\Delta}(j)\right]^{-1}}\leq 1,

(2.1)

where
M_{j\ell}
is
j\ell-
th entry of
M^{-1},
and
\delta_{j\ell}=1
if
j=\ell,
and
0
otherwise.

To get elegant closed form expressions for tail probabilities using the above result, it is important that the covariance matrix be ‘nice’. Often, this will not be the case in our computations. To deal with the same, we shall be using the following comparison inequality.

Lemma 2.5

(Slepian’s lemma)
.

Let
i\geq 0.
Let
\bar{Y},\bar{Z}\in\mathbb{R}^{i}
be multivariate Gaussian random vectors such that
\mathbb{E}[\bar{Y}]=\mathbb{E}[\bar{Z}],

\textnormal{Var}[\bar{Y}(j)]=\textnormal{Var}[\bar{Z}(j)]
for all
j,
and
\textnormal{Cov}[\bar{Y}(j),\bar{Y}(\ell)]\geq\textnormal{Cov}[\bar{Z}(j),\bar{Z}(\ell)]
for all
j,\ell
with
j\neq\ell.
Then, for any
\bar{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{i},
we have
\mathbb{P}\{\bar{Y}\geq\bar{u}\}\geq\mathbb{P}\{\bar{Z}\geq\bar{u}\}.

The proof of this result follows from
[
2
, Theorem 2.3]
by substituting
h(\bar{x}(1),\bar{x}(2),\ldots,\bar{x}(i))=\prod_{j=1}^{i}\mathds{1}[\bar{x}(j)\geq\bar{u}(j)],
where
h
is as defined there. The only catch is that this function is not smooth. But this can be easily overcome by approximating the indicators with smooth increasing functions.

We prove both the Poisson as well the CLT portions of Theorem
1.4
using the Stein-Chen method. As is the case here, this method is useful when we are dealing with limit distributions of a sum of dependent indicator random variables. To apply this method, a bound on the total variation distance between the above mentioned sum and a Poisson random variable having the same mean is required. We give one such bound in Theorem
2.9
. In order to be able to apply this bound to excursions of Gaussian random vectors, an additional technical result is required. This is given in Theorem
2.11
, the proof of which is along the lines given in
[
18
, Theorem 2.2]
.

Definition 2.6
.

Let
Y,Z
be
\mathbb{R}-
valued random variables. The total variation distance between
Y
and
Z
is
\|Y-Z\|_{\textnormal{TV}}:=\sup_{R\subseteq\mathbb{R}}|\mathbb{P}\{Y\in R\}-\mathbb{P}\{Z\in R\}|.

Remark 2.7
.

If
Y
and
Z
are integer valued random variables defined on the same probability space, then
\|Y-Z\|_{\textnormal{TV}}\leq\mathbb{P}\{Y\neq Z\};
see
[
15
, pg 129]
.

Remark 2.8
.

\|\textnormal{Poi}(\lambda_{1})-\textnormal{Poi}(\lambda_{2})\|_{\textnormal{TV}}\leq|\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}|;
see

[
1
, Corollary 3.1]
.

Theorem 2.9
.

[
18
, Theorem 2.1]

(Stein-Chen method)

Let
\Gamma
be a finite index set. For
i\in\Gamma,
let
\Gamma_{i}:=\Gamma\setminus\{i\}.
Let
S=\sum_{i\in\Gamma}\mathds{1}_{i}
and
\lambda=\mathbb{E}[S],
where
\{\mathds{1}_{i}\}_{i\in\Gamma}
are some indicator random variables. For each
i,
let there be a probability space with indicator random variables
\{\zeta_{ji}\}_{j\in\Gamma}
and
\{\eta_{ji}\}_{j\in\Gamma_{i}}
defined on it such that

\mathscr{L}(\zeta_{ji}:j\in\Gamma)=\mathscr{L}(\mathds{1}_{j}:j\in\Gamma),

and

\mathscr{L}(\eta_{ji}:j\in\Gamma_{i})=\mathscr{L}(\mathds{1}_{j}:j\in\Gamma_{i}\;|\;\mathds{1}_{i}=1),

where
\mathscr{L}(\cdot)
denotes the distribution function. Then, for
\mathcal{U}_{i}=\sum_{j\in\Gamma}\zeta_{ji}
and
\mathcal{V}_{i}=\sum_{j\in\Gamma_{i}}\eta_{ji},

\|S-\textnormal{Poi}(\lambda)\|_{\textnormal{TV}}\leq\frac{1-e^{-\lambda}}{\lambda}\sum_{i\in\Gamma}\mathbb{E}[\mathds{1}_{i}]\;\mathbb{E}|\mathcal{U}_{i}-\mathcal{V}_{i}|.

In addition, if there exists a partition
\Gamma_{i}=\Gamma_{i}^{+}\cup\Gamma_{i}^{-}\cup\Gamma_{i}^{0}
with
\zeta_{ji}\leq\eta_{ji}
a.s. for
j\in\Gamma_{i}^{+},
and
\zeta_{ji}\geq\eta_{ji}
a.s. for
j\in\Gamma_{i}^{-},
then

\|S-\textnormal{Poi}(\lambda)\|_{\textnormal{TV}}\leq\frac{1-e^{-\lambda}}{\lambda}\left[\sum_{i\in\Gamma}[\mathbb{E}[\mathds{1}_{i}]]^{2}+\sum_{i\in\Gamma}\sum_{j\in\Gamma_{i}^{+}\cup\Gamma_{i}^{-}}|\textnormal{Cov}[\mathds{1}_{i},\mathds{1}_{j}]|+\sum_{i\in\Gamma}\sum_{j\in\Gamma_{i}^{0}}\left[\mathbb{E}[\mathds{1}_{i}\mathds{1}_{j}]+\mathbb{E}[\mathds{1}_{i}]\mathbb{E}[\mathds{1}_{j}]\right]\right].

Remark 2.10
.

For ease of use here, the above result is stated slightly differently from the original version given in
[
18
, Theorem 2.1]
.

Theorem 2.11
.

Let
j,k\geq 0.
Let
\bar{Y}_{i},
for
0\leq i\leq j,
be a random vector in
\mathbb{R}^{k}
so that
(\bar{Y}_{0},\ldots,\bar{Y}_{j})
is multivariate Gaussian with
\textnormal{Cov}[\bar{Y}_{0}(\ell_{1}),\bar{Y}_{i}(\ell_{2})]\geq 0
for all
0\leq i\leq j
and
1\leq\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\leq k.
Then, for any
\bar{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{k},
there exists a probability space with
\mathbb{R}^{k}-
valued random vectors
\{\bar{Z}_{i}:1\leq i\leq j\}
and
\{\bar{Z}^{\prime}_{i}:1\leq i\leq j\}
such that

\mathscr{L}(\bar{Z}_{i}:0\leq i\leq j)=\mathscr{L}(\bar{Y}_{i}:0\leq i\leq j),

\mathscr{L}(\bar{Z}^{\prime}_{i}:0\leq i\leq j)=\mathscr{L}(\bar{Y}_{i}:0\leq i\leq j\;|\;\bar{Y}_{0}\geq\bar{u}),

and, for all
0\leq i\leq j,1\leq\ell\leq k,

\bar{Z}_{i}(\ell)\leq\bar{Z}^{\prime}_{i}(\ell)\text{ a.s.}

The latter implies that
\mathds{1}[\bar{Z}_{i}\geq\bar{u}]\leq\mathds{1}[\bar{Z}^{\prime}_{i}\geq\bar{u}]
a.s. for all
0\leq i\leq j.

Proof.

Let
g_{\bar{u}}:\mathbb{R}^{k}\to\mathbb{R}
be the map
g_{\bar{u}}(\bar{y})=\mathds{1}[\bar{y}\geq\bar{u}].
Clearly,
g_{\bar{u}}
is monotonically increasing in its arguments (with respect to coordinate-wise partial order). Let
h:\mathbb{R}^{(j+1)k}\to\mathbb{R}
be an arbitrary increasing function in the above sense.
Then, from
[
20
, Corollary 3]
,

\mathbb{E}[g_{\bar{u}}(\bar{Y}_{0}(1),\ldots,\bar{Y}_{0}(k))\,h(\bar{Y}_{0}(1),\ldots,\bar{Y}_{0}(k),\ldots,\bar{Y}_{j}(1),\ldots,\bar{Y}_{j}(k))]\geq\\
\mathbb{E}[g_{\bar{u}}(\bar{Y}_{0}(1),\ldots,\bar{Y}_{0}(k))]\mathbb{E}[h(\bar{Y}_{0}(1),\ldots,\bar{Y}_{0}(k),\ldots,\bar{Y}_{j}(1),\ldots,\bar{Y}_{j}(k))].

(2.2)

Hence, we have

\mathbb{E}[h(\bar{Y}_{0}(1),\ldots,\bar{Y}_{0}(k),\ldots,\bar{Y}_{j}(1),\ldots,\bar{Y}_{j}(k))\;|\;\bar{Y}_{0}\geq\bar{u}]\geq\\
\mathbb{E}[h(\bar{Y}_{0}(1),\ldots,\bar{Y}_{0}(k),\ldots,\bar{Y}_{j}(1),\ldots,\bar{Y}_{j}(k))].

The desired result now holds from the equivalence of conditions (i) and (iv) in
[
25
, Theorem 1]
(under the coordinate-wise partial order).
∎

3
Outline of Proof of Key Results

Here we first state all our major intermediate results and then prove Theorems
1.3
and
1.4
. Proofs of these intermediate results are given later in Sections
4
,
5
,
6
, and the Appendix; the page numbers are noted near the statements. The result here either needs none or only a subset of the assumptions from Table
1
. While
\Cr{a:rCAsm}
is never needed in its entirety, various weaker implications of it are used at different times. These are stated in Table
2
.

\begin{cases}1=1\\[8.61108pt]

\Cr{a:r1Asm}
and
\Cr{a:r2LAsm}

Table 2:
Weaker Implications of Assumption
\Cr{a:r1Asm}
and
\Cr{a:rCAsm}.

Understanding the statistical behaviour of Betti numbers is not straightforward. This is because they, being rank of some space, are not nice enough combinatorial objects to be handled directly. Hence, as remarked in Section
1
, the trick is to use good approximators. We provide these in Theorem
3.3
. But to see the motivation behind them, we initially discuss few relevant properties of the Betti numbers of our Čech complex
\mathcal{K}(n;u),
which we establish separately.

First we show that the Čech and Vietoris-Rips complexes from Definitions
1.1
and
1.2
are equivalent. The statement is given below and the proof follows from Lemma
4.3
.

Proposition 3.1
.

Let
n\geq 0
and
u\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}.
Then,
\mathcal{K}(n;u)=\hat{\mathcal{K}}(n;u).

An immediate and an important consequence of the above result and Definition
1.2
is that its
1-
skeleton, the underlying graph, completely characterizes
\mathcal{K}(n;u).
In that,
\sigma
is a face in
\mathcal{K}(n;u)
if and only if the vertices in
\sigma
form a clique in the
1-
skeleton of
\mathcal{K}(n;u).
In other words,
\mathcal{K}(n;u)
is the clique complex associated with its
1-
skeleton.

Henceforth, we shall say that the
k-
th Betti number is non-trivial if it is at least
2
when
k=0,
and at least
1
when
k\geq 1.
Then, from Proposition
3.1
, and Lemma
2.1
along with the discussions above it on the relationship between Betti numbers and NTCs, we right away have that every induced subcomplex of
\mathcal{K}(n;u)
with non-trivial
k-
th Betti number has at least
2k+2
vertices. Note that the induced subcomplex whose
1-
skeleton is isomorphic to
O_{k}
has non-trivial
\beta_{k}.
Hence, it follows that the minimal induced subcomplex having non-trivial
\beta_{k}
must be the one that is isolated, has
2k+2
vertices, and whose
1-
skeleton is isomorphic to
O_{k}.
Extending these facts to our setup, we prove the following additional characteristic of induced subcomplexes with non-trivial
\beta_{k}.
Its proof is via induction and follows from Theorem
4.4
.

Theorem 3.2
.

In every induced subcomplex
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\mathcal{K}(n;u)
with non-trivial
k-
th Betti number, where
0\leq k<d,
there exist at least
2k+2
vertices with pairwise
\|\cdot\|_{1}
-distances at least
2.

In general, the
1-
skeleton of a minimal induced subcomplex with non-trivial
k-
th Betti number can either be
\|\cdot\|_{1}-
isometric to
O_{k}
or not; Figure
4
gives an example for each of these cases when
d=3
and
k=1.
Keeping in mind this observation, Figure
2
, and the well known topological fact, that the
k-
th Betti number is bounded from above by the number of
k-
faces, we now introduce the different terms with which we approximate the Betti numbers. Let

\displaystyle\check{S}_{n,k}(u)

\displaystyle:=\begin{cases}\text{number of isolated vertices in $\mathcal{K}(n;u),$}&\hskip 15.00002pt\text{if $k=0,$}\\[4.30554pt]
\parbox{270.00041pt}{number of subgraph components in the $1-$skeleton of $\mathcal{K}(n;u)$ that are isomorphic and $\|\cdot\|_{1}-$isometric to $O_{k},$
}&\hskip 15.00002pt\text{if $1\leq k<d;$}\end{cases}

and

\displaystyle\check{N}_{n,k}(u)

\displaystyle:=\begin{cases}0,&\hskip 15.00002pt\text{if $k=0,$}\\[4.30554pt]
\parbox{270.00041pt}{number of subgraph components in the $1-$skeleton of
$\mathcal{K}(n;u)$ that are isomorphic but not $\|\cdot\|_{1}$-isometric to $O_{k},$
}&\hskip 15.00002pt\text{if $1\leq k<d.$}\end{cases}

Further, for
0\leq k<d,
to deal with non-minimal subcomplexes that contribute to the
k-
th Betti number, let
\check{L}_{n,k}(u)
be the number of subgraphs in the
1-
skeleton of
\mathcal{K}(n;u)
that are isomorphic to a graph
G\in\mathcal{P}_{k},
where
\mathcal{P}_{k}
is as specified below. With
\Gamma_{2k+1}
defined as above Definition
1.1
, let
\mathcal{G}_{k}
be the geometric graph on
\Gamma_{2k+1}
with respect to
\mathscr{B}_{\infty}(\cdot,\tfrac{1}{2});
i.e., an edge is present between two vertices whenever the
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}-
distance between them equals
1.
Now, if
k\geq 2,
let

\mathcal{P}_{k}:=\{\text{Subgraphs }G\equiv(V,E)\subseteq\mathcal{G}_{k}:G\text{ is connected, }\text{ elements of $V$ are ordered }\\
\text{lexicographically, }|V|\geq 2k+3,\;\exists\text{ $2k+2$ vertices in $V$ with pairwise}\\
\text{$\|\cdot\|_{1}$-distances $\geq 2,$ $\exists$ \text{unique $(k+1)-$sized subset of $V$ that forms a clique in $G$}}\};

(3.1)

whereas, if
k=1,
let

\mathcal{P}_{1}:=\{\text{Subgraphs }G\equiv(V,E)\subseteq\mathcal{G}_{1}:G\text{ is connected, }\text{ elements of $V$ are ordered }\\
\text{lexicographically, }|V|\geq 5,\;\text{ $\exists\;4$ vertices in $V$ with pairwise $\|\cdot\|_{1}$-distances $\geq 2$}\};

(3.2)

and, lastly, if
k=0,
let

\mathcal{P}_{0}:=\{\text{Subgraphs }G\equiv(V,E)\subseteq\mathcal{G}_{0}:G\text{ is connected, }\\
\text{ elements of $V$ are ordered }\text{lexicographically, }|V|=2\}.

(3.3)

Loosely,
\mathcal{P}_{k}
is the set of subgraphs to which each
k-
face, of an induced subcomplex with non-trivial
\beta_{k},
can be extended into. For
1\leq k<d,
the idea is similar to
[
22
, Fig. 1]
; the difference is, while there the fact that each subgraph has
2k+3
vertices is harnessed, we additionally exploit the property that there is a further subset of
2k+2
vertices with pairwise
\|\cdot\|_{1}-
distances at least
2.

Figure 4:
A few examples of minimal induced subcomplexes with non-trivial
\beta_{1}
when
d=3.

Using the above notions, the aforementioned approximators for Betti numbers of
\mathcal{K}(n;u)
are the lower and upper bounds given below. Its proof is via induction and follows from Theorem
4.7
.

Theorem 3.3
.

Let
u\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
and
k
be such that
0\leq k<d.
Then,

\check{S}_{n,k}(u)\leq\beta_{n,k}(u)\leq\check{S}_{n,k}(u)+\check{N}_{n,k}(u)+{}_{,k}\,\check{L}_{n,k}(u),

where
{\Cr{c:Betti}}_{,k}=1
when
k=0
or
2\leq k<d,
while
{\Cr{c:Betti}}_{,1}
is an universal upper bound on the number of edges in a graph
G\in\mathcal{P}_{1}.

Remark 3.4
.

The constant
{\Cr{c:Betti}}_{,1}
in the above result differs for the same reasons as discussed below
[
22
, Fig. 3]
. But, roughly,
\check{L}_{n,1}(u)
needs to be scaled suitably for it be a valid upper bound for the number of edges and subsequently for the first Betti number.

We now elaborate on how we use these approximators or bounds to prove our key results. The core idea is to show that, as
n
and
u
become large,
\check{N}_{n,k}(u)
and
\check{L}_{n,k}(u)
become negligible compared to
\check{S}_{n,k}(u)
and that the latter more or less determines
\beta_{n,k}(u).
At this point, it is worth noting that the two terms
\check{S}_{n,k}(u)
and
\check{N}_{n,k}(u)
are not simple combinatorial objects themselves. In that, these terms count components and the isolation condition, enforced thereby, is not easy to deal with directly. We overcome this difficulty by further approximating these terms with even simpler expressions. Specifically, instead of
\check{S}_{n,k}(u),
we actually deal with

S_{n,k}(u)=\begin{cases}\sum_{\bar{t}\in\Gamma_{n}}\mathds{1}[X_{\bar{t}}\geq u],&\text{ if }k=0,\\[4.30554pt]
\sum_{(\bar{t},\,\bar{\Omega})\in\Gamma_{n}\times\tbinom{\langle d\rangle}{b_{k}}}\mathds{1}[\bar{X}_{\bar{t},\bar{\Omega}}\geq u\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{2b_{k}}],&\text{ if }1\leq k<d.\end{cases}

(3.4)

Here, for
i\geq 1,

\langle i\rangle
is the increasingly ordered set
(1,\ldots,i);

\tbinom{\langle d\rangle}{b_{k}}
is the collection of all increasingly ordered subsets of
\langle d\rangle
with size
b_{k};
for
\bar{\Omega}\equiv(\omega_{1},\ldots,\omega_{k+1})\in\tbinom{\langle d\rangle}{b_{k}},

\bar{X}_{\bar{t},\bar{\Omega}}=(X_{\bar{t}+\bar{e}_{\omega_{1}}},X_{\bar{t}-\bar{e}_{\omega_{1}}},\ldots,X_{\bar{t}+\bar{e}_{\omega_{b_{k}}}},X_{\bar{t}-\bar{e}_{\omega_{b_{k}}}});

(3.5)

and
\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{i}
is the all ones
i-
dimensional row vector. The set
\tbinom{\langle d\rangle}{b_{k}}
captures the different orientations in which the minimal Betti generating complexes can occur; see the image on the right in Figure
2
for an illustration. Working with
S_{n,k}(u)
is much easier than
\check{S}_{n,k}(u)
as the former does not involve conditions enforcing isolation and, for
k
such that
1\leq k<d,
it also has no condition on
X_{\bar{t}}
itself.

In the same spirit, as against
\check{N}_{n,k}(u),
we work with
N_{n,k}(u)
which is defined as follows. For each
k
such that
1\leq k<d-1,
let

\mathcal{N}_{k}:=\{\text{Subgraphs }G\equiv(V,E)\subseteq\mathcal{G}_{0}:|V|=2k+2,\text{ elements of $V$ are ordered}\\
\text{lexicographically, }G\text{ is isomorphic but not $\|\cdot\|_{1}-$isometric to }O_{k}\},

(3.6)

where
\mathcal{G}_{0}
is as defined above (
3.1
). For
\bar{t}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
and
G\equiv(V,E),
where
V\subseteq\mathbb{Z}^{d}
is ordered, let
\bar{X}_{\bar{t},G}
be the ordered vector
(X_{\bar{t}+\bar{v}}:\bar{v}\in V).
Then,

N_{n,k}(u):=\begin{cases}0,&\text{ if $k=0,d-1,$}\\
\sum_{(\bar{t},\,G)\in\Gamma_{n}\times\mathcal{N}_{k}}\mathds{1}[\bar{X}_{\bar{t},G}\geq u\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{2b_{k}}],&\text{ if $1\leq k<d-1.$}\end{cases}

(3.7)

Separately, for
0\leq k<d,
let

L_{n,k}(u):=\sum_{(\bar{t},\,G)\in\Gamma_{n}\times\mathcal{P}_{k}}\mathds{1}[\bar{X}_{\bar{t},G}\geq u\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{|V|}],

(3.8)

where
\mathcal{P}_{k}
is as in (
3.1
), (
3.2
), or (
3.3
);
V
is the vertex set of
G;
and
\bar{X}_{\bar{t},G}
is as defined above. While
\check{L}_{n,k}(u)
does not count components, nevertheless, the above expression is the easier to handle.

The next result compares
S_{n,k}(u),N_{n,k}(u),
and
L_{n,k}(u)
with the approximators in Theorem
3.3
; its proof is given in Section
5
, p.
5
. As we shall see in Remark
5.1
later, the additional
D_{n,k}(u)
term is due to boundary conditions.

Lemma 3.5
.

Fix
k
so that
0\leq k<d.
Let
n\geq 0
and
u\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}.
Then, the below statements hold:

1.

\check{S}_{n,k}(u)\leq S_{n,k}(u)\leq\check{S}_{n,k}(u)+{\Cr{c:Betti}}_{,k}\,\check{L}_{n,k}(u)+D_{n,k}(u),

where
{\Cr{c:Betti}}_{,k}
is as in Theorem
3.3
, and

D_{n,k}(u):=\begin{cases}0,&\text{ if $k=0,$}\\[4.30554pt]
\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\bar{t},\,\bar{\Omega})\in\Gamma_{n}\times\tbinom{\langle d\rangle}{b_{k}}:\\
\|\bar{t}\|_{\infty}=n\end{subarray}}\mathds{1}[\bar{X}_{\bar{t},\bar{\Omega}}\geq u\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{2b_{k}}],&\text{ if $1\leq k<d.$}\end{cases}

(3.9)

2.

\check{N}_{n,k}(u)\leq N_{n,k}(u).

3.

\check{L}_{n,k}(u)\leq L_{n,k}(u).

An immediate consequence of Theorem
3.3
and Lemma
3.5
is the following result, which bounds the difference between
S_{n,k}(u)
and
\beta_{n,k}(u).

Lemma 3.6
.

Fix
k
such that
0\leq k<d.
Let
n\geq 0
and
u\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}.
Then,

|\beta_{n,k}(u)-S_{n,k}(u)|\leq N_{n,k}(u)+{\Cr{c:Betti}}_{,k}\,L_{n,k}(u)+D_{n,k}(u),

where
{\Cr{c:Betti}}_{,k}
is as in Theorem
3.3
.

Because of the above result, in order to prove Theorems
1.3
and
1.4
, it suffices to establish the corresponding results first for
S_{n,k}(u)
and then show that
N_{n,k}(u),

L_{n,k}(u),
and
D_{n,k}(u)
are all negligible relative to
S_{n,k}(u).
Indeed, this is precisely what we do.

In the above line of thought, our first result describes the asymptotic behaviour of the mean value, along with the rate of convergence, of the different terms in Lemma
3.6
. Before stating it formally, we introduce few notations. For
m\geq 1,
and
\rho,\mu^{\prime}\in[0,1),
let

\phi_{m}(\rho,\mu^{\prime})=\dfrac{m+1+(m-1)\rho-2m\mu^{\prime}}{2(1+(m-1)\rho-m[\mu^{\prime}]^{2})}.

(3.10)

Now, define constants

\varrho_{k}:=\phi_{2b_{k}-1}(\rho_{2},\rho_{3}),\quad\text{ if $1\leq k<d,$}

(3.11)

and

\varphi_{k}:=\phi_{2b_{k}}(\rho_{2},\rho_{1})=\frac{1-2a_{k}[2\rho_{1}-1]}{2[1-2a_{k}\rho_{1}^{2}]},\quad\text{ if $0\leq k<d,$}

(3.12)

Lemma 3.7
.

Fix
k
such that
0\leq k<d.
Let
\lambda_{n,k}(u)
be as in (
1.4
) and let
\{u_{n}\}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{\infty}
be such that
\lim_{n\to\infty}u_{n}=\infty.
Then, the following statements are true.

1.

If
\Cr{a:r0Asm}
and
\Cr{a:r2Asm}
hold, then
\left|\dfrac{\mathbb{E}[S_{n,k}(u_{n})]}{\lambda_{n,k}(u_{n})}-1\right|=O\left(\dfrac{1}{u_{n}^{2}}\right).

2.

If
\Cr{a:r0Asm},\Cr{a:r2Asm},
and
\Cr{a:rqAsm}
hold, then
\varrho_{k}>a_{k},
for
1\leq k<d-1,
and

\dfrac{\mathbb{E}[N_{n,k}(u_{n})]}{\lambda_{n,k}(u_{n})}=\begin{cases}0,&\text{ if $k=0,d-1,$}\\
O(\exp[-(\varrho_{k}-a_{k})u_{n}^{2}]),&\text{ if $1\leq k<d-1.$}\end{cases}

3.

If
\Cr{a:r0Asm},\Cr{a:r1Asm},

\Cr{a:ComC}(k),
and
\Cr{a:r2LAsm}
hold, then
\varphi_{k}>a_{k}
and
\dfrac{\mathbb{E}[L_{n,k}(u_{n})]}{\lambda_{n,k}(u_{n})}=O(u_{n}^{-1}\exp[-(\varphi_{k}-a_{k})u_{n}^{2}]).

4.

If
\Cr{a:r0Asm}
and
\Cr{a:r2Asm}
hold, then

\dfrac{\mathbb{E}[D_{n,k}(u_{n})]}{\lambda_{n,k}(u_{n})}=\begin{cases}0,&\text{ if $k=0,$}\\
O\left(\dfrac{1}{n}\right),&\text{ if $1\leq k<d.$}\end{cases}

The proof of this result is given in Section
5
, p.
5
and uses Slepian’s lemma (Lemma
2.5
) and Multivariate Gaussian tail estimates (Lemma
2.4
); the Savage condition in the latter is ensured due to our assumptions in Table
1
.

It is now straightforward to establish Theorem
1.3
and the Statement
1
of Theorem
1.4
.

Proof of Theorem
1.3
.

By a simple triangle inequality and Lemma
3.6
,

\left|\frac{\mathbb{E}[\beta_{n,k}(u_{n})]}{\lambda_{n,k}(u_{n})}-1\right|\leq\left|\dfrac{\mathbb{E}[S_{n,k}(u_{n})]}{\lambda_{n,k}(u_{n})}-1\right|+\dfrac{\mathbb{E}[N_{n,k}(u_{n})]}{\lambda_{n,k}(u_{n})}+\dfrac{\mathbb{E}[L_{n,k}(u_{n})]}{\lambda_{n,k}(u_{n})}+\dfrac{\mathbb{E}[D_{n,k}(u_{n})]}{\lambda_{n,k}(u_{n})}.

Because
1/u_{n}^{2}+\exp[-(\varphi_{k}-a_{k})u_{n}^{2}]+\exp[-(\varrho_{k}-a_{k})u_{n}^{2}]=O(1/u_{n}^{2}),
the desired result is now easy to see from Lemma
3.7
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.4
.
1
.

From Markov’s inequality, we have

1-\mathbb{P}\{\beta_{n,k}(u_{n})=0\}=\mathbb{P}\{\beta_{n,k}(u_{n})\geq 1\}\leq\mathbb{E}[\beta_{n,k}(u_{n})].

On the other hand,

\mathbb{E}[\beta_{n,k}(u_{n})]=\lambda_{n,k}(u_{n})\frac{\mathbb{E}[\beta_{n,k}(u_{n})]}{\lambda_{n,k}(u_{n})}=\lambda_{n,k}(u_{n})O\left(1+\frac{1}{u_{n}^{2}}+\frac{1}{n}\right)=\lambda_{n,k}(u_{n})O(1)=O(\lambda_{n,k}(u_{n})),

where the second relation follows from Theorem
1.3
, while the third one follows because both
u_{n}
and
n
grow to
\infty.
Finally, from (
1.4
) and (
1.5
), and since
\nu_{n}\to\infty,
we have

\lambda_{n,k}(u_{n})=O\left(e^{-\nu_{n}}\left[\frac{\log n}{\log n+\nu_{n}}\right]^{b_{k}}\right).

The desired result is now easy to see.
∎

Moving on, our next result shows that
S_{n,k}(u_{n})
has a Poisson behaviour when
\lambda_{n,k}(u_{n})
converges to a constant. Let

\vartheta_{k}:=\frac{[1+(2b_{k}-1)\rho_{2}-2b_{k}\rho_{1}]^{2}}{1+(2b_{k}-1)\rho_{2}-2b_{k}\rho_{1}^{2}}=\frac{b_{k}[1-2a_{k}\rho_{1}]^{2}}{a_{k}[1-2a_{k}\rho_{1}^{2}]},\quad\text{ if $0\leq k<d,$}

(3.13)

Theorem 3.8
.

Fix
k
such that
0\leq k<d.
Let
\lambda_{n,k}(u)
be as in (
1.4
) and
\{u_{n}\}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{\infty}
be as in (
1.5
) with
\lim_{n\to\infty}\nu_{n}=\log\left[\tfrac{1}{\lambda}\right]
for some fixed
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}.
Suppose
\Cr{a:r0Asm},\Cr{a:r1Asm},\Cr{a:ComC}(k),\Cr{a:PoiG},
and
\Cr{a:r2LAsm}
hold. Then,
0<\vartheta_{k}<1;
further,

\|S_{n,k}(u_{n})-\textnormal{Poi}(\mathbb{E}[S_{n,k}(u_{n})])\|_{\textnormal{TV}}=O\left(n^{-d\vartheta_{k}/(2b_{k})}[\log n]^{-(1-\vartheta_{k})/2}+\frac{\log n}{n}\sum_{q=1}^{2dn}\rho_{q}\right).

The
k=0
case of this result has been shown in
[
18
, Theorem 3.6]
using Theorem
2.9
. We build upon their ideas to show the multi-dimensional version here. Our proof is given in Section
5
, p.
5
.

Using the above result and Lemma
3.7
, we now establish the Poisson result for
\beta_{n,k}(u_{n}).

Proof of Theorem
1.4
.
2
.

By the triangle inequality, and Remarks
2.7
and
2.8
,

\displaystyle\|\beta_{n,k}(u_{n})-\textnormal{Poi}(\lambda)\|_{\textnormal{TV}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|S_{n,k}(u_{n})-\textnormal{Poi}(\mathbb{E}[S_{n,k}(u_{n})])\|_{\textnormal{TV}}

(3.14)

\displaystyle\|\textnormal{Poi}(\mathbb{E}[S_{n,k}(u_{n})])-\textnormal{Poi}(\lambda)\|_{\textnormal{TV}}+\|\beta_{n,k}(u_{n})-S_{n,k}(u_{n})\|_{\textnormal{TV}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|S_{n,k}(u_{n})-\textnormal{Poi}(\mathbb{E}[S_{n,k}(u_{n})])\|_{\textnormal{TV}}

\displaystyle+\;|\mathbb{E}[S_{n,k}(u_{n})]-\lambda|+\mathbb{P}\{\beta_{n,k}(u_{n})\neq S_{n,k}(u_{n})\}.

Separately, Markov’s inequality and Lemma
3.6
show that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\{\beta_{n,k}(u_{n})\neq S_{n,k}(u_{n})\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\{|\beta_{n,k}(u_{n})-S_{n,k}(u_{n})|\geq 1\}

(3.15)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[N_{n,k}(u_{n})]+\mathbb{E}[L_{n,k}(u_{n})]+\mathbb{E}[D_{n,k}(u_{n})].

Further, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[N_{n,k}(u_{n})]+\mathbb{E}[L_{n,k}(u_{n})]+\mathbb{E}[D_{n,k}(u_{n})]

(3.16)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O(\exp[-(\varrho_{k}-a_{k})u_{n}^{2}])+O(u_{n}^{-1}\exp[-(\varphi_{k}-a_{k})u_{n}^{2}])+O\left(\frac{1}{n}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O\left(\frac{[\log n]^{(\varrho_{k}-a_{k})b_{k}/a_{k}}}{n^{d(\varrho_{k}-a_{k})/a_{k}}}\right)+O\left(\frac{[\log n]^{(\varphi_{k}-a_{k})b_{k}/a_{k}-0.5}}{n^{d(\varphi_{k}-a_{k})/a_{k}}}\right)+O\left(\frac{1}{n}\right)

(3.17)

and

|\mathbb{E}[S_{n,k}(u_{n})]-\lambda_{n,k}(u_{n})|=O\left(\frac{1}{u_{n}^{2}}\right)=O\left(\frac{1}{\log n}\right),

(3.18)

where (
3.16
) and (
3.18
) hold due to Lemma
3.7
and the fact that
\lambda_{n,k}(u_{n})\to\lambda,
a constant, while (
3.17
) follows by observing
\exp[-(\varrho_{k}-a_{k})u_{n}^{2}]=[\tau_{k}(2n+1)^{d}u_{n}^{-2b_{k}}]^{-(\varrho_{k}-a_{k})/a_{k}}[\lambda_{n,k}(u_{n})]^{(\varrho_{k}-a_{k})/a_{k}},
and so on, and then making use of the facts that
\lambda_{n,k}(u_{n})\to\lambda
and
a_{k}u_{n}^{2}/[d\log n]\to 1.

By substituting (
1.5
) in (
1.4
), additionally observe that

|\lambda_{n,k}(u_{n})-\lambda|=\left|\left[\frac{\log[\tau_{k}(2n+1)^{d}]}{\left[\log[\tau_{k}(2n+1)^{d}]-b_{k}\log\left[\frac{\log[\tau_{k}(2n+1)^{d}]}{a_{k}}\right]+\nu_{n}\right]}\right]^{b_{k}}e^{-\nu_{n}}-\lambda\right|.

Using the triangle inequality and the fact that
\lim_{n\to\infty}\nu_{n}=\log[1/\lambda],
it then follows that

|\lambda_{n,k}(u_{n})-\lambda|=O(|e^{-\nu_{n}}-\lambda|)+O\left(\left|\left[\frac{\log[\tau_{k}(2n+1)^{d}]}{\log[\tau_{k}(2n+1)^{d}]-b_{k}\log\left[\frac{\log[\tau_{k}(2n+1)^{d}]}{a_{k}}\right]+\nu_{n}}\right]^{b_{k}}-1\right|\right).

Recall that, if
f(x)=x^{b_{k}},
then, by the monotonicity of
f^{\prime}(x)
and the mean value theorem,

|f(x)-f(1)|\leq b_{k}\left[\sup_{c\in\{x,1\}}x^{b_{k}-1}\right]|x-1|.

Applying this above and using the fact that
\lim_{n\to\infty}\nu_{n}
is a constant, we get

|\lambda_{n,k}(u_{n})-\lambda|=O(|e^{-\nu_{n}}-\lambda|)+O\left(\frac{\log\log n}{\log n}\right).

Consequently, the triangle inequality and (
3.18
) show

|\mathbb{E}[S_{n,k}(u_{n})]-\lambda|=O(|e^{-\nu_{n}}-\lambda|)+O\left(\frac{\log\log n}{\log n}\right).

(3.19)

The desired result now follows from (
3.14
), (
3.15
), (
3.17
), (
3.19
), and Theorem
3.8
.
∎

Finally, it remains to establish the non-vanishing regime behavior in Theorem
1.4
. Towards that, we establish the following two results. The first one discusses second order moments of
S_{n,k}(u),N_{n,k}(u),L_{n,k}(u),
and
D_{n,k}(u),
while the next one obtains a CLT for
S_{n,k}(u).
Unlike Theorem
1.4
, note that the CLT for
S_{n,k}(u)
holds for the entire non-vanishing regime (see Remark

R
13

).

Lemma 3.9
.

Fix
k
such that
0\leq k<d.
Let
\lambda_{n,k}(u)
be as in (
1.4
) and
\{u_{n}\}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{\infty}
be as in (
1.5
) with
\nu_{n}\to-\infty.
Then, the following statements are true.

1.

If
\Cr{a:r0Asm},\Cr{a:r1Asm},\Cr{a:ComC}(k),

\Cr{a:CLTG},
and
\Cr{a:r2LAsm}
hold, then
\left|\dfrac{\textnormal{Var}[S_{n,k}(u_{n})]}{\lambda_{n,k}(u_{n})}-1\right|=O\left(\frac{1}{u_{n}^{2}}\right).

2.

If
\Cr{a:r0Asm},\Cr{a:CLTG},\Cr{a:r2Asm},
and
\Cr{a:rqAsm}
hold, then

\dfrac{\mathbb{E}[N_{n,k}^{2}(u_{n})]}{\lambda_{n,k}^{2}(u_{n})}=\begin{cases}0,&\text{ if $k=0,d-1,$ }\\
O\left(\dfrac{\exp[-(\varrho_{k}-a_{k})u_{n}^{2}]}{\lambda_{n,k}(u_{n})}\right)+O\left(\exp[-2(\varrho_{k}-a_{k})u_{n}^{2}]\right),&\text{ if $1\leq k<d-1.$}\end{cases}

3.

If
\Cr{a:r0Asm},\Cr{a:r1Asm},\Cr{a:ComC}(k),

\Cr{a:CLTG},
and
\Cr{a:r2LAsm}
hold, then

\dfrac{\mathbb{E}[L_{n,k}^{2}(u_{n})]}{\lambda_{n,k}^{2}(u_{n})}=O\left(\dfrac{u_{n}^{-1}\exp[-(\varphi_{k}-a_{k})u_{n}^{2}]}{\lambda_{n,k}(u_{n})}\right)+O\left(u_{n}^{-2}\exp[-2(\varphi_{k}-a_{k})u_{n}^{2}]\right).

4.

If
\Cr{a:r0Asm},\Cr{a:r1Asm},\Cr{a:ComC}(k),

\Cr{a:CLTG},
and
\Cr{a:r2LAsm}
hold, then

\dfrac{\mathbb{E}[D_{n,k}^{2}(u_{n})]}{\lambda_{n,k}^{2}(u_{n})}=\begin{cases}0,&\text{ if $k=0,$}\\
O\left(\dfrac{1}{n\lambda_{n,k}(u_{n})}\right)+O\left(\dfrac{1}{n^{2}}\right),&\text{ if $1\leq k<d.$}\end{cases}

The proof of this result is given in Section
5
, p.
5
. Statements
1
and
4
need the covariance bounds from Lemmas
5.6
and
5.7
, while Statements
2
and
3
require the covariance type bounds from Lemma
5.8
.

Theorem 3.10
.

Fix
k
such that
0\leq k<d.
Let
\lambda_{n,k}(u)
be as in (
1.4
) and
\{u_{n}\}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{\infty}
be as in (
1.5
) with
\lim_{n\to\infty}\nu_{n}=-\infty.
Suppose
\Cr{a:r0Asm},\Cr{a:r1Asm},\Cr{a:ComC}(k),\Cr{a:CLTG},
and
\Cr{a:r2LAsm}
hold. Then,

\frac{S_{n,k}(u_{n})-\mathbb{E}[S_{n,k}(u_{n})]}{\sqrt{\lambda_{n,k}(u_{n})}}\Rightarrow N(0,1).

The proof for this result is given in Section
5
, p.
5
. Under the new set of assumptions, the key trick, as in Theorem
3.8
, again is to show
\|S_{n,k}(u_{n})-\textnormal{Poi}(\mathbb{E}[S_{n,k}(u_{n})])\|_{\textnormal{TV}}\to 0.
The desired result then follows from the fact that a sequence of Poisson random variables with mean tending to
\infty,
after suitable normalization, converge in distribution to a standard Gaussian.

We now discuss the proof of Theorem
1.4
.
3
.

Proof of Theorem.
1.4
.
3
.

By Chebyshev’s inequality,

\mathbb{P}\left\{\left|\frac{\beta_{n,k}(u_{n})-\mathbb{E}[\beta_{n,k}(u_{n})]}{\lambda_{n,k}(u_{n})}\right|\geq\epsilon\right\}\leq\frac{\textnormal{Var}[\beta_{n,k}(u_{n})]}{\epsilon^{2}\lambda_{n,k}^{2}(u_{n})}

for all
\epsilon>0.
Additionally, if
\epsilon\in(0,1),
then it follows from Theorem
1.3
that for all large enough
n

\{\beta_{n,k}(u_{n})=0\}\subseteq\left\{\left|\frac{\beta_{n,k}(u_{n})-\mathbb{E}[\beta_{n,k}(u_{n})]}{\lambda_{n,k}(u_{n})}\right|\geq\epsilon\right\},

and hence

\mathbb{P}\{\beta_{n,k}(u_{n})=0\}\leq\mathbb{P}\left\{\left|\frac{\beta_{n,k}(u_{n})-\mathbb{E}[\beta_{n,k}(u_{n})]}{\lambda_{n,k}(u_{n})}\right|\geq\epsilon\right\}.

Consequently, both (
1.7
) and (
1.8
) are simple consequences of (
1.6
) which we now prove.

Observe that

\displaystyle\textnormal{Var}[\beta_{n,k}(u_{n})]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\textnormal{Var}[S_{n,k}(u_{n})]+2\,\textnormal{Var}[\beta_{n,k}(u_{n})-S_{n,k}(u_{n})]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\textnormal{Var}[S_{n,k}(u_{n})]+2\mathbb{E}|\beta_{n,k}(u_{n})-S_{n,k}(u_{n})|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O\left(\textnormal{Var}[S_{n,k}(u_{n})]+\mathbb{E}[N_{n,k}^{2}(u_{n})]+\mathbb{E}[L_{n,k}^{2}(u_{n})]+\mathbb{E}[D_{n,k}^{2}(u_{n})]\right),

where the first relation follows since, for any two random variables
Y_{1}
and
Y_{2},
we have
2|\textnormal{Cov}[Y_{1},Y_{2}]|\leq\textnormal{Var}[Y_{1}]+\textnormal{Var}[Y_{2}];
the second holds because
\textnormal{Var}[Y]\leq\mathbb{E}|Y|^{2}
for any random variable
Y;
while the third one follows from Lemma
3.6
. From Lemma
3.9
, it is then easy to see that

\frac{\textnormal{Var}[\beta_{n,k}(u_{n})]}{\lambda_{n,k}^{2}(u_{n})}=O\left(\frac{1}{\lambda_{n,k}(u_{n})}\right)+O\left(\exp[-2(\varrho_{k}-a_{k})u_{n}^{2}]\right)+O\left(u_{n}^{-2}\exp[-2(\varphi_{k}-a_{k})]\right)+O\left(\frac{1}{n^{2}}\right).

Since
\varrho_{k}>a_{k}
and
\varphi_{k}>a_{k}
on account on Lemma
3.7
and, also, since
\lambda_{n,k}(u_{n})\to\infty,
it follows that (
1.6
) holds, as desired.

We now turn to proving (
1.9
). Let

C^{\nu}_{k}:=\begin{cases}\frac{\varphi_{k}-a_{k}}{\varphi_{k}-a_{k}/2},&\text{ if $k=0,$}\\[4.30554pt]
\min\left\{\frac{\varrho_{k}-a_{k}}{\varrho_{k}-a_{k}/2},\frac{\varphi_{k}-a_{k}}{\varphi_{k}-a_{k}/2},\frac{2}{d}\right\},&\text{ if $1\leq k<d.$}\end{cases}

(3.20)

Since
\Cr{a:rCAsm}
holds, note that
\rho_{2}\geq 0.
Hence,
a_{k}\geq 0.
Therefore, from Lemmas
3.7
.
2
and
3.7
.
3
and by the above definition, it follows that
C^{\nu}_{k}\in(0,1].

Suppose that
1\leq k<d.
Using Lemma
3.7
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left\{\left|\frac{[\beta_{n,k}(u_{n})-S_{n,k}(u_{n})]-\mathbb{E}[\beta_{n,k}(u_{n})-S_{n,k}(u_{n})]}{\sqrt{\lambda_{n,k}(u_{n})}}\right|>\epsilon\right\}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{2}{\epsilon}\frac{\mathbb{E}[N_{n,k}(u_{n})]+\mathbb{E}[L_{n,k}(u_{n})]+\mathbb{E}[D_{n,k}(u_{n})]}{\sqrt{\lambda_{n,k}(u_{n})}}

for any
\epsilon>0.
Hence, (
1.9
) follows from Theorem
3.10
and Slutsky’s Theorem once we show that the three terms on the RHS decays to zero.

Observe that

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbb{E}[N_{n,k}(u_{n})]}{\sqrt{\lambda_{n,k}(u_{n})}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O\left(\frac{(2n+1)^{d/2}}{u_{n}^{b_{k}}}\exp[-(\varrho_{k}-a_{k}/2)u_{n}^{2}]\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O\left(\frac{(2n+1)^{d/2}}{u_{n}^{b_{k}}}\exp\left[-[\varrho_{k}/a_{k}-1/2]\left[[1-C]\log(2n+1)^{d}-b_{k}\log\left[\log(2n+1)^{d}\right]\right]\right]\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O\left(\frac{1}{u_{n}^{b_{k}}}\exp\left[-[\varrho_{k}/a_{k}-1/2]\left[\left[\frac{\varrho_{k}-a_{k}}{\varrho_{k}-a_{k}/2}-C\right]\log(2n+1)^{d}-b_{k}\log\left[\log(2n+1)^{d}\right]\right]\right]\right)

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle 0,

where the first relation follows from Lemma
3.7
.
2
and (
1.4
), the second holds due to (
1.5
) and the assumptions on
\nu_{n},
the third relation follows by simple algebra, while the last relation follows from (
3.20
) and since
C<C^{\nu}_{k}.

Similarly, one can see that
\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}[L_{n,k}(u_{n})]/\sqrt{\lambda_{n,k}(u_{n})}=0.

Next, observe that

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbb{E}[D_{n,k}(u_{n})]}{\sqrt{\lambda_{n,k}(u_{n})}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O\left(\frac{(2n+1)^{d/2}}{nu_{n}^{b_{k}}}\exp[-a_{k}u_{n}^{2}/2]\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O\left(\frac{(2n+1)^{d/2}}{nu_{n}^{b_{k}}}\exp\left[-[1/2]\left[[1-C]\log(2n+1)^{d}-b_{k}\log\left[\log(2n+1)^{d}\right]\right]\right]\right)

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle 0,

where the first relation follows from Lemma
3.7
.
4
and (
1.4
), the second one holds on account of (
1.5
) and the assumptions on
\nu_{n},
while the last relation follows since
C^{\nu}_{k}\leq 2/d
and
C<C^{\nu}_{k}.

This establishes (
1.9
) for the
1\leq k<d
case. One can similarly argue the
k=0
case. This completes the proof.
∎

4
Key Properties of Geometric Complexes on a Lattice

Here we establish the main properties (Proposition
3.1
and Theorems
3.2
and
3.3
) of our Čech and equivalently Vietoris-Rips complex that we mentioned in Section
3
earlier. We also prove some additional features that we use later in Sections
5
and
6
. While we employ these results afterwards for our random setup from Section
1
, we emphasize that the discussion here is completely from a deterministic perspective.

Throughout this section, let
V
be an arbitrary fixed subset of
\mathbb{Z}^{d}.
As in Definitions
1.1
and
1.2
respectively, let
\mathcal{K}
be the Čech and
\hat{\mathcal{K}}
be the Vietoris-Rips complex on
V
with respect to
\mathscr{B}_{\infty}(\cdot,\tfrac{1}{2}).

Our first aim is to show that
\mathcal{K}=\hat{\mathcal{K}}.
This follows from their definitions and holds since these are defined on a lattice. We begin with two trivial facts.

Fact 4.1
.

If
r_{1},r_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}
with
|r_{1}-r_{2}|=1,
then for all
r\in\mathbb{Z}
with
\max\{|r-r_{1}|,|r-r_{2}|\}\leq 1,
we have

r\in\{r_{1},r_{2}\}.

Fact 4.2
.

If
r_{1},r_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}
with
|r_{1}-r_{2}|=2,
then for all
r\in\mathbb{Z}
with
\max\{|r-r_{1}|,|r-r_{2}|\}\leq 1,
we have
r=(r_{1}+r_{2})/2.

Lemma 4.3
.

\mathcal{K}=\hat{\mathcal{K}}.

Proof.

By definition,
\sigma\in\mathcal{K}
implies that
\sigma\in\hat{\mathcal{K}}.
It thus suffices to show only the converse. Clearly, if
\sigma\in\hat{\mathcal{K}}
and
|\sigma|\in\{1,2\},
then
\sigma\in\mathcal{K}.
Consider
\sigma\equiv\{\bar{t}_{0},\ldots,\bar{t}_{k}\}\in\hat{\mathcal{K}}
for some
k\geq 2.
As
\sigma\in\hat{\mathcal{K}},

\mathscr{B}_{\infty}(\bar{t}_{i},1/2)\cap\mathscr{B}_{\infty}(\bar{t}_{j},1/2)\neq\emptyset,
or equivalently
\|\bar{t}_{i}-\bar{t}_{j}\|_{\infty}=1,
for each pair
\bar{t}_{i},\bar{t}_{j}.
Without loss of generality, let
\bar{t}_{0}\equiv\bar{0}.
Then, using Fact
4.1
, for any coordinate index
l,
exactly one of the following cases is true:

i)

\bar{t}_{0}(l)=\bar{t}_{1}(l)=\bar{t}_{2}(l)=\cdots=\bar{t}_{k}(l)=0,
or

ii)

for some
i\in\{1,\ldots,k\},

\bar{t}_{i}(l)=1;
hence,
\bar{t}_{i^{\prime}}(l)\in\{0,1\}
for all
i^{\prime}\in\{1,\ldots,k\}\setminus i,
or

ii)

for some
i\in\{1,\ldots,k\},

\bar{t}_{i}(l)=-1;
hence,
\bar{t}_{i^{\prime}}(l)\in\{-1,0\}
for all
i^{\prime}\in\{1,\ldots,k\}\setminus i.

Now define the vector
\bar{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
as follows:

\bar{t}(l)=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{2},&\text{ if Cases i) or ii) hold, }\\
-\frac{1}{2},&\text{ if Case iii) holds.}\end{cases}

Trivially,
\bar{t}\in\mathscr{B}_{\infty}(\bar{t}_{i},1/2)
for each
i\in\{0,\ldots,k\}.
The desired result is now easy to see.
∎

Precisely due to this equivalence,
\mathcal{K}
here and
\mathcal{K}(n;u)
from Definition
1.1
is both a Čech complex as well as a Vietoris-Rips complex.

We next establish the deterministic equivalent of Theorem
3.2
.

Theorem 4.4
.

In every induced subcomplex
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\mathcal{K}
with non-trivial
k-
th Betti number, where
0\leq k<d,
there exist at least
2k+2
vertices with pairwise
\|\cdot\|_{1}
-distances at least
2.

Before giving its proof, we state and prove two technical lemmas. For any
\bar{t}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
and
1\leq j\leq d,
let
\mathfrak{N}(\bar{t}),\mathfrak{N}^{{}^{-}}_{j}(\bar{t}),
and
\mathfrak{N}^{{}^{+}}_{j}(\bar{t})
be suitable
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}-
neighbourhoods of
\bar{t}
in
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
given by

\mathfrak{N}(\bar{t}):=\{\bar{t}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}:\|\bar{t}-\bar{t}^{\prime}\|_{\infty}\leq 1\},

\mathfrak{N}^{{}^{-}}_{j}(\bar{t}):=\{\bar{t}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{N}(\bar{t}):\bar{t}^{\prime}(j)=\bar{t}(j)-1\},\text{ and }\mathfrak{N}^{{}^{+}}_{j}(\bar{t}):=\{\bar{t}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{N}(\bar{t}):\bar{t}^{\prime}(j)=\bar{t}(j)+1\}.

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
\bar{t}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
and
j\in\{1,\ldots,d\}.
Then, the following statements hold:

•

For every induced subcomplex
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\mathcal{K}
such that
\mathcal{F}_{0}(\mathcal{C})\subseteq\mathfrak{N}(\bar{t})\setminus\mathfrak{N}^{{}^{-}}_{j}(\bar{t})
and
\bar{t}+\bar{e}_{j}\in\mathcal{C},
all its Betti numbers are trivial.

•

For every induced subcomplex
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\mathcal{K}
such that
\mathcal{F}_{0}(\mathcal{C})\subseteq\mathfrak{N}(\bar{t})\setminus\mathfrak{N}^{{}^{+}}_{j}(\bar{t})
and
\bar{t}-\bar{e}_{j}\in\mathcal{C},

all its Betti numbers are trivial.

Proof.

The first statement is proved here; the other one follows similarly. We use induction on
k,
the index of Betti numbers. For any
\bar{t}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{N}(\bar{t})\setminus\mathfrak{N}^{{}^{-}}_{j}(\bar{t}),
we have

\|\bar{t}^{\prime}-(\bar{t}+\bar{e}_{j})\|_{\infty}\leq 1.

(4.1)

From this, the
k=0
case is easy to see. Now assume the result for some
(k-1).
For the sake of contradiction, assume that there exists
\mathcal{C}
with
\mathcal{F}_{0}(\mathcal{C})\subseteq\mathfrak{N}(\bar{t})\setminus\mathfrak{N}^{{}^{-}}_{j}(\bar{t})
and
\bar{t}+\bar{e}_{j}\in\mathcal{C}
such that
\beta_{k}(\mathcal{C})\geq 1.
This then immediately implies that there exists a
k-
NTC in
\mathcal{C}
with minimal vertex support. In fact, we claim that there exists a
k-
NTC
\gamma
in
\mathcal{C}
having minimal vertex support such that
\bar{v}\equiv\bar{t}+\bar{e}_{j}\in\textnormal{vsupp}(\gamma).
For the time being, suppose that this latter claim is true. Then, for any
\bar{v}^{\prime}\in\textnormal{vsupp}(\gamma)
such that
\bar{v}^{\prime}\neq\bar{v},
from Lemma
2.2
,
\gamma\cap\textnormal{lk}(\bar{v}^{\prime})
is a
(k-1)-
NTC in
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}:=\textnormal{lk}(\bar{v}^{\prime})\cap\{\sigma\in\mathcal{C}:\sigma\subseteq\textnormal{vsupp}(\gamma)\},
i.e., the
(k-1)-
th Betti number of
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
is non-trivial. But
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
is an induced subcomplex of
\mathcal{K}
with
\mathcal{F}_{0}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime})\subseteq\mathfrak{N}(\bar{t})\setminus\mathfrak{N}^{{}^{-}}_{j}(\bar{t})
and
\bar{v}\in\mathcal{C}^{\prime},
where the latter is due to (
4.1
). These statements contradict the induction hypothesis and we are done.

It only remains to establish our above claim. Let
\gamma\equiv\sum_{\sigma\in\textnormal{supp}(\gamma)}c_{\sigma}\sigma
be a
k-
NTC in
\mathcal{C}
with minimal vertex support such that
\bar{v}\notin\textnormal{vsupp}(\gamma),
where
\textnormal{supp}(\gamma)
restriction in the summation means that the linear sum representation of
\gamma
has only those faces
\sigma
for which
c_{\sigma}\neq 0.
Pick
\bar{v}^{\prime}\in\textnormal{vsupp}(\gamma);
clearly,
\bar{v}^{\prime}\neq\bar{v}.
Consider the
k-
chain

\gamma_{1}=\gamma\cap\textnormal{st}(\bar{v}^{\prime})=\sum_{\sigma\in\textnormal{st}(\bar{v}^{\prime})}c_{\sigma}\sigma,

and let

\gamma_{1}*\{\bar{v}\}:=\sum_{\sigma\in\textnormal{st}(\bar{v}^{\prime})}c_{\sigma}(\sigma,\bar{v}),

where
(\sigma,\bar{v})
is the oriented
(k+1)-
face whose first
(k+1)
vertices are those of
\sigma
and in the same order as in
\sigma,
and the last vertex is
\bar{v};
and
\gamma\cap\textnormal{st}(\bar{v}^{\prime})
is defined as in Remark
2.3
. As
\mathcal{C}
is an induced complex,
\gamma_{1}*\{\bar{v}\}
is necessarily a
(k+1)-
chain in
\mathcal{C}.
With
(\gamma\cap\textnormal{lk}(\bar{v}^{\prime}))*\{v\}
defined as in the spirit of
\gamma_{1}*\{\bar{v}\},
and using the relation
\gamma\cap\textnormal{lk}(\bar{v}^{\prime})=\partial_{k}(\gamma_{1})
from Remark
2.3
, it is easy to see that

\partial_{k+1}(\gamma_{1}*\{\bar{v}\})=(\gamma\cap\textnormal{lk}(\bar{v}^{\prime}))*\{v\}+(-1)^{k+1}\gamma_{1}.

Let
\gamma_{2}:=\gamma-(-1)^{k+1}\partial_{k+1}(\gamma^{\prime}*\{\bar{v}\}).
Then, as
\partial_{k}\circ\partial_{k+1}=0,

\gamma_{2}\in[\gamma].
From Remark
2.3
,
\bar{v}^{\prime}\notin\textnormal{vsupp}(\gamma_{2}),
while, from the definition of
\gamma_{2},

\bar{v}\in\textnormal{vsupp}(\gamma_{2})
and
\textnormal{vsupp}(\gamma_{2})\setminus\{\bar{v}\}\subseteq\textnormal{vsupp}(\gamma).
Consequently, because
\gamma
is a
k-
NTC with minimal vertex support,
\gamma_{2}
is also a
k-
NTC with minimal vertex support. Since
\bar{v}\in\textnormal{vsupp}(\gamma_{2}),
our claim follows.
∎

Lemma 4.6
.

Let
j\in\{1,\ldots,d\}
and
k
be such that
1\leq k<d.
Let
\gamma
be a
k-
NTC in
\mathcal{K}
with minimal vertex support. Then, the following statements hold:

•

If
\bar{t}\in\textnormal{vsupp}(\gamma)
and
\mathfrak{N}^{{}^{-}}_{j}(\bar{t})\cap\textnormal{vsupp}(\gamma)=\emptyset,
then
\bar{t}+\bar{e}_{j}\notin\textnormal{vsupp}(\gamma).

•

If
\bar{t}\in\textnormal{vsupp}(\gamma)
and
\mathfrak{N}^{{}^{+}}_{j}(\bar{t})\cap\textnormal{vsupp}(\gamma)=\emptyset,
then
\bar{t}-\bar{e}_{j}\notin\textnormal{vsupp}(\gamma).

Proof.

We only prove the first statement here; the other one follows similarly. From Lemma
2.2
,
\gamma\cap\textnormal{lk}(\bar{t})
is a
(k-1)-
NTC in
\mathcal{C}:=\textnormal{lk}(\bar{t})\cap\{\sigma\in\mathcal{K}:\sigma\subseteq\textnormal{vsupp}(\gamma)\},
i.e., the
(k-1)-
th Betti number of
\mathcal{C}
is non-trivial. Also,
\mathfrak{N}^{{}^{-}}_{j}(\bar{t})\cap\textnormal{vsupp}(\gamma)=\emptyset
implies that
\mathcal{F}_{0}(\mathcal{C})\subseteq\mathfrak{N}(\bar{t})\setminus\mathfrak{N}^{{}^{-}}_{j}(\bar{t}).
The desired result now follows from Lemma
4.5
.
∎

We are now ready to prove Theorem
4.4
.

Proof of Theorem
4.4
.

We use induction on
k
. The result is trivially true for
k=0.
Suppose the result holds for some
k-1.
Now consider an induced subcomplex
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\mathcal{K}
such that
\beta_{k}(C)\geq 1.
For the sake of contradiction, let the assumption below hold.

:\text{every $2k+2$ vertices in $\mathcal{C}$ have a pair $\bar{t},\bar{t}^{\prime}$ such that $\|\bar{t}-\bar{t}^{\prime}\|_{1}=1.$}

Since
\beta_{k}(\mathcal{C})\geq 1,
there exists at least one
k-
NTC in
\mathcal{C}
with minimal vertex support. Below we show that, under assumption
\Cr{a:pwDist},
no such cycle can exist. This gives the desired contradiction.

Let
\gamma
be an arbitrary
k-
NTC with minimal vertex support in
\mathcal{C}.
From Lemma
2.1
,
|\textnormal{vsupp}(\gamma)|\geq 2k+2.
By Assumption
\Cr{a:pwDist}
above, every
2k+2
vertices in
\textnormal{vsupp}(\gamma)
have a pair
\bar{t},\bar{t}^{\prime}
such that
\|\bar{t}-\bar{t}^{\prime}\|_{1}=1;
without loss of generality, let
\bar{0},\bar{e}_{1}\in\textnormal{vsupp}(\gamma).
Since
\gamma
is a
k-
NTC of minimal vertex support and since
\bar{0},\bar{e}_{1}\in\textnormal{vsupp}(\gamma),
it follows from Lemma
4.6
that
\mathfrak{N}^{{}^{-}}_{1}(\bar{0})\cap\textnormal{vsupp}(\gamma)\neq\emptyset
and
\mathfrak{N}^{{}^{+}}_{1}(\bar{e}_{1})\cap\textnormal{vsupp}(\gamma)\neq\emptyset.
Let
\bar{v}_{{}_{-}},\bar{v}_{{}_{+}}
respectively lie in
\mathfrak{N}^{{}^{-}}_{1}(\bar{0})\cap\textnormal{vsupp}(\gamma)
and
\mathfrak{N}^{{}^{+}}_{1}(\bar{0})\cap\textnormal{vsupp}(\gamma).

Through a series of claims, we first show that Assumption
\Cr{a:pwDist}
forces all vertices in
\textnormal{vsupp}(\gamma)
to be sufficiently close to either
\bar{0}
or
\bar{e}_{1}.
We then show that this violates Lemma
4.5
. Since
\gamma
is arbitrary, the desired result follows.

\textbf{Claim}_{1}:
For all
\bar{t}\in\textnormal{vsupp}(\gamma),

-1\leq\bar{t}(1)\leq 2.

For sake of contradiction, suppose that there exists
\bar{t}\in\textnormal{vsupp}(\gamma)
with
\bar{t}(1)\geq 3.
From Lemma
2.2
,
\gamma\cap\textnormal{lk}(\bar{t})
is a
(k-1)-
NTC. Hence, by induction hypothesis, there are
2k
vertices in
\mathfrak{N}(\bar{t})\cap\textnormal{vsupp}(\gamma)
such that their pairwise
\|\cdot\|_{1}-
distances are
\geq 2.
Similarly, it follows that there are
2k
vertices in
\mathfrak{N}(\bar{v}_{{}_{-}})\cap\textnormal{vsupp}(\gamma)
with pairwise
\|\cdot\|_{1}-
distances
\geq 2.
But then we have a contradiction to Assumption
\Cr{a:pwDist}
above. By a symmetric argument, one can obtain a contradiction if
\exists\bar{t}\in\textnormal{vsupp}(\gamma)
with
\bar{t}(1)\leq-2.
Thus, the above claim follows.

\textbf{Claim}_{2}:
For all
\bar{t}\in\textnormal{vsupp}(\gamma),

|\bar{t}(j)|\leq 2
for each
j\neq 1.

To see this, suppose that there exists a
\bar{t}\in\textnormal{vsupp}(\gamma)
with
|\bar{t}(j)|\geq 3
for some
j\neq 1.
Then, from Lemma
2.2
and the induction hypothesis, there exist
2k
vertices, say
\bar{t}_{1},\ldots,\bar{t}_{2k},
in
\mathfrak{N}(\bar{t})\cap\textnormal{vsupp}(\gamma)
with pairwise
\|\cdot\|_{1}-
distances
\geq 2.
If
\bar{t}(1)\geq 1,
then it is easy see that
\{\bar{t}_{1},\ldots,\bar{t}_{2k},\bar{v}_{{}_{-}},\bar{e}_{1}\}
is a set of
2k+2
vertices with pairwise
\|\cdot\|_{1}-
distances
\geq 2.
On the other hand, if
\bar{t}(1)\leq 0,
then
\{\bar{t}_{1},\ldots,\bar{t}_{2k},\bar{0},\bar{v}_{{}_{+}}\}
is a set of
2k+2
vertices with pairwise
\|\cdot\|_{1}-
distances
\geq 2.
Both contradict assumption
\Cr{a:pwDist}.
The above claim follows.

In fact, we can improve upon
\textbf{Claim}_{2}
as shown below.

\textbf{Claim}_{3}:
For all
\bar{t}\in\textnormal{vsupp}(\gamma),

|\bar{t}(j)|\leq 1
for each
j\neq 1.

Again, for sake of contradiction, assume there exists a
\bar{t}\in\textnormal{supp}(\gamma)
with
|\bar{t}(j)|=2
for some
j\geq 1.
For ease of exposition, first assume
\bar{t}(j)=2
and
\bar{t}(1)\geq 1.
From Lemma
2.2
and the induction hypothesis, there are
2k
vertices, say
\bar{t}_{1},\ldots,\bar{t}_{2k},
in
\mathfrak{N}(\bar{t})\cap\textnormal{vsupp}(\gamma)
with pairwise
\|\cdot\|_{1}-
distances
\geq 2.
By similar arguments as for
\textbf{Claim}_{1},
it now follows that for each
\bar{v}\in\textnormal{vsupp}(\gamma),

\bar{v}(j)\geq-1.
In fact, we now show that it cannot even be
-1;
not even in
\mathfrak{N}^{{}^{-}}_{1}(\bar{0})
or
\mathfrak{N}^{{}^{+}}_{1}(\bar{e}_{1}).
To see this, suppose there exists a
\bar{v}\in\textnormal{vsupp}(\gamma)
such that
\bar{v}(j)=-1.

•

Subcase
\bar{v}(1)\geq 1.
From
\textbf{Claim}_{1},
we have
\mathfrak{N}^{{}^{-}}_{1}(\bar{v}_{{}_{-}})\cap\textnormal{vsupp}(\gamma)=\emptyset.
Hence, from Lemma
4.6
,
\bar{v}_{{}_{-}}+\bar{e}_{1}\notin\textnormal{vsupp}(\gamma).
Combining this with
\bar{t}(1)\geq 1,
it follows that
\|\bar{v}_{{}_{-}}-\bar{t}_{i}\|_{1}\geq 2
for all
i\in\{1,\ldots,2k\}.
Also, since
\bar{t}(j)=2
and
\bar{v}(j)=-1,

\|\bar{v}-\bar{t}_{i}\|_{1}\geq 2
for all
i\in\{1,\ldots,2k\}.
As
\bar{v}_{{}_{-}}(1)=-1
and
\bar{v}(1)\geq 1,
we further have
\|\bar{v}_{{}_{-}}-\bar{v}\|_{1}\geq 2.
In other words,
\{\bar{t}_{1},\ldots,\bar{t}_{2k},\bar{v}_{{}_{-}},\bar{v}\}
