is a set of
2k+2
vertices with pairwise
\|\cdot\|_{1}-
distances
\geq 2.
This contradicts
\Cr{a:pwDist}
above.

•

Subcase
\bar{v}(1)\leq 0.
Again, from Lemma
2.2
and the induction hypothesis, there are
2k
vertices, say
\bar{v}_{1},\ldots,\bar{v}_{2k},
in
\mathfrak{N}(\bar{v})\cap\textnormal{vsupp}(\gamma)
with pairwise
\|\cdot\|_{1}-
distances
\geq 2.
From
\textbf{Claim}_{1},
we have
\mathfrak{N}^{{}^{+}}_{1}(\bar{v}_{{}_{+}})\cap\textnormal{vsupp}(\gamma)=\emptyset.
Hence, from Lemma
4.6
,
\bar{v}_{{}_{+}}-\bar{e}_{1}\notin\textnormal{vsupp}(\gamma).
Combining this with
\bar{v}(1)\leq 0,
we have
\|\bar{v}_{{}_{+}}-\bar{v}_{i}\|_{1}\geq 2
for all
i\in\{1,\ldots,2k\}.
As
\bar{v}(j)=-1
and
\bar{t}(j)=2,
we additionally have
\|\bar{t}-\bar{v}_{i}\|_{1}\geq 2
for all
i\in\{1,\ldots,2k\}.
Separately, from
\textbf{Claim}_{2},
we have
\mathfrak{N}^{{}^{+}}_{j}(\bar{t})\cap\textnormal{vsupp}(\gamma)=\emptyset.
Hence, from Lemma
4.6
,
\bar{t}-\bar{e}_{j}\notin\textnormal{vsupp}(\gamma).
This necessarily implies that
\|\bar{t}-\bar{v}_{{}_{+}}\|_{1}\geq 2.
Putting all the above together, it follows that
\{\bar{v}_{1},\ldots,\bar{v}_{2k},\bar{v}_{{}_{+}},\bar{t}\}
is a set of
2k+2
vertices with pairwise
\|\cdot\|_{1}-
distances
\geq 2.
This contradicts Assumption
\Cr{a:pwDist}
above.

Hence, it follows that there cannot exist
\bar{v}\in\textnormal{vsupp}(\gamma)
such that
\bar{v}(j)=-1.
In particular,
\mathfrak{N}^{{}^{-}}_{j}(\bar{e}_{1})\cap\textnormal{vsupp}(\gamma)=\emptyset.
But from Lemma
4.6
, we then immediately have
\bar{e}_{1}+\bar{e}_{j}\notin\textnormal{vsupp}(\gamma).
This means that
\|\bar{e}_{1}-\bar{t}_{i}\|_{1}\geq 2
for all
i\in\{1,\ldots,2k\}.
Consequently, as in the first subcase above,
\{\bar{t}_{1},\ldots,\bar{t}_{2k},\bar{v}_{{}_{-}},\bar{e}_{1}\}
is a set of
2k+2
vertices with pairwise
\|\cdot\|_{1}-
distances
\geq 2.
This contradicts Assumption
\Cr{a:pwDist}
again. So there exists no
\bar{t}\in\textnormal{vsupp}(\gamma)
such that
\bar{t}(1)\geq 1
and
\bar{t}(j)=2
for some
j\neq 1.
Similarly, by symmetric arguments, one can show that there cannot exist any
\bar{t}\in\textnormal{vsupp}(\gamma)
such that
|\bar{t}(j)|=2
for some
j\neq 1.
The desired claim now follows.

Combining the three claims above, it follows that all vertices in
\textnormal{vsupp}(\gamma)
must be sufficiently close to either
\bar{0}
or
\bar{e}_{1};
i.e., for all
\bar{t}\in\textnormal{vsupp}(\gamma),
either
\|\bar{t}-\bar{0}\|_{\infty}\leq 1
or
\|\bar{t}-\bar{e}_{1}\|_{\infty}\leq 1.
Consider
\bar{v}_{{}_{-}}
as defined earlier. From Lemma
2.2
,
\gamma\cap\textnormal{lk}(\bar{v}_{{}_{-}})
is a
(k-1)
-NTC in
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}:=\textnormal{lk}(\bar{v}_{{}_{-}})\cap\{\sigma\in\mathcal{C}:\sigma\subseteq\textnormal{vsupp}(\gamma)\},
i.e.,
\beta_{k-1}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime})\geq 1.
But observe that, since all vertices in
\textnormal{vsupp}(\gamma)
are sufficiently close to either
\bar{0}
or
\bar{e}_{1}
as described above,
\mathcal{F}_{0}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime})\subseteq\mathfrak{N}(-\bar{e}_{1})\setminus\mathfrak{N}^{{}^{-}}_{1}(-\bar{e}_{1}).
Further, since
\mathcal{C}
is an induced subcomplex of
\mathcal{K},
it follows that
\bar{0}\in\mathcal{C}^{\prime}.
Together, these contradict Lemma
4.5
. Thus,
\gamma
cannot exist as desired.
∎

We next prove the deterministic variant of Theorem
3.3
.
Let
\check{S}_{k}(\mathcal{K}),

\check{N}_{k}(\mathcal{K}),
and
\check{L}_{k}(\mathcal{K})
be the respective deterministic analogues of
\check{S}_{n,k}(u),

\check{N}_{n,k}(u),
and
\check{L}_{n,k}(u)
from Section
3
obtained by replacing
\mathcal{K}(n;u)
there with
\mathcal{K}.
The statement of Theorem
3.3
then translates to the following.

Theorem 4.7
.

For
0\leq k<d,

\check{S}_{k}(\mathcal{K})\leq\beta_{k}(\mathcal{K})\leq\check{S}_{k}(\mathcal{K})+\check{N}_{k}(\mathcal{K})+{\Cr{c:Betti}}_{,k}\,\check{L}_{k}(\mathcal{K}).

Proof.

The
k=0
case is trivially true. So suppose that
1\leq k<d.

Because
\mathcal{K}
is also a Vietoris-Rips complex, the induced subcomplex associated with a subgraph component that is isomorphic to
O_{k}
contributes exactly
1
to the
k-
th Betti number. From this, the lower bound is easy to see.

It now only remains to show the upper bound. Suppose that
k\geq 2.
As discussed above Lemma
2.1
,
\beta_{k}(\mathcal{K})=\sum_{\mathcal{C}}\beta_{k}(\mathcal{C}),
where the sum is over the isolated induced subcomplexes of
\mathcal{K}.
Using Lemma
2.1
and the standard inequality
\beta_{k}(\mathcal{C})\leq|\mathcal{F}_{k}(\mathcal{C})|
from the simplicial homology theory, we then have

\beta_{k}(\mathcal{K})\leq\check{S}_{k}(\mathcal{K})+\check{N}_{k}(\mathcal{K})+\sum_{\mathcal{C}:|\mathcal{F}_{0}(\mathcal{C})|\geq 2k+3}|\mathcal{F}_{k}(\mathcal{C})|\;\mathds{1}[\beta_{k}(\mathcal{C})\geq 1].

Consider the
1-
skeleton of an arbitrary
\mathcal{C}
with
|\mathcal{F}_{0}(\mathcal{C})|\geq 2k+3
and
\beta_{k}(\mathcal{C})\geq 1.
Using Theorem
4.4
, in this skeleton there exists a tree
G_{\mathcal{C}}
such that: (a) it has at least
2k+3
vertices, (b) it has a further subset of
2k+2
vertices having pairwise
\|\cdot\|_{1}-
distances at least two, and (c) its
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}-
diameter is at most
4k+3.
The vertex properties in (a) and (b) trivially hold in a tree with diameter
4k+3,
justifying the
4k+3
limit. Now let
\sigma
be any
k-
face in
\mathcal{C}.
Clearly, there exists a subgraph in
\mathcal{C}
connecting
\textnormal{vsupp}(\sigma)
to
G_{\mathcal{C}}.
Depending on the
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}-
distance of
\textnormal{vsupp}(\sigma)
from
G_{\mathcal{C}},
and removing edges if necessary, it is easy to see that there exists a subgraph isomorphic to one in
\mathcal{P}_{k}
such that
\textnormal{vsupp}(\sigma)
forms the only
(k+1)-
sized clique in this subgraph. Therefore,
|\mathcal{F}_{k}(\mathcal{C})|\mathds{1}[\beta_{k}(\mathcal{C})\geq 1]\leq\check{L}_{k}(\mathcal{C}).
The desired result now follows.

By making suitable changes to the above argument, in the exact same spirit as discussed below
[
22
, Fig. 3]
, the desired result follows for the
k=1
case as well.
∎

The rest of the section describes some useful properties concerning subgraph components in the
1-
skeleton of
\mathcal{K}
that are isomorphic to
O_{k}.
Fix
k\geq 1
and let
V\equiv\{\bar{t}_{1},\ldots,\bar{t}_{2k+2}\}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}^{d}
be such that the geometric graph
G
on these vertices, with respect to
\mathscr{B}_{\infty}(\cdot,\tfrac{1}{2}),
is isomorphic to
O_{k}.

The following result is a consequence of Theorem
4.4
and the fact that the induced subcomplex of
\mathcal{K}
associated with
G
has non-trivial
k-
th Betti number.

Corollary 4.8
.

For any
i_{1},i_{2}\in\{1,\ldots,2k+2\}
with
i_{1}\neq i_{2},

\|\bar{t}_{i_{1}}-\bar{t}_{i_{2}}\|_{1}\geq 2.

Observe that, for every vertex in
O_{k},
there is precisely one other vertex to which it is not connected to. The following result shows that the
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}-
distance between the corresponding vertex pairs in
G
is precisely
2.

Lemma 4.9
.

Fix an arbitrary
i\in\{1,\ldots,2k+2\}.
Then, there exists a unique
i^{\prime}
such that
\|\bar{t}_{i}-\bar{t}_{i^{\prime}}\|_{\infty}\geq 2;
in fact,
\|\bar{t}_{i}-\bar{t}_{i^{\prime}}\|_{\infty}=2.

Proof.

Fix an arbitrary
i.
Because
G
is isomorphic to
O_{k},
clearly, there exists a unique
i^{\prime}
such that
\|\bar{t}_{i}-\bar{t}_{i^{\prime}}\|_{\infty}\geq 2.
However, for each
j\neq i,i^{\prime},
we additionally have
\|\bar{t}_{j}-\bar{t}_{i}\|_{\infty}=\|\bar{t}_{j}-\bar{t}_{i^{\prime}}\|_{\infty}=1.
But the latter condition can hold only if
\|\bar{t}_{i}-\bar{t}_{i^{\prime}}\|_{\infty}=2.
The desired result thus follows.
∎

From the above result and the definition of
G,
the below statement is immediate.

Corollary 4.10
.

G
is
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}-
isometric to
O_{k}.

For a general
k,
the graph
G
can either be
\|\cdot\|_{1}-
isometric to
O_{k}
or not. We now show that, if
k=d-1,
then it has to be.

Lemma 4.11
.

If
k=d-1,
then
G
is also
\|\cdot\|_{1}-
isometric to
O_{d-1}.

Proof.

First, observe that
V
has
2d
vertices. Separately, from Corollary
4.10
, we have that, for each
i,
there is a unique
i^{\prime}
such that
\|\bar{t}_{i}-\bar{t}_{i^{\prime}}\|_{\infty}=2.
Hence,
V
can be written as
\{\bar{t}_{1},\bar{t}_{1^{\prime}},\ldots,\bar{t}_{d},\bar{t}_{d^{\prime}}\}.
Let
\mathcal{I}_{i}
be the set of coordinate indices in which
\bar{t}_{i}
and
\bar{t}_{i^{\prime}}
differ by
2;
this is clearly non-empty.

Fix some arbitrary
i,j.
From Corollary
4.10
,

\|\bar{t}_{j_{1}}-\bar{t}_{j_{2}}\|_{\infty}=1
for
j_{1}\in\{j,j^{\prime}\}
and
j_{2}\in\{i,i^{\prime}\}.
From Fact
4.2
, it then follows that, for each
l\in I_{i},

\bar{t}_{j}(l)=\bar{t}_{j^{\prime}}(l)=(\bar{t}_{i}(l)+\bar{t}_{i^{\prime}}(l))/2,
and viceversa. Consequently,
\mathcal{I}_{i}\cap\mathcal{I}_{j}=\emptyset.
As
i,j
were arbitrary, and since there are only
d
distinct coordinate indices, the desired result is easy to see.
∎

The next result states that, for general
k,
if
G
is indeed
\|\cdot\|_{1}-
isometric to
O_{k},
then its vertices have a unique ordered representation.

Lemma 4.12
.

G
is
\|\cdot\|_{1}-
isometric to
O_{k}
if and only if there is a unique
\bar{t}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
and a unique
\bar{\Omega}\equiv(\omega_{1},\ldots,\omega_{k+1})\in\tbinom{\langle d\rangle}{b_{k}}
such that the vertices of
G
are, up to permutation,

(\bar{t}+\bar{e}_{\omega_{1}},\bar{t}-\bar{e}_{\omega_{1}},\ldots,\bar{t}+\bar{e}_{\omega_{k+1}},\bar{t}-\bar{e}_{\omega_{k+1}}).

Proof.

We only show the necessary part of the statement as the other direction is obvious. Suppose
G
is
\|\cdot\|_{1}-
isometric to
O_{k}.
Then, using Corollary
4.10
, we have that, for any
i,
there exists a unique
i^{\prime}\neq i
such that
\|\bar{t}_{i}-\bar{t}_{i^{\prime}}\|_{\infty}=\|\bar{t}_{i}-\bar{t}_{i^{\prime}}\|_{1}=2;
further, for each
j_{1}\in\{i,i^{\prime}\}
and
j_{2}\notin\{i,i^{\prime}\},

\|\bar{t}_{j_{1}}-\bar{t}_{j_{2}}\|_{\infty}=1
and
\|\bar{t}_{j_{1}}-\bar{t}_{j_{2}}\|_{1}=2.
From these observations and Fact
4.2
, it is easy to see that the desired result holds with
\bar{t}=(\sum_{i=1}^{2k+2}\bar{t}_{i})/(2k+2).

∎

Suppose
k<d-1.
Then, it is possible that
G
is only isomorphic but not
\|\cdot\|_{1}-
isometric to
O_{k}.
The next result show that, while there is no unique representation, there does exist a common neighbour to all its vertices with respect to the
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}-
distance.

Lemma 4.13
.

Suppose
G
is not
\|\cdot\|_{1}-
isometric to
O_{k}.
Then, there is a
\bar{t}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
such that
\|\bar{t}-\bar{t}_{i}\|_{\infty}=1
for all
j.

Proof.

Fix an arbitrary
i.
It follows from Corollary
4.10
that there exists a unique
i^{\prime}\neq i
such that
\|\bar{t}_{i}-\bar{t}_{i^{\prime}}\|_{\infty}=2.
Now define
\bar{t}
using the following rule: for each
l\in\langle d\rangle,

\bar{t}(l):=\begin{cases}(\bar{t}_{i}(l)+\bar{t}_{i^{\prime}}(l))/2,&\text{ if $|\bar{t}_{i}(l)-\bar{t}_{i^{\prime}}(l)|=2,$}\\
\bar{t}_{i}(l),&\text{ otherwise. }\end{cases}

Keeping in mind
\|\bar{t}_{i}-\bar{t}_{i^{\prime}}\|_{\infty}=2,
it is easy to see that
\|\bar{t}-\bar{t}_{i}\|_{\infty}=\|\bar{t}-\bar{t}_{i^{\prime}}\|_{\infty}=1.

Now consider any arbitrary
j\notin\{i,i^{\prime}\}.
Again, from Corollary
4.10
, we have that
\|\bar{t}_{j}-\bar{t}_{j^{\prime}}\|_{\infty}=1
for all
j^{\prime}\in\{i,i^{\prime}\}.
From these observations and Fact
4.2
, it is easy to see that
\|\bar{t}_{j}-\bar{t}\|_{\infty}=1.

Since
j
is arbitrary, the desired result follows.
∎

Whenever
G
is only isomorphic but not
\|\cdot\|_{1}-
isometric to
O_{k},
our final result here shows that the vertex set of
G
can be partitioned into two non-empty subsets such that the pairwise
\|\cdot\|_{1}-
distances between vertices in the two subsets is at least
3.

Lemma 4.14
.

Suppose
G
is not
\|\cdot\|_{1}-
isometric to
O_{k}.
Then, its vertex set
V
can be partitioned into non-empty subsets
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
so that
1\leq|V_{1}|\leq k+1;
further, for every
\bar{t}_{j_{1}}\in V_{1}
and
\bar{t}_{j_{2}}\in V_{2},

\|\bar{t}_{j_{1}}-\bar{t}_{j_{2}}\|_{1}\geq 3.

Proof.

From Corollary
4.8
and the fact that
G
is not
\|\cdot\|_{1}-
isometric to
O_{k},
it follows that there exists a pair
i_{1},i_{2}
such that
\|\bar{t}_{i_{1}}-\bar{t}_{i_{2}}\|_{1}\geq 3.
With such a pair, partition
V
into two subsets
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
where

V_{1}:=\{\bar{t}_{j}:\|\bar{t}_{i_{1}}-\bar{t}_{j}\|_{1}\leq 2\}\text{ and }V_{2}:=V\setminus V_{1}.

Clearly,
V_{1}
contains
\bar{t}_{i_{1}}
and
V_{2}
contains
\bar{t}_{i_{2}};
so, both are non-empty. We now show that for
\bar{t}_{j_{1}}\in V_{1}
and
\bar{t}_{j_{2}}\in V_{2}

\|\bar{t}_{j_{1}}-\bar{t}_{j_{2}}\|_{1}\geq 3.

Fix
\bar{t}_{j_{1}}\in V_{1}
and
\bar{t}_{j_{2}}\in V_{2}.
From Corollary
4.10
, either

\|\bar{t}_{j_{1}}-\bar{t}_{j_{2}}\|_{\infty}=2\text{ or }\|\bar{t}_{j_{1}}-\bar{t}_{j_{2}}\|_{\infty}=1.

Keeping this in mind, we break our arguments into several cases:

1.

j_{1}=i_{1}:
Here the claim follows from the definition of
V_{1}.

2.

j_{1}\neq i_{1}
and
\|\bar{t}_{j_{1}}-\bar{t}_{j_{2}}\|_{\infty}=2:
Clearly, there exists a coordinate index
l
such that
|\bar{t}_{j_{1}}(l)-\bar{t}_{j_{2}}(l)|=2.
If there is more than one such coordinate index, then we are already done. So, consider the case when there is precisely one such
l.
It suffices to show that there is a coordinate index, other than
l,
where
\bar{t}_{j_{1}}
and
\bar{t}_{j_{2}}
differ by at least
1.
Due to Corollary
4.10
, it follows that
\|\bar{t}_{i_{1}}-\bar{t}_{j_{1}}\|_{\infty}=\|\bar{t}_{i_{1}}-\bar{t}_{j_{2}}\|_{\infty}=1.
Hence, using Fact
4.2
,
|\bar{t}_{i_{1}}(l)-\bar{t}_{j_{1}}(l)|=|\bar{t}_{i_{1}}(l)-\bar{t}_{j_{2}}(l)|=1.
From the definition of
V_{1},
we also have
\|\bar{t}_{i_{1}}-\bar{t}_{j_{2}}\|_{1}\geq 3.
Further, by combining the definition of
V_{1}
and Corollary
4.8
, we have
\|\bar{t}_{i_{1}}-\bar{t}_{j_{1}}\|_{1}=2.
These observations imply that, other than
l,

\bar{t}_{j_{2}}
must differ from
\bar{t}_{i_{1}}
in at least two other coordinates, and the value of the difference must be exactly
1;
while
\bar{t}_{j_{1}}
must vary from
\bar{t}_{i_{1}}
in only one other coordinate, with the difference being exactly one again. The desired claim is now easy to see.

3.

j_{1}\neq i_{1}
and
\|\bar{t}_{j_{1}}-\bar{t}_{j_{2}}\|_{\infty}=1:
From Lemma
4.9
, there exists a unique
j_{1}^{\prime}
such that
\|\bar{t}_{j_{1}}-\bar{t}_{j_{1}^{\prime}}\|_{\infty}=2.
The subsequent arguments are broken into further subcases.

(a)

j_{1}^{\prime}=i_{1}:
From the definition of
V_{1}
and Corollary
4.8
,
\|\bar{t}_{i_{1}}-\bar{t}_{j_{1}}\|_{1}=2.
This implies there is precisely one coordinate index
l
in which
\bar{t}_{i_{1}}
and
\bar{t}_{j_{1}}
differ and that
|\bar{t}_{i_{1}}(l)-\bar{t}_{j_{1}}(l)|=2.
Due to Corollary
4.10
,
\|\bar{t}_{j_{1}}-\bar{t}_{j_{2}}\|_{\infty}=\|\bar{t}_{i_{1}}-\bar{t}_{j_{2}}\|_{\infty}=1.
But, from the definition of
V_{1},

\|\bar{t}_{i_{1}}-\bar{t}_{j_{2}}\|_{1}\geq 3.
Combining these observations with Fact
4.2
, the desired claim is now easy to see.

(b)

j_{1}^{\prime}\neq i_{1}:
Again, from Corollary
4.10
,
\|\bar{t}_{\ell_{1}}-\bar{t}_{\ell_{2}}\|_{\infty}=1
for each
\ell_{1}\in\{j_{1},j_{1}^{\prime}\}
and
\ell_{2}\in\{i_{1},j_{2}\}.
Along with Fact
4.2
, the above implies that, for each coordinate index
l
where
|\bar{t}_{j_{1}}(l)-\bar{t}_{j_{1}^{\prime}}(l)|=2,
we have
|\bar{t}_{i_{1}}(l)-\bar{t}_{j_{2}}(l)|=0
and
|\bar{t}_{j_{1}}(l)-\bar{t}_{j_{2}}(l)|=1.
Let
\mathcal{I}
be the collection of all such
l.
Now fix an
l\in\mathcal{I}.
From the definition of
V_{1}
and Corollary
4.8
,
\|\bar{t}_{i_{1}}-\bar{t}_{j_{1}}\|_{1}=2.
Therefore,
\bar{t}_{i_{1}}
and
\bar{t}_{j_{2}}
differ in only one coordinate, other than
l,
and that difference is precisely
1.
But, from the definition of
V_{1},

\|\bar{t}_{i_{1}}-\bar{t}_{j_{2}}\|_{1}\geq 3.
Now, whether
\|\bar{t}_{i_{1}}-\bar{t}_{j_{2}}\|_{\infty}
equals
1
or
2,
the desired result is easy to see.

Since
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
are non-empty and
|V_{1}|+|V_{2}|=2k+2,
it is easy to see that
1\leq\min\{|V_{1}|,|V_{2}|\}\leq k+1.
Hence, by using the above arguments and interchanging the labels of
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
if necessary, we have the desired result.
∎

5
Asymptotic Behavior of Betti Approximators

The intermediate results concerning the asymptotic behaviour of Betti approximators from Section
3
are proved here. Often, some technical results are needed. Their proofs are given either in Section
6
or the Appendix.

Using (
3.4
), (
3.7
), (
3.8
), and Lemma
4.12
, we first make the following observations.

Remark 5.1
.

For
k=0,

S_{n,0}
is the number of vertices in
\mathcal{K}(n;u).
On the other hand, for
k\geq 1,

S_{n,k}(u)
is roughly the number of subgraphs in
\mathcal{K}(n;u)
which are isomorphic and
\|\cdot\|_{1}-
isometric to
O_{k}.
We say ‘roughly’ because
S_{n,k}(u)
counts additional subgraphs at the boundary of
\Gamma_{n}
which cannot be part of
\mathcal{K}(n;u).
Separately, and as remarked earlier in Section
3
, note that
S_{n,k}(u)
does not require that these subgraphs be components themselves.

Remark 5.2
.

For
k\geq 1,

N_{n,k}(u)
overcounts (by a constant factor) the number of subgraphs in
\mathcal{K}(n;u)
that are isomorphic but not
\|\cdot\|_{1}-
isometric to
O_{k}.
The overcounting is due to the two facts: (i) the same subgraph is counted for more than one
\bar{t},
and (ii) extra subgraphs are counted at the boundary of
\Gamma_{n}
that cannot be part of
\mathcal{K}(n;u)
. But this does not matter for us, since it will only add a constant factor in the associated estimates.

Remark 5.3
.

In the same spirit as in Remark
5.2
,
L_{n,k}(u)
overestimates the number of subgraphs in
\mathcal{K}(n;u)
that is isomorphic to a graph in
\mathcal{P}_{k}.
In particular, we note that
L_{n,0}(u)
is at least twice the number of edges in
\mathcal{K}(n;u).

Separately, using (
3.6
) and Lemma
4.14
, we note the following.

Remark 5.4
.

For each
G\equiv(V,E)\in\mathcal{N}_{k},
Lemma
4.14
gives a partition of
V
with certain properties. It is not difficult to see that such a partition is not unique. For convenience, however, we shall henceforth assume that each
G
is uniquely associated with one such partition.

We are now ready to prove Lemma
3.5
.

Proof of Lemma
3.5
.

We prove each statement separately.

•

Arguments for Statement
1
: Consider the
k=0
case first. From Remark
5.1
above, the lower bound follows trivially. It remains to show the upper bound. Note that a vertex in
\mathcal{K}(n;u)
can either be isolated or not. Separately, given a graph, recall that a connected component with
i
vertices must have at least
i-1
edges within it. Therefore, the number of vertices in a connected component, with at least two vertices, is bounded from above by twice the number of edges in it. From these observations and Remark
5.3
, the upper bound is easy to see.

Now suppose that
1\leq k<d.
For
(\bar{t},\,\bar{\Omega})\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\times\tbinom{\langle d\rangle}{b_{k}},
let
G_{\bar{t},\bar{\Omega}}
be the geometric graph on the vertex set
\{\bar{t}\pm\bar{e}_{\omega_{1}},\ldots,\bar{t}\pm\bar{e}_{\omega_{b_{k}}}\}
with respect to
\mathscr{B}_{\infty}(\cdot,\tfrac{1}{2}).
Then, from (
3.4
), we have

S_{n,k}(u)=\sum_{(\bar{t},\,\bar{\Omega})\in\Gamma_{n}\times\tbinom{\langle d\rangle}{b_{k}}}\mathds{1}[\bar{X}_{\bar{t},\bar{\Omega}}\geq u\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{2b_{k}}]\;\mathds{1}[G_{\bar{t},\bar{\Omega}}\text{ forms a component}]\\[8.61108pt]
+\sum_{(\bar{t},\,\bar{\Omega})\in\Gamma_{n}\times\tbinom{\langle d\rangle}{b_{k}}}\mathds{1}[\bar{X}_{\bar{t},\bar{\Omega}}\geq u\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{2b_{k}}]\;\mathds{1}[G_{\bar{t},\bar{\Omega}}\text{ does not form a component}].

(5.1)

Therefore, it follows from Remark
5.1
and the definition of
\check{S}_{n,k}(u)
that

\check{S}_{n,k}(u)\leq\sum_{(\bar{t},\,\bar{\Omega})\in\Gamma_{n}\times\tbinom{\langle d\rangle}{b_{k}}}\mathds{1}[\bar{X}_{\bar{t},\bar{\Omega}}\geq u\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{2b_{k}}]\;\mathds{1}[G_{\bar{t},\bar{\Omega}}\text{ forms a component}].

From this, we get the lower bound.

With regards to the upper bound, first observe that

\sum_{(\bar{t},\,\bar{\Omega})\in\Gamma_{n}\times\tbinom{\langle d\rangle}{b_{k}}}\mathds{1}[\bar{X}_{\bar{t},\bar{\Omega}}\geq u\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{2b_{k}}]\;\mathds{1}[G_{\bar{t},\bar{\Omega}}\text{ forms a component}]\leq\check{S}_{n,k}(u)+D_{n,k}(u).

On the other hand, from the definition of
\check{L}_{n,k}(u),
we have

\sum_{(\bar{t},\,\bar{\Omega})\in\Gamma_{n}\times\tbinom{\langle d\rangle}{b_{k}}}\mathds{1}[\bar{X}_{\bar{t},\bar{\Omega}}\geq u\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{2b_{k}}]\;\mathds{1}[G_{\bar{t},\bar{\Omega}}\text{ does not form a component}]\leq\check{L}_{n,k}(u).

From these observations and (
5.1
), the upper bound is easy to see. The desired result follows.

•

Arguments for Statement
2
: Consider the definition of
\check{N}_{n,k}(u),
(
3.7
), Lemmas
4.13
and
4.11
, and Remark
5.2
. By the same argument used for deriving the lower bound above, the desired result follows.

•

Arguments for Statement
3
: From the definition of
\check{L}_{n,k}(u),
(
3.8
), and Remark
5.3
, the desired result follows trivially.

This completes the proof.
∎

We now aim to prove Lemma
3.7
. But, for that, we first need probability estimates for the events associated with the indicators in (
3.4
), (
3.7
), (
3.8
), and (
3.9
). Due to the stationarity assumption on field
X
(see Section
1
), it suffices to obtain these estimates for
\bar{t}=0.
For the
k\geq 1
case in (
3.4
) and (
3.9
), by additionally using the
\|\cdot\|_{1}-
isotropy assumption on
X,
it in fact follows that we only need to consider
\bar{\Omega}=\langle b_{k}\rangle.
The next result gives these bounds. Its proof is in Section
6
, p.
6
, and uses Lemmas
2.4
and
2.5
; the Savage condition holds due to our assumptions in Table
1
.

Lemma 5.5
.

Let
\{u_{n}\}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{\infty}
be such that
\lim_{n\to\infty}u_{n}=\infty.
Then, the following claims are true.

1.

If
\Cr{a:r0Asm}
holds, then
\left|\dfrac{\mathbb{P}\{X_{\bar{0}}\geq u_{n}\}}{\tau_{0}u_{n}^{-2b_{0}}\exp[-a_{0}u_{n}^{2}]}-1\right|=O\left(\dfrac{1}{u_{n}^{2}}\right).

2.

Suppose
1\leq k<d.
If
\Cr{a:r0Asm}
and
\Cr{a:r2Asm}
hold, then
\left|\dfrac{\mathbb{P}\{\bar{X}_{\bar{0},\langle b_{k}\rangle}\geq u_{n}\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{2b_{k}}\}}{\left[\tau_{k}/\tbinom{d}{b_{k}}\right]u_{n}^{-2b_{k}}\exp[-a_{k}u_{n}^{2}]}-1\right|=O\left(\dfrac{1}{u_{n}^{2}}\right).

3.

Suppose
1\leq k<d-1.
If
\Cr{a:r0Asm},\Cr{a:r2Asm},
and
\Cr{a:rqAsm}
hold, and
G\in\mathcal{N}_{k},
then

\mathbb{P}\{\bar{X}_{\bar{0},G}\geq u_{n}\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{2b_{k}}\}=O\left(u_{n}^{-2b_{k}}\exp[-\varrho_{k}\,u_{n}^{2}]\right).

4.

If
\Cr{a:r0Asm}
and
\Cr{a:r1Asm}
hold, then

\mathbb{P}\{(X_{\bar{0}},X_{\bar{e}_{1}})\geq u_{n}\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{2}\}=O\left(u_{n}^{-(2b_{0}+1)}\exp[-\varphi_{0}\,u_{n}^{2}]\right).

5.

Suppose
1\leq k<d.
If
\Cr{a:r0Asm},\Cr{a:r1Asm},
and
\Cr{a:ComC}(k)
hold, then

\mathbb{P}\{(\bar{X}_{\bar{0},\langle k+1\rangle},X_{\bar{0}})\geq u_{n}\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{2b_{k}+1}\}=O\left(u_{n}^{-(2b_{k}+1)}\exp[-\varphi_{k}\,u_{n}^{2}]\right),

6.

Suppose
0\leq k<d.
If
\Cr{a:r0Asm},\Cr{a:r1Asm},

\Cr{a:r2LAsm},
and
\Cr{a:ComC}(k)
hold, and
G\in\mathcal{P}_{k},
then

\mathbb{P}\{\bar{X}_{\bar{0},G}\geq u_{n}\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{|V|}\}=O\left(u_{n}^{-(2b_{k}+1)}\exp[-\varphi_{k}\,u_{n}^{2}]\right),

We are now ready to prove Lemma
3.7
.

Proof of Lemma
3.7
.

Each statement is proved individually.

•

Arguments for Statement
1
: First consider the case
1\leq k<d.
Using (
3.4
), we have

\mathbb{E}[S_{n,k}(u_{n})]=\sum_{(\bar{t},\,\bar{\Omega})\in\Gamma_{n}\times\tbinom{\langle d\rangle}{b_{k}}}\mathbb{P}\{\bar{X}_{\bar{t},\bar{\Omega}}\geq u\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{2b_{k}}\},

where
\bar{X}_{\bar{t},\bar{\Omega}}
is as in (
3.5
). For each
(\bar{t},\,\bar{\Omega})\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\times\tbinom{\langle d\rangle}{b_{k}},
note that the pairwise
\|\cdot\|_{1}-
distances between the indices involved in the definition of
\bar{X}_{\bar{t},\bar{\Omega}}
is exactly
2.
Consequently, using the fact that the field
X
is both stationary and
\|\cdot\|_{1}-
isotropic, it follows that

\mathbb{E}[S_{n,k}(u_{n})]=(2n+1)^{d}\binom{d}{b_{k}}\mathbb{P}\{\bar{X}_{\bar{0},\langle b_{k}\rangle}\geq u_{n}\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{2b_{k}}\}.

From (
1.4
), we then have

\frac{\mathbb{E}[S_{n,k}(u_{n})]}{\lambda_{n,k}(u_{n})}=\dfrac{\mathbb{P}\{\bar{X}_{\bar{0},\langle b_{k}\rangle}\geq u_{n}\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{2b_{k}}\}}{\left[\tau_{k}/\tbinom{d}{b_{k}}\right]u_{n}^{-2b_{k}}\exp[-a_{k}u_{n}^{2}]}.

(5.2)

The desired result now follows from Lemma
5.5
.
2
.

The case
k=0
follows similarly using Lemma
5.5
.
1
, except, no condition on
\rho_{2}
is needed here.

•

Arguments for Statement
2
: The expectation bound is trivially true when
k=0
or
d-1.
Suppose that
1\leq k<d-1.
From (
3.7
) and the fact that the field
X
is stationary,

\mathbb{E}[N_{n,k}(u_{n})]=(2n+1)^{d}\sum_{G\in\mathcal{N}_{k}}\mathbb{P}\{\bar{X}_{\bar{0},G}\geq u_{n}\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{2b_{k}}\}.

Therefore, from (
1.4
),

\frac{\mathbb{E}[N_{n,k}(u_{n})]}{\lambda_{n,k}(u_{n})}\leq\frac{|\mathcal{N}_{k}|\left[\sup_{G\in\mathcal{N}_{k}}\mathbb{P}\{\bar{X}_{\bar{0},G}\geq u_{n}\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{2k+2}\}\right]}{\tau_{k}u_{n}^{-2b_{k}}\exp[-a_{k}u_{n}^{2}]}.

Since
|\mathcal{N}_{k}|
is finite, by invoking Lemma
5.5
.
3
, the desired bound follows.

It only remains to show that
\varrho_{k}>a_{k}
for
1\leq k<d-1.
But, from (
1.2
) and (
3.11
),

\varrho_{k}-a_{k}=\frac{(2b_{k}-1)(\rho_{2}-\rho_{3})(1-\rho_{3}+(b_{k}-1)(\rho_{2}-\rho_{3}))}{(1-\rho_{3}^{2}+2(b_{k}-1)(\rho_{2}-\rho_{3}^{2}))(1+(2b_{k}-1)\rho_{2})}>0,

where the positivity follows since
0\leq\rho_{3}<\rho_{2}<1,
which itself holds on due to
\Cr{a:r0Asm},

\Cr{a:r2Asm},
and
\Cr{a:rqAsm}.
This completes the proof.

•

Arguments for Statement
3
: Using (
3.8
), Lemma
5.5
.
6
, and similar arguments as in the proof of Statement
2
above, the expectation bound is easy see. It only remains to show
\varphi_{k}>a_{k}
for
k\geq 0.

Using (
1.3
), (
1.2
), and (
3.12
), note that

\varphi_{k}-a_{k}=(1-2a_{k}\rho_{1})^{2}/[2(1-2a_{k}\rho_{1}^{2})].

(5.3)

For the
k=0
case, the expression on the right is positive due to
\Cr{a:r0Asm}
and
\Cr{a:r1Asm};
for the
1\leq k<d
case, it is positive due to
\Cr{a:ComC}(k)
and the facts that
\rho_{2}\geq 0
and
0\leq\rho_{1}<1,
which itself hold due to
\Cr{a:r0Asm},

\Cr{a:r1Asm},
and
\Cr{a:r2LAsm}.
We are now done.

•

Arguments for Statement
4
: The
k=0
case is trivial. So, suppose that
1\leq k<d.
A simple observation here is that
|\{(\bar{t},\,\bar{\Omega})\in\Gamma_{n}\times\tbinom{\langle d\rangle}{b_{k}}:\|\bar{t}\|_{\infty}=n\}|\leq 2d(2n+1)^{d-1}.
Further, from Lemma
5.5
.
2
,

\dfrac{\mathbb{P}\{\bar{X}_{\bar{0},\langle b_{k}\rangle}\geq u_{n}\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{2b_{k}}\}}{\left[\tau_{k}/\tbinom{d}{b_{k}}\right]u_{n}^{-2b_{k}}\exp[-a_{k}u_{n}^{2}]}=O\left(1+\frac{1}{u_{n}^{2}}\right)=O(1),

where the latter holds since
u_{n}\to\infty.
Arguing now as in the proof of Statement
1
above, the desired result is easy to see.

This completes the proof.
∎

Our next objective is to establish Theorem
3.8
by making use of Theorem
2.9
. As mentioned earlier, the
k=0
case of this result has been proved in
[
18
, Theorem 3.6]
. That proof makes use of Lemma 3.4 there, which discusses several covariance bounds concerning the indicators that add up to give
S_{n,0}(u);
see (
3.4
) for the definition of the latter. Below we restate a few of these covariance bounds in a form that is convenient to us. While these bounds have been shown in
[
18
]
, they also follow from our proof for Lemma
5.7
stated below.

From now on, whenever appropriate, we shall write
\mathds{1}[X_{\bar{t}}\geq u]
as
\mathds{1}_{\bar{t}}(u).

Lemma 5.6
.

Let
\bar{t},\bar{t}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
and
\{u_{n}\}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{\infty}
be such that
\lim_{n\to\infty}u_{n}=\infty.
Let
a_{0}
and
b_{0}
be as in (
1.3
). Suppose
\Cr{a:r0Asm},

\Cr{a:r1Asm},
and
\Cr{a:r2LAsm}
hold. Then, the following statements are true.

1.

\textnormal{Cov}[\mathds{1}_{\bar{t}}(u_{n}),\mathds{1}_{\bar{t}^{\prime}}(u_{n})]\geq 0
for all
n\geq 0.

2.

If
\|\bar{t}-\bar{t}^{\prime}\|_{1}=q\geq 1,
then
\textnormal{Cov}[\mathds{1}_{\bar{t}}(u_{n}),\mathds{1}_{\bar{t}^{\prime}}(u_{n})]=O\left(u_{n}^{-4b_{0}}\exp\left[\dfrac{-2a_{0}u_{n}^{2}}{1+2a_{0}\rho_{q}}\right]\right).

3.

If
\|\bar{t}-\bar{t}^{\prime}\|_{1}=q\geq 1,
then

\textnormal{Cov}[\mathds{1}_{\bar{t}}(u_{n}),\mathds{1}_{\bar{t}^{\prime}}(u_{n})]=O\left(\rho_{q}u_{n}^{-(4b_{0}-2)}\exp\left[\dfrac{-2a_{0}u_{n}^{2}}{1+2a_{0}\rho_{q}}\right]\right)=O\left(\rho_{q}\exp\left[\dfrac{-2a_{0}u_{n}^{2}}{1+2a_{0}\rho_{q}}\right]\right).

In each statement, the constants involved in the
O
notation are independent of both
\bar{t}
and
\bar{t}^{\prime}.

In order to handle the
1\leq k<d
case in Theorem
3.8
, our first aim is to obtain similar covariance bounds for the indicators that sum up to
S_{n,k}(u);
the latter is as defined in (
3.4
). These bounds are stated next.

Henceforth, as and when convenient, we shall shorten
\mathds{1}[\bar{X}_{\bar{t},\bar{\Omega}}\geq u\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{2b_{k}}]
to
\mathds{1}_{\bar{t},\bar{\Omega}}(u).
Separately, keeping in mind that the covariance sequence
\{\rho_{q}\}
may not be monotonically decreasing, for
(\bar{t},\,\bar{\Omega}),(\bar{t}^{\prime},\bar{\Omega}^{\prime})\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\times\tbinom{\langle d\rangle}{b_{k}},
let

\tilde{\rho}_{(\bar{t},\,\bar{\Omega}),(\bar{t}^{\prime},\bar{\Omega}^{\prime})}:=\max\left\{\rho_{\|[\bar{t}+\alpha\bar{e}_{\omega}]-[\bar{t}^{\prime}+\alpha^{\prime}\bar{e}_{\omega^{\prime}}]\|_{1}}:\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\in\{-1,+1\},\omega\in\bar{\Omega},\omega^{\prime}\in\bar{\Omega}^{\prime}\right\}.

(5.4)

The triangle inequality shows that, whenever
\tilde{\rho}_{(\bar{t},\,\bar{\Omega}),(\bar{t}^{\prime},\bar{\Omega}^{\prime})}=\rho_{q},

\max\{q-2,0\}\leq\|\bar{t}-\bar{t}^{\prime}\|_{1}\leq q+2.

(5.5)

From this, it immediately follows that there exists a
k-
dependent positive constant
,k
such that, for any
(\bar{t},\,\bar{\Omega})\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\times\tbinom{\langle d\rangle}{b_{k}}
and
q\geq 1,

|\{(\bar{t}^{\prime},\bar{\Omega}^{\prime})\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\times\tbinom{\langle d\rangle}{b_{k}}:\tilde{\rho}_{(\bar{t},\,\bar{\Omega}),(\bar{t}^{\prime},\bar{\Omega}^{\prime})}=\rho_{q}\}|\leq{\Cr{c:upBdRho}}_{,k}\;q^{d-1}.

(5.6)

Lemma 5.7
.

Fix
k
such that
1\leq k<d.
Let
(\bar{t},\,\bar{\Omega}),(\bar{t}^{\prime},\bar{\Omega}^{\prime})\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\times\tbinom{\langle d\rangle}{b_{k}},
and
\{u_{n}\}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{\infty}
be such that
\lim_{n\to\infty}u_{n}=\infty.
Let
a_{k}
and
b_{k}
be as in (
1.2
) and let
\varphi_{k}
be as in (
3.12
). Suppose
\Cr{a:r0Asm},\Cr{a:r1Asm},\Cr{a:r2LAsm},
and
\Cr{a:ComC}(k)
hold. Then, the following statements are true.

1.

\textnormal{Cov}[\mathds{1}_{\bar{t},\bar{\Omega}}(u_{n}),\mathds{1}_{\bar{t}^{\prime},\bar{\Omega}^{\prime}}(u_{n})]\geq 0
for all
n.

2.

If
(\bar{t},\,\bar{\Omega})\neq(\bar{t}^{\prime},\bar{\Omega}^{\prime}),
then

\textnormal{Cov}[\mathds{1}_{\bar{t},\bar{\Omega}}(u_{n}),\mathds{1}_{\bar{t}^{\prime},\bar{\Omega}^{\prime}}(u_{n})]=O\left(u_{n}^{-(2b_{k}+1)}\exp[-\varphi_{k}u_{n}^{2}]\right).

3.

If
\tilde{\rho}_{(\bar{t},\,\bar{\Omega}),(\bar{t}^{\prime},\bar{\Omega}^{\prime})}=\rho_{q}
with
q\geq 1,
then

\textnormal{Cov}[\mathds{1}_{\bar{t},\bar{\Omega}}(u_{n}),\mathds{1}_{\bar{t}^{\prime},\bar{\Omega}^{\prime}}(u_{n})]=O\left(u_{n}^{-4b_{k}}\exp\left[\frac{-2a_{k}u_{n}^{2}}{1+2a_{k}\rho_{q}}\right]\right).

4.

If
\tilde{\rho}_{(\bar{t},\,\bar{\Omega}),(\bar{t}^{\prime},\bar{\Omega}^{\prime})}=\rho_{q}
with
q\geq 1,
then

\textnormal{Cov}[\mathds{1}_{\bar{t},\bar{\Omega}}(u_{n}),\mathds{1}_{\bar{t}^{\prime},\bar{\Omega}^{\prime}}(u_{n})]=O\left(\rho_{q}\;u_{n}^{-(4b_{k}-2)}\exp\left[\frac{-2a_{k}u_{n}^{2}}{1+2a_{k}\rho_{q}}\right]\right).

In each statement, while the constants involved in the
O
notation do depend on
k,
they are independent of both
(\bar{t},\,\bar{\Omega})
and
(\bar{t}^{\prime},\bar{\Omega}^{\prime}).

We prove this result in Section
6
, p.
6
. Statements
1
,
2
, and
3
of this result follow by an application of Lemmas
2.4
and
2.5
; these ideas are multivariate extensions of those discussed in the proof of
[
18
, Lemma 3.4]
. In contrast, our proof of Statement
4
above is significantly different to the one used in
[
18
]
to derive (the analogous version of) Lemma
5.6
.
3
. There, given that the setup is much simpler, the proof proceeds via first principles. In contrast, here we first approximate the desired covariance by a definite integral and then make use of Lemma
A.4
, which provides bounds on the integrand.

Proof of Theorem
3.8
.

Because
\Cr{a:r0Asm},\Cr{a:r1Asm},

\Cr{a:ComC}(k),
and
\Cr{a:r2LAsm}
hold, both
1+(2b_{k}-1)\rho_{2}-2b_{k}\rho_{1}
and
\rho_{1}
lie in
(0,1),
while
\rho_{2}\in[0,1).
Using this, it is easy to see that
0<\vartheta_{k}<1,
as desired.

It now remains to establish the total variation bounds. The
k=0
case has been proved in
[
18
, Theorem 3.6]
. We only deal with the
1\leq k<d
case here. So, fix one such
k.

For any
n\geq 0,
it is not difficult to see from (
3.4
), Theorems
2.9
and
2.11
, and Lemma
5.7
.
1
that

\displaystyle\|S_{n,k}(u_{n})-\textnormal{Poi}(\mathbb{E}[S_{n,k}(u_{n})])\|_{\textnormal{TV}}

(5.7)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1-e^{-\lambda_{n,k}(u_{n})}}{\lambda_{n,k}(u_{n})}\Bigg[\sum_{(\bar{t},\,\bar{\Omega})\in\Gamma_{n}\times\tbinom{\langle d\rangle}{b_{k}}}\hskip-5.0pt[\mathbb{E}[\mathds{1}_{\bar{t},\bar{\Omega}}(u_{n})]]^{2}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\bar{t},\,\bar{\Omega}),(\bar{t}^{\prime},\bar{\Omega}^{\prime})\in\Gamma_{n}\times\tbinom{\langle d\rangle}{b_{k}},\\
(\bar{t},\,\bar{\Omega})\neq(\bar{t}^{\prime},\bar{\Omega}^{\prime})\end{subarray}}\hskip-5.0pt\textnormal{Cov}[\mathds{1}_{\bar{t},\bar{\Omega}}(u_{n}),\mathds{1}_{\bar{t}^{\prime},\bar{\Omega}^{\prime}}(u_{n})]\Bigg]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\lambda_{n,k}(u_{n})}\Bigg[\sum_{(\bar{t},\,\bar{\Omega})\in\Gamma_{n}\times\tbinom{\langle d\rangle}{b_{k}}}\hskip-5.0pt[\mathbb{E}[\mathds{1}_{\bar{t},\bar{\Omega}}(u_{n})]]^{2}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\bar{t},\,\bar{\Omega}),(\bar{t}^{\prime},\bar{\Omega}^{\prime})\in\Gamma_{n}\times\tbinom{\langle d\rangle}{b_{k}},\\
(\bar{t},\,\bar{\Omega})\neq(\bar{t}^{\prime},\bar{\Omega}^{\prime})\end{subarray}}\hskip-5.0pt\textnormal{Cov}[\mathds{1}_{\bar{t},\bar{\Omega}}(u_{n}),\mathds{1}_{\bar{t}^{\prime},\bar{\Omega}^{\prime}}(u_{n})]\Bigg],

where the last relation follows since
1-e^{-x}\leq 1.
Since
\Cr{a:r1Asm}
and
\Cr{a:r2LAsm}
imply
\Cr{a:r2Asm}
and also because
\vartheta_{k}/2b_{k}\in(0,1),
the desired result clearly follows from the following two claims:

1.

If
\Cr{a:r0Asm}
and
\Cr{a:r2Asm}
hold, then
\dfrac{1}{\lambda_{n,k}(u_{n})}\sum_{(\bar{t},\,\bar{\Omega})\in\Gamma_{n}\times\tbinom{\langle d\rangle}{b_{k}}}[\mathbb{E}[\mathds{1}_{\bar{t},\bar{\Omega}}(u_{n})]]^{2}=O\left(\dfrac{1}{n^{d}}\right).

2.

If
\Cr{a:r0Asm},\Cr{a:r1Asm},\Cr{a:ComC}(k),\Cr{a:PoiG},
and
\Cr{a:r2LAsm},
hold, then

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\lambda_{n,k}(u_{n})}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\bar{t},\,\bar{\Omega}),(\bar{t}^{\prime},\bar{\Omega}^{\prime})\in\Gamma_{n}\times\tbinom{\langle d\rangle}{b_{k}}\\
(\bar{t},\,\bar{\Omega})\neq(\bar{t}^{\prime},\bar{\Omega}^{\prime})\end{subarray}}\textnormal{Cov}[\mathds{1}_{\bar{t},\bar{\Omega}}(u_{n}),\mathds{1}_{\bar{t}^{\prime},\bar{\Omega}^{\prime}}(u_{n})]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O\left(n^{-d\vartheta_{k}/(2b_{k})}[\log n]^{-(1-\vartheta_{k})/2}+\frac{\log n}{n}\sum_{q=1}^{2dn}\rho_{q}\right).

In the remainder of this proof, we establish the above bounds.

•

Arguments for Claim
1
: We have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\lambda_{n,k}(u_{n})}\sum_{(\bar{t},\,\bar{\Omega})\in\Gamma_{n}\times\tbinom{\langle d\rangle}{b_{k}}}[\mathbb{E}[\mathds{1}_{\bar{t},\bar{\Omega}}(u_{n})]]^{2}

(5.8)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(2n+1)^{d}\tbinom{d}{b_{k}}[\mathbb{P}\{\bar{X}_{\bar{0},\langle b_{k}\rangle}\geq u_{n}\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{2b_{k}}\}]^{2}/\lambda_{n,k}(u_{n})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\lambda_{n,k}(u_{n})}{(2n+1)^{d}\tbinom{d}{b_{k}}}\left[\frac{\mathbb{P}\{\bar{X}_{\bar{0},\langle b_{k}\rangle}\geq u_{n}\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{2b_{k}}\}}{\left[\tau_{k}/\tbinom{d}{b_{k}}\right]u_{n}^{-2b_{k}}\exp[-a_{k}u_{n}^{2}]}\right]^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\lambda_{n,k}(u_{n})}{(2n+1)^{d}\tbinom{d}{b_{k}}}O\left(1+\frac{1}{u_{n}^{2}}+\frac{1}{u_{n}^{4}}\right),

where the first relation is due to the stationarity and
\|\cdot\|_{1}-
isotropy of the field
X,
the second is because of (
1.4
), while third one is a consequence of Lemma
5.5
.
2
. The desired result now follows from the fact that
u_{n}\to\infty
and
\lambda_{n,k}(u_{n})\to\lambda,
a constant.

•

Arguments for Claim
2
: Pick a
\delta
satisfying

0<(4a_{k}+1)\delta<\frac{2a_{k}\rho_{1}}{1+2a_{k}\rho_{1}}<1.

(5.9)

While the effectiveness of this choice will become clear below, the choice itself can be made because
\Cr{a:r0Asm},\Cr{a:r1Asm},
and
\Cr{a:r2LAsm}
ensure
\rho_{1}\in(0,1)
and
\rho_{2}\in[0,1).
For any
(\bar{t},\,\bar{\Omega}),(\bar{t}^{\prime},\bar{\Omega}^{\prime})\in\Gamma_{n}\times\tbinom{\langle d\rangle}{b_{k}},
it is easy to see from (
5.5
) that
\tilde{\rho}_{(\bar{t},\,\bar{\Omega}),(\bar{t}^{\prime},\bar{\Omega}^{\prime})}=\rho_{q}
for some
q\in\{0,\ldots,2dn+2\}.
Keeping this in mind, we rewrite the covariance sum in the claim as
T_{1}(n)+\ldots+T_{4}(n),
where

T_{1}(n):=\frac{1}{\lambda_{n,k}(u_{n})}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\bar{t},\,\bar{\Omega}),(\bar{t}^{\prime},\bar{\Omega}^{\prime})\in\Gamma_{n}\times\tbinom{\langle d\rangle}{b_{k}}:\\[1.50694pt]
(\bar{t},\,\bar{\Omega})\,\neq\,(\bar{t}^{\prime},\bar{\Omega}^{\prime}),\;\tilde{\rho}_{(\bar{t},\,\bar{\Omega}),(\bar{t}^{\prime},\bar{\Omega}^{\prime})}\,=\,\rho_{0}\end{subarray}}\textnormal{Cov}[\mathds{1}_{\bar{t},\bar{\Omega}}(u_{n}),\mathds{1}_{\bar{t}^{\prime},\bar{\Omega}^{\prime}}(u_{n})],

(5.10)

T_{2}(n):=\frac{1}{\lambda_{n,k}(u_{n})}\sum_{q:\rho_{q}\geq\delta,\;q\neq 0}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\bar{t},\,\bar{\Omega}),(\bar{t}^{\prime},\bar{\Omega}^{\prime})\in\Gamma_{n}\times\tbinom{\langle d\rangle}{b_{k}}:\\[1.50694pt]
\tilde{\rho}_{(\bar{t},\,\bar{\Omega}),(\bar{t}^{\prime},\bar{\Omega}^{\prime})}\,=\,\rho_{q}\end{subarray}}\textnormal{Cov}[\mathds{1}_{\bar{t},\bar{\Omega}}(u_{n}),\mathds{1}_{\bar{t}^{\prime},\bar{\Omega}^{\prime}}(u_{n})],

(5.11)

T_{3}(n):=\frac{1}{\lambda_{n,k}(u_{n})}\sum_{q:\rho_{q}<\delta,\;q\leq(2n+1)^{\delta}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\bar{t},\,\bar{\Omega}),(\bar{t}^{\prime},\bar{\Omega}^{\prime})\in\Gamma_{n}\times\tbinom{\langle d\rangle}{b_{k}}:\\[1.50694pt]
\tilde{\rho}_{(\bar{t},\,\bar{\Omega}),(\bar{t}^{\prime},\bar{\Omega}^{\prime})}\,=\,\rho_{q}\end{subarray}}\textnormal{Cov}[\mathds{1}_{\bar{t},\bar{\Omega}}(u_{n}),\mathds{1}_{\bar{t}^{\prime},\bar{\Omega}^{\prime}}(u_{n})],

(5.12)

and

T_{4}(n):=\frac{1}{\lambda_{n,k}(u_{n})}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q:\rho_{q}<\delta,\\[1.50694pt]
(2n+1)^{\delta}<q\leq 2dn+2\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\bar{t},\,\bar{\Omega}),(\bar{t}^{\prime},\bar{\Omega}^{\prime})\in\Gamma_{n}\times\tbinom{\langle d\rangle}{b_{k}}:\\[1.50694pt]
\tilde{\rho}_{(\bar{t},\,\bar{\Omega}),(\bar{t}^{\prime},\bar{\Omega}^{\prime})}\,=\,\rho_{q}\end{subarray}}\textnormal{Cov}[\mathds{1}_{\bar{t},\bar{\Omega}}(u_{n}),\mathds{1}_{\bar{t}^{\prime},\bar{\Omega}^{\prime}}(u_{n})].

(5.13)

We now sequentially bound each of these four terms. Since
\lambda_{n,k}(u_{n})\to\lambda,
a constant, we ignore the
1/\lambda_{n,k}(u_{n})
scaling factor, present in each term, in the computations below.

Using the stationarity property of the field
X,
the fact that
\{(\bar{t}^{\prime},\bar{\Omega}^{\prime}):\tilde{\rho}_{(\bar{t},\,\bar{\Omega}),(\bar{t}^{\prime},\bar{\Omega}^{\prime})}=\rho_{0}\}
is finite for each
(\bar{t},\,\bar{\Omega}),
and Lemma
5.7
.
2
, it follows that

T_{1}(n)=O\left((2n+1)^{d}u_{n}^{-(2b_{k}+1)}\exp[-\varphi_{k}u_{n}^{2}]\right).

(5.14)

Hence, writing
\exp[-\varphi_{k}u_{n}^{2}]
as
[\tau_{k}(2n+1)^{d}u_{n}^{-2b_{k}}]^{-\varphi_{k}/a_{k}}[\lambda_{n,k}(u_{n})]^{\varphi_{k}/a_{k}}
and, again, using the fact that
\lambda_{n,k}(u_{n})\to\lambda,
it follows from (
5.3
) that

T_{1}(n)=O\left(n^{-d\vartheta_{k}/2b_{k}}u_{n}^{-(1-\vartheta_{k})}\right).

Consider the second term. We have

\displaystyle T_{2}(n)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O\Biggl(\sum_{q:\rho_{q}\geq\delta,\;q\neq 0}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\bar{t},\,\bar{\Omega}),(\bar{t}^{\prime},\bar{\Omega}^{\prime})\in\Gamma_{n}\times\tbinom{\langle d\rangle}{b_{k}}:\\[1.50694pt]
\tilde{\rho}_{(\bar{t},\,\bar{\Omega}),(\bar{t}^{\prime},\bar{\Omega}^{\prime})}\,=\,\rho_{q}\end{subarray}}u_{n}^{-4b_{k}}\exp\left[\frac{-2a_{k}u_{n}^{2}}{1+2a_{k}\rho_{q}}\right]\Biggr)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O\Biggl(\sum_{q:\rho_{q}\geq\delta,\;q\neq 0}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\bar{t},\,\bar{\Omega}),(\bar{t}^{\prime},\bar{\Omega}^{\prime})\in\Gamma_{n}\times\tbinom{\langle d\rangle}{b_{k}}:\\[1.50694pt]
\tilde{\rho}_{(\bar{t},\,\bar{\Omega}),(\bar{t}^{\prime},\bar{\Omega}^{\prime})}\,=\,\rho_{q}\end{subarray}}u_{n}^{-4b_{k}}\exp\left[\frac{-2a_{k}u_{n}^{2}}{1+2a_{k}\rho_{1}}\right]\Biggr)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O\left((2n+1)^{d}u_{n}^{-4b_{k}}\exp\left[\frac{-2a_{k}u_{n}^{2}}{1+2a_{k}\rho_{1}}\right]\sum_{q:\rho_{q}\geq\delta,\;q\neq 0}q^{d-1}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O\left((2n+1)^{d}u_{n}^{-4b_{k}}\exp\left[\frac{-2a_{k}u_{n}^{2}}{1+2a_{k}\rho_{1}}\right]\right),

where the first relation follows from Lemma
5.7
.
3
, the second one holds since
\rho_{q}\leq\rho_{1}
which itself holds due to
\Cr{a:r2LAsm},
the truth of the third one is on account of (
5.6
) and the fact that
|\Gamma_{n}\times\tbinom{\langle d\rangle}{b_{k}}|=O((2n+1)^{d}),
while the last one follows because
\{q:\rho_{q}\geq\delta\}
is a finite set which is true due to
\Cr{a:PoiG}.
As we did for
T_{1}(n),
by expressing the exponential term suitably so as to enable use of the fact that
\lambda_{n,k}(u_{n})\to\lambda,
we get

T_{2}(n)=O\left(n^{-d(1-2a_{k}\rho_{1})/(1+2a_{k}\rho_{1})}\;u_{n}^{-8b_{k}a_{k}\rho_{1}/(1+2a_{k}\rho_{1})}\right).

We now handle the third term. As above, using (
5.6
) and Lemma
5.7
.
3
, we have

T_{3}(n)=O\left((2n+1)^{d}u_{n}^{-4b_{k}}\sum_{q:\rho_{q}<\delta,\;q\leq(2n+1)^{\delta}}q^{d-1}\exp\left[\frac{-2a_{k}u_{n}^{2}}{1+2a_{k}\rho_{q}}\right]\right).

Using the fact that the sum only concerns those
q
where
\rho_{q}\leq\delta,
it then follows that

T_{3}(n)=O\left(n^{d}u_{n}^{-4b_{k}}\exp\left[\frac{-2a_{k}u_{n}^{2}}{1+2a_{k}\delta}\right]\sum_{q:q\leq(2n+1)^{\delta}}q^{d-1}\right)=O\left(n^{d(1+\delta)}u_{n}^{-4b_{k}}\exp\left[\frac{-2a_{k}u_{n}^{2}}{1+2a_{k}\delta}\right]\right).

The idea now is to show that our choice of
\delta
in (
5.9
) ensures
T_{2}(n)
and
T_{3}(n)
have similar rates. Towards this, we have

\displaystyle T_{3}(n)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O\left(n^{d[(\delta+1)-2/(1+2a_{k}\delta)]}\;u_{n}^{-8b_{k}a_{k}\delta/(1+2a_{k}\delta)}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O\left(n^{d[(\delta+1)-2/(1+2a_{k}\delta)]}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O\left(n^{-d}n^{d\delta}n^{2d[1-1/(1+2a_{k}\delta)]}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O\left(n^{-d}n^{d\delta}n^{4a_{k}\delta d/(1+2a_{k}\delta)]}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O\left(n^{-d}n^{d(1+4a_{k})\delta}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O\left(n^{-d}n^{2a_{k}\rho_{1}d/(1+2a_{k}\rho_{1})}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O\left(n^{-d}n^{2a_{k}\rho_{1}d/(1+2a_{k}\rho_{1})}\left[n^{d}u_{n}^{-4b_{k}}\right]^{2a_{k}\rho_{1}/(1+2a_{k}\rho_{1})}\right),

where the first relation is obtained by expressing the exponential term suitably as before, the second relation follows by dropping the
u_{n}
expression, which itself can be done because
\delta>0;
the third relation follows by multiplying and dividing by
n^{d};
the fifth relation is obtained by ignoring the
1+2a_{k}\delta
term, which can be done since
1+a_{k}\delta>1;
the sixth relation is due to (
5.9
); while we get the last relation by artificially introducing
\left[n^{d}u_{n}^{-4b_{k}}\right]^{2a_{k}\rho_{1}/(1+2a_{k}\rho_{1})},
which can done since it grows to
\infty
on account of the exponent being positive and
\lim_{n\to\infty}a_{k}u_{n}^{2}/(d\log n)
being
1.
From this last relation, it is easy to see that

T_{3}(n)=O\left(n^{-d(1-2a_{k}\rho_{1})/(1+2a_{k}\rho_{1})}\;u_{n}^{-8b_{k}a_{k}\rho_{1}/(1+2a_{k}\rho_{1})}\right),

as desired.

We finally deal with the fourth term. As before, using (
5.6
) and Lemma
5.7
.
4
,

\displaystyle T_{4}(n)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O\left((2n+1)^{d}\sum_{q:\rho_{q}<\delta,\;(2n+1)^{\delta}<q\leq 2dn+2}q^{d-1}\rho_{q}\,u_{n}^{-(4b_{k}-2)}\,\exp\left[\frac{-2a_{k}u_{n}^{2}}{1+2a_{k}\rho_{q}}\right]\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O\Bigg(n^{2d-1}\sum_{q:\rho_{q}<\delta,\;(2n+1)^{\delta}<q\leq 2dn+2}\rho_{q}\,u_{n}^{-(4b_{k}-2)}\,\exp\left[\frac{-2a_{k}u_{n}^{2}}{1+2a_{k}\rho_{q}}\right]\Bigg).

Due to
\Cr{a:PoiG},
there exists a positive constant so that
\rho_{q}\log q\leq\Cr{c:CovDecay}
for each
q\geq 1.
Hence, for any
q>(2n+1)^{\delta},
we have

\rho_{q}\log(2n+1)\leq\frac{\Cr{c:CovDecay}}{\delta}.

(5.15)

Now, observe that

\displaystyle u_{n}^{-(4b_{k}-2)}\,\exp\left[\frac{-2a_{k}u_{n}^{2}}{1+2a_{k}\rho_{q}}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O\left((2n+1)^{-2d/(1+2a_{k}\rho_{q})}\;u_{n}^{2}\;u_{n}^{-8b_{k}a_{k}\rho_{q}/(1+2a_{k}\rho_{q})}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O\left((2n+1)^{d[4a_{k}\rho_{q}/(1+2a_{k}\rho_{q})-2]}\;u_{n}^{2}\;u_{n}^{-8b_{k}a_{k}\rho_{q}/(1+2a_{k}\rho_{q})}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O\left((2n+1)^{d[4a_{k}\rho_{q}/(1+2a_{k}\rho_{q})-2]}\;u_{n}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O\left((2n+1)^{-2d}[(2n+1)^{\rho_{q}}]^{4a_{k}d}\;u_{n}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O\left(n^{-2d}\;u_{n}^{2}\right),

where the first relation follows by suitably modifying the exponential term as before; the third relation follows by dropping the last
u_{n}
expression as the exponent is negative; the fourth relation follows by dropping the
1+2a_{k}\rho_{q}
term; while the last relation follows from (
5.15
) which shows that
(2n+1)^{\rho_{q}}
is a constant with respect to
q
and
n.
Consequently, we have

T_{4}(n)=O\left(\frac{u_{n}^{2}}{n}\sum_{q=1}^{2dn}\rho_{q}\right).

The desired result now follows from the bounds on
T_{1}(n),\ldots,T_{4}(n)
obtained above, and the following reasons: first,
a_{k}u_{n}^{2}/[d\log n]\to 1;
second,

\frac{\vartheta_{k}}{2b_{k}}=\frac{[1-2a_{k}\rho_{1}]^{2}}{2a_{k}[1-2a_{k}\rho_{1}^{2}]}<\frac{1-2a_{k}\rho_{1}}{1+2a_{k}\rho_{1}};

the latter holds because
\rho_{1}\in(0,1),

\rho_{2}\in[0,1)
and
1-2a_{k}\rho_{1}>0,
which itself are true on account of
\Cr{a:r0Asm},\Cr{a:r1Asm},\Cr{a:ComC}(k)
and
\Cr{a:r2LAsm}.

This completes the proof.
∎

We now proceed towards proving Lemma
3.9
. As the first step, we establish Lemma
5.8
which, at a loose level, provides covariance bounds between indicators in (
3.7
) and (
3.8
). Before stating it, we introduce few notations.

From Remark
5.4
, recall that the vertex set of each
G\equiv(V,E)\in\mathcal{N}_{k},

1\leq k<d-1,
has a uniquely associated partition (
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
) so that the properties mentioned in Lemma
4.14
hold. Keeping this in mind, let

N_{n,k,j}(u):=\begin{cases}0,&\text{ if $k=0,d-1,$ }\\
\sum_{(\bar{t},\,G)\in\Gamma_{n}\times\mathcal{N}_{k}:|V_{1}|=j}\mathds{1}[\bar{X}_{\bar{t},G}\geq u\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{2b_{k}}]&\text{ if $1\leq k<d-1,$}\end{cases}

(5.16)

where
1\leq j\leq b_{k}
and
u\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}.
It is easy to see that

N_{n,k}(u_{n})=\sum_{j=1}^{b_{k}}N_{n,k,j}(u_{n}).

(5.17)

For
1\leq k<d-1;

\bar{t},\bar{t}^{\prime}\in\Gamma_{n};
and
G\equiv(V,E),G^{\prime}\equiv(V^{\prime},E^{\prime})\in\mathcal{N}_{k};
let

\tilde{\rho}^{N}_{(\bar{t},\,G),(\bar{t}^{\prime},\,G^{\prime})}:=\max\left\{\rho_{\|[\bar{t}+\bar{v}]-[\bar{t}^{\prime}+\bar{v}^{\prime}]\|_{1}}:\bar{v}\in V,\bar{v}^{\prime}\in V^{\prime}\right\};

(5.18)

this mimics the definition in (
5.4
). In the same spirit, for
0\leq k<d;\bar{t},\bar{t}^{\prime}\in\Gamma_{n};
and
G,G^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{k};
define
\tilde{\rho}^{L}_{(\bar{t},\,G),(\bar{t}^{\prime},\,G^{\prime})}.
Recall that
|\mathcal{G}_{k}|,
which is used in (
3.6
), (
3.1
), (
3.2
), and (
3.3
), is finite for each
k\geq 0.
Thus, by repeating the arguments that were used to derive (
5.6
), it is not difficult to see that, for each
(\bar{t},\,G)\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\times\mathcal{N}_{k}
and
q\geq 1,

|\{(\bar{t}^{\prime},\,G^{\prime})\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\times\mathcal{N}_{k}:\tilde{\rho}^{N}_{(\bar{t},\,G),(\bar{t}^{\prime},\,G^{\prime})}=\rho_{q}\}|\leq{}_{,k}\;q^{d-1},

(5.19)

where
{\Cr{c:upBdRhoN}}_{,k}
is a positive constant (depending on
k
). Similarly, for each
(\bar{t},\,G)\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}_{k}
and
q\geq 1,

|\{(\bar{t}^{\prime},\,G^{\prime})\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}_{k}:\tilde{\rho}^{L}_{(\bar{t},\,G),(\bar{t}^{\prime},\,G^{\prime})}=\rho_{q}\}|\leq{}_{,k}\;q^{d-1},

(5.20)

where
{\Cr{c:upBdRhoL}}_{,k}
is another positive constant. Separately, let

\mathds{1}^{{}^{N}}_{\bar{t},k}(u)\equiv\mathds{1}[\bar{X}_{\bar{t},G^{*}}\geq u\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{2b_{k}}],\quad\text{ if $1\leq k<d-1,$}

(5.21)

where
G^{*}\equiv(V,E)\in\mathcal{N}_{k}
is some arbitrary but fixed graph such that, for a partition
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
of
V
as in Lemma
4.14
, we have
|V_{1}|=1,
and
\bar{X}_{\bar{t},G^{*}}
is as defined above (
3.7
). Also, let

\mathds{1}^{{}^{L}}_{\bar{t},k}(u)\equiv\begin{cases}\mathds{1}[(X_{\bar{t}},X_{\bar{t}+\bar{e}_{1}})\geq u\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{2b_{0}+1}],&\text{ if $k=0,$ }\\[4.30554pt]
\mathds{1}[(\bar{X}_{\bar{t},\langle k+1\rangle},X_{\bar{t}})\geq u\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{2b_{k}+1}],&\text{ if $1\leq k<d,$ }\end{cases}

(5.22)

where
\bar{X}_{\bar{t},\langle b_{k}\rangle}
is as defined in (
3.5
).

Lemma 5.8
.

The following statements are true.

1.

Suppose
\Cr{a:r0Asm},\Cr{a:r2Asm},
and
\Cr{a:rqAsm}
hold. Fix
k
so that
1\leq k<d-1
and let
\{u_{n}\}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{\infty}
be such that
\lim_{n\to\infty}u_{n}=\infty.
Then, there is a constant
\delta^{N}>0
and a family Gaussian random vectors
\{\bar{Z}_{\mu}\in\mathbb{R}^{4b_{k}}:\mu\in[0,\delta^{N}]\}
such that

0\leq\mathbb{P}\{\bar{Z}_{\mu}\geq u_{n}\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{4b_{k}}\}-\left[\mathbb{E}[\mathds{1}^{{}^{N}}_{\bar{0},k}(u_{n})]\right]^{2}=O\left(\mu\;u_{n}^{-(4b_{k}-2)}\exp\left[\frac{-2\varrho_{k}u_{n}^{2}}{1+2\mu\varrho_{k}}\right]\right),

(5.23)

where the hidden constants are independent of
\mu,
while
\varrho_{k}
is as in (
3.11
). Moreover, if
(\bar{t},\,G),(\bar{t}^{\prime},\,G^{\prime})\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\times\mathcal{N}_{k}
is such that
\tilde{\rho}^{N}_{(\bar{t},\,G),(\bar{t}^{\prime},\,G^{\prime})}=\mu\leq\delta^{N}
and
|V_{1}|=|V^{\prime}_{1}|,
then for all sufficiently large
u_{n}

\mathbb{P}\{(\bar{X}_{\bar{t},G},\bar{X}_{\bar{t}^{\prime},G^{\prime}})\geq u_{n}\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{4b_{k}}\}\leq\mathbb{P}\{\bar{Z}_{\mu}\geq u_{n}\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{4b_{k}}\}.

(5.24)

Above,
V_{1}
(resp.
V^{\prime}_{1}
) is the first subset in the uniquely associated partition of the vertex set of
G
(resp.
G^{\prime}
); see Remark
5.4
and Lemma
4.14
.

2.

Suppose
\Cr{a:r0Asm},\Cr{a:r1Asm},\Cr{a:ComC}(k),
and
\Cr{a:r2LAsm}
hold. Fix
k
so that
0\leq k<d
and let
\{u_{n}\}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{\infty}
be such that
\lim_{n\to\infty}u_{n}=\infty.
Then, there is a constant
\delta^{L}>0
and a family of Gaussian random vectors
\{\bar{Z}_{\mu}:\mu\in[0,\delta^{L}]\}
such that

0\leq\mathbb{P}\{\bar{Z}_{\mu}\geq u_{n}\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{4b_{k}+2}\}-\left[\mathbb{E}[\mathds{1}^{{}^{L}}_{\bar{0},k}(u_{n})]\right]^{2}=O\left(\mu\;u_{n}^{-4b_{k}}\exp\left[\frac{-2\varphi_{k}u_{n}^{2}}{1+2\mu\varphi_{k}}\right]\right),

(5.25)

where the hidden constants are independent of
\mu,
while
\varphi_{k}
is as in (
3.12
). Moreover, if
(\bar{t},\,G),(\bar{t}^{\prime},\,G^{\prime})\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}_{k}
is such that
\tilde{\rho}^{L}_{(\bar{t},\,G),(\bar{t}^{\prime},\,G^{\prime})}=\mu\leq\delta^{L},
then

\mathbb{P}\{(\bar{X}_{\bar{t},G},\bar{X}_{\bar{t}^{\prime},G^{\prime}})\geq u_{n}\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{|V|+|V^{\prime}|}\}\leq\mathbb{P}\{\bar{Z}_{\mu}\geq u_{n}\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{4b_{k}+2}\}.

(5.26)

Above,
V
and
V^{\prime}
are the vertex sets of
G
and
G^{\prime},
respectively.

The proof for this result is given in Section
6
, p.
6
.

Proof of Lemma
3.9
.

We discuss each statement individually.

•

Arguments for Statement
1
: We first consider the case
1\leq k<d.
We claim that

\left|\frac{\textnormal{Var}[S_{n,k}(u_{n})]}{\lambda_{n,k}(u_{n})}-\frac{\mathbb{E}[S_{n,k}(u_{n})]}{\lambda_{n,k}(u_{n})}\right|=O\left(u_{n}^{-1}\exp[-[\varphi_{k}-a_{k}]u_{n}^{2}]\right).

(5.27)

Using the triangle inequality, Lemma
3.7
.
1
, and the fact that
\varphi_{k}>a_{k}
(see Lemma
3.7
.
3
),
it is then easy to see that the desired result holds.

It remains to establish (
5.27
). From (
3.4
), we have

\mathbb{E}[S_{n,k}(u_{n})]=\sum_{(\bar{t},\,\bar{\Omega})\in\Gamma_{n}\times\tbinom{\langle d\rangle}{b_{k}}}\mathbb{E}[\mathds{1}_{\bar{t},\bar{\Omega}}(u_{n})]

and

\textnormal{Var}[S_{n,k}(u_{n})]=\sum_{(\bar{t},\,\bar{\Omega})\in\Gamma_{n}\times\tbinom{\langle d\rangle}{b_{k}}}\textnormal{Var}[\mathds{1}_{\bar{t},\bar{\Omega}}(u_{n})]+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\bar{t},\,\bar{\Omega}),(\bar{t}^{\prime},\bar{\Omega}^{\prime})\in\Gamma_{n}\times\tbinom{\langle d\rangle}{b_{k}}\\
(\bar{t},\,\bar{\Omega})\neq(\bar{t}^{\prime},\bar{\Omega}^{\prime})\end{subarray}}\textnormal{Cov}[\mathds{1}_{\bar{t},\bar{\Omega}}(u_{n}),\mathds{1}_{\bar{t}^{\prime},\bar{\Omega}^{\prime}}(u_{n})].

Hence, using Lemma
5.7
.
1
and the fact that
\textnormal{Var}[\mathds{1}_{\bar{t},\bar{\Omega}}(u_{n})]=\mathbb{E}[\mathds{1}_{\bar{t},\bar{\Omega}}(u_{n})]-[\mathbb{E}[\mathds{1}_{\bar{t},\bar{\Omega}}(u_{n})]]^{2},
we get

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\textnormal{Var}[S_{n,k}(u_{n})]}{\lambda_{n,k}(u_{n})}-\frac{\mathbb{E}[S_{n,k}(u_{n})]}{\lambda_{n,k}(u_{n})}\right|

(5.28)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{\sum_{(\bar{t},\,\bar{\Omega})\in\Gamma_{n}\times\tbinom{\langle d\rangle}{b_{k}}}[\mathbb{E}[\mathds{1}_{\bar{t},\bar{\Omega}}(u_{n})]]^{2}}{\lambda_{n,k}(u_{n})}+\frac{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\bar{t},\,\bar{\Omega}),(\bar{t}^{\prime},\bar{\Omega}^{\prime})\in\Gamma_{n}\times\tbinom{\langle d\rangle}{b_{k}}\\
(\bar{t},\,\bar{\Omega})\neq(\bar{t}^{\prime},\bar{\Omega}^{\prime})\end{subarray}}\textnormal{Cov}[\mathds{1}_{\bar{t},\bar{\Omega}}(u_{n}),\mathds{1}_{\bar{t}^{\prime},\bar{\Omega}^{\prime}}(u_{n})]}{\lambda_{n,k}(u_{n})}.

Observe that
\Cr{a:r0Asm}
and
\Cr{a:r2Asm}
hold; the latter being a consequence of
\Cr{a:r2LAsm}
and
\Cr{a:r1Asm}.
Arguing as we did to obtain (
5.8
) and then substituting
\eqref{Defn:Lambdanku},
it then follows that

\displaystyle\frac{\sum_{(\bar{t},\,\bar{\Omega})\in\Gamma_{n}\times\tbinom{\langle d\rangle}{b_{k}}}[\mathbb{E}[\mathds{1}_{\bar{t},\bar{\Omega}}(u_{n})]]^{2}}{\lambda_{n,k}(u_{n})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O\left(u_{n}^{-2b_{k}}\exp[-a_{k}u_{n}^{2}]\left(1+\frac{1}{u_{n}^{2}}+\frac{1}{u_{n}^{4}}\right)\right)

(5.29)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O\left(u_{n}^{-2b_{k}}\exp[-a_{k}u_{n}^{2}]\right).

With
\tilde{\rho}_{(\bar{t},\,\bar{\Omega}),(\bar{t}^{\prime},\bar{\Omega}^{\prime})}
as in (
5.4
), now write the covariance sum in (
5.28
) as
T_{1}(n)+T_{2}(n),
where
T_{1}(n)
is exactly as in (
5.10
), while

T_{2}(n):=\frac{1}{\lambda_{n,k}(u_{n})}\;\sum_{q=1}^{2dn+2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\bar{t},\,\bar{\Omega}),(\bar{t}^{\prime},\bar{\Omega}^{\prime})\in\Gamma_{n}\times\tbinom{\langle d\rangle}{b_{k}}:\\[1.50694pt]
\tilde{\rho}_{(\bar{t},\,\bar{\Omega}),(\bar{t}^{\prime},\bar{\Omega}^{\prime})}\,=\,\rho_{q}\end{subarray}}\textnormal{Cov}[\mathds{1}_{\bar{t},\bar{\Omega}}(u_{n}),\mathds{1}_{\bar{t}^{\prime},\bar{\Omega}^{\prime}}(u_{n})].

Note that
\Cr{a:r0Asm},\Cr{a:r1Asm},\Cr{a:r2LAsm},
and
\Cr{a:ComC}(k)
hold. Therefore, making the same arguments as those that were used to derive (
5.14
), and, additionally, using the fact that
\lambda_{n,k}(u_{n})
cannot be ignored unlike there, it follows from (
1.4
) that

T_{1}(n)=O\left(u_{n}^{-1}\exp[-[\varphi_{k}-a_{k}]u_{n}^{2}]\right).

(5.30)

On the other hand, we have

\displaystyle T_{2}(n)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\lambda_{n,k}(u_{n})}\;O\left((2n+1)^{d}\sum_{q=1}^{2dn+2}\rho_{q}q^{d-1}u_{n}^{-(4b_{k}-2)}\exp\left[\frac{-2a_{k}u_{n}^{2}}{1+2a_{k}\rho_{q}}\right]\right)

(5.31)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\lambda_{n,k}(u_{n})}O\left((2n+1)^{d}u_{n}^{-(4b_{k}-2)}\exp\left[\frac{-2a_{k}u_{n}^{2}}{1+2a_{k}\rho_{1}}\right]\sum_{q=1}^{2dn+2}\rho_{q}q^{d-1}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\lambda_{n,k}(u_{n})}O\left((2n+1)^{d}u_{n}^{-(4b_{k}-2)}\exp\left[\frac{-2a_{k}u_{n}^{2}}{1+2a_{k}\rho_{1}}\right]\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O\left(u_{n}^{-2(b_{k}-1)}\exp\left[\frac{-a_{k}(1-2a_{k}\rho_{1})u_{n}^{2}}{1+2a_{k}\rho_{1}}\right]\right),

where the first relation holds due to (
5.6
), Lemma
5.7
.
4
, and the stationarity property of the field
X;
the second one is obtained by using the fact that
\rho_{q}\leq\rho_{1}
which itself holds on account of
\Cr{a:r2LAsm},
the third is true to due to
\Cr{a:CLTG},
while the last one is got by substituting (
1.4
).

On account of
\Cr{a:r0Asm},\Cr{a:r1Asm},\Cr{a:ComC}(k),
and
\Cr{a:r2LAsm},
note that
\rho_{1}\in(0,1),

\rho_{2}\in[0,1)
and
1-2a_{k}\rho_{1}>0.
This shows that the coefficient before
u_{n}^{2}
in the exponential terms in (
5.29
) and (
5.31
) is negative; while the same holds in (
5.30
) due to
\varphi_{k}>a_{k}
(see Lemma
3.7
.
3
). Therefore, the terms in (
5.29
), (
5.30
), and (
5.31
), all decay to zero with
n;
it remains the identify the one that decays the slowest. Also, from the conditions on
\rho_{1},\rho_{2}
discussed above,
(1-2a_{k}\rho_{1})/(1+2a_{k}\rho_{1})<1,
which shows that the coefficient in the exponential term in (
5.31
) is larger than that in (
5.29
). On the other hand, additionally using (
5.3
), one can easily see that (
5.30
) decays slower than (
5.31
). Thus, our claim in (
5.27
) is true which gives the desired result.

The case
k=0
follows similarly by using Lemma
5.6
.

•

Arguments for Statement
2
: The result is trivially true for
k=0
and
k=d-1
cases. So, suppose that
1\leq k<d-1.

Since
\Cr{a:r0Asm},\Cr{a:r2Asm},
and
\Cr{a:rqAsm}
hold, we have
0\leq\rho_{3}<\rho_{2}<1.
Further, from Lemma
3.7
.
2
, we have
\varrho_{k}>a_{k},
where
\varrho_{k}
is as in (
3.11
). Combining the two, it is then easy to see that
\varrho_{k}\in(0,\infty).

Let
\delta^{N}>0
be as in Lemma
5.8
.
1
and let
\delta\in(0,1)
be such that
\delta\leq\min\{\delta^{N},\frac{1}{2\varrho_{k}}\}.
From (
5.17
) and since
k
is finite, we get

\mathbb{E}[N_{n,k}^{2}(u_{n})]=O\left(\sum_{j=1}^{b_{k}}\mathbb{E}[N^{2}_{n,k,j}(u_{n})]\right).

Now, fix an arbitrary
j
so that
1\leq j\leq b_{k}.
Then,
\mathbb{E}[N_{n,k,j}^{2}(u_{n})]/\lambda_{n,k}^{2}(u_{n})
can be written as
T_{1}(n)+T_{2}(n)+T_{3}(n),
where

T_{1}(n)=\frac{1}{\lambda_{n,k}^{2}(u_{n})}\sum_{(\bar{t},\,G)\in\Gamma_{n}\times\mathcal{N}_{k}:|V_{1}|=j}\mathbb{P}\{\bar{X}_{\bar{t},G}\geq u_{n}\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{2b_{k}}\},

T_{2}(n)=\frac{1}{\lambda_{n,k}^{2}(u_{n})}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q:\rho_{q}>\delta,\\[1.50694pt]
q\leq Q_{n}\end{subarray}}\;\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\bar{t},\,G),(\bar{t}^{\prime},\,G^{\prime})\in\Gamma_{n}\times\mathcal{N}_{k}:|V_{1}|=|V^{\prime}_{1}|=j\\[1.50694pt]
(\bar{t},\,G)\neq(\bar{t}^{\prime},\,G^{\prime}),\;\tilde{\rho}^{N}_{(\bar{t},\,G),(\bar{t}^{\prime},\,G^{\prime})}=\rho_{q}\end{subarray}}\mathbb{P}\{(\bar{X}_{\bar{t},G},\bar{X}_{\bar{t}^{\prime},G^{\prime}})\geq u_{n}\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{4b_{k}}\},

and

T_{3}(n):=\frac{1}{\lambda_{n,k}^{2}(u_{n})}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q:\rho_{q}\leq\delta,\\[1.50694pt]
q\leq Q_{n}\end{subarray}}\;\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\bar{t},\,G),(\bar{t}^{\prime},\,G^{\prime})\in\Gamma_{n}\times\mathcal{N}_{k}:|V_{1}|=|V^{\prime}_{1}|=j\\[1.50694pt]
(\bar{t},\,G)\neq(\bar{t}^{\prime},\,G^{\prime}),\;\tilde{\rho}^{N}_{(\bar{t},\,G),(\bar{t}^{\prime},\,G^{\prime})}=\rho_{q}\end{subarray}}\mathbb{P}\{(\bar{X}_{\bar{t},G},\bar{X}_{\bar{t}^{\prime},G^{\prime}})\geq u_{n}\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{4b_{k}}\}.

Here,
Q_{n}=O(2n+1);
this bound for
q
follows from (
5.18
), the fact that
\|\bar{t}-\bar{t}^{\prime}\|\leq 2dn
for any
(\bar{t},\,G),(\bar{t}^{\prime},\,G^{\prime})\in\Gamma_{n}\times\mathcal{N}_{k},
and since each
G\in\mathcal{N}_{k}
is a subset of a bounded graph
\mathcal{G}_{0}
(defined above (
3.1
)). This bound is in similar spirit to
2dn+2
that was used in the proof of Theorem
3.8
(see the discussion above (
5.10
)). In the remainder of this proof, we bound each of the above three terms.

Consider
T_{1}(n).
We have

\displaystyle T_{1}(n)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\lambda_{n,k}^{2}(u_{n})}(2n+1)^{d}|\mathcal{N}_{k}|u_{n}^{-2b_{k}}\exp[-\varrho_{k}u_{n}^{2}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O\left(\frac{\exp[-(\varrho_{k}-a_{k})u_{n}^{2}]}{\lambda_{n,k}(u_{n})}\right),

where the first relation follows from Lemma
5.5
.
3
, and by making use of the facts that the field
X
is stationary and
|\{G\in\mathcal{N}_{k}:|V_{1}|=j\}|\leq|\mathcal{N}_{k}|;
while the next one follows from (
1.4
).

With regards to
T_{2}(n),
note that

\displaystyle T_{2}(n)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\lambda_{n,k}^{2}(u_{n})}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q:\rho_{q}>\delta,\\[1.50694pt]
q\leq Q_{n}\end{subarray}}\;\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\bar{t},\,G),(\bar{t}^{\prime},\,G^{\prime})\in\Gamma_{n}\times\mathcal{N}_{k}:|V_{1}|=|V^{\prime}_{1}|=j\\[1.50694pt]
(\bar{t},\,G)\neq(\bar{t}^{\prime},\,G^{\prime}),\,\tilde{\rho}^{N}_{(\bar{t},\,G),(\bar{t}^{\prime},\,G^{\prime})}=\rho_{q}\end{subarray}}\mathbb{P}\{\bar{X}_{\bar{t},G}\geq u_{n}\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{2b_{k}}\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O\left(\frac{1}{\lambda_{n,k}^{2}(u_{n})}\left[\sum_{q:\rho_{q}>\delta}q^{d-1}\right]\sum_{(\bar{t},\,G)\in\Gamma_{n}\times\mathcal{N}_{k}:|V_{1}|=j}\mathbb{P}\{\bar{X}_{\bar{t},G}\geq u_{n}\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{2b_{k}}\}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O\left(\frac{1}{\lambda_{n,k}^{2}(u_{n})}\sum_{(\bar{t},\,G)\in\Gamma_{n}\times\mathcal{N}_{k}:|V_{1}|=j}\mathbb{P}\{\bar{X}_{\bar{t},G}\geq u_{n}\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{2b_{k}}\}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O\left(\frac{\exp[-(\varrho_{k}-a_{k})u_{n}^{2}]}{\lambda_{n,k}(u_{n})}\right),

where the first relation follows by dropping variables, the second one follows on account of (
5.19
), the third one holds due to fact that
|\{q:\rho_{q}>\delta\}|
is finite which itself is true since
\lim_{q\to\infty}\rho_{q}=0
on account of
\Cr{a:CLTG},
while the truth of the last one can be seen using arguments similar to those used to bound
T_{1}(n)
above.

Moving onto
T_{3}(n),
observe that

\displaystyle T_{3}(n)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O\Bigg(\frac{1}{\lambda_{n,k}^{2}(u_{n})}\sum_{q:\rho_{q}\leq\delta,\;q\leq Q_{n}}\;\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\bar{t},\,G),(\bar{t}^{\prime},\,G^{\prime})\in\Gamma_{n}\times\mathcal{N}_{k}:|V_{1}|=|V^{\prime}_{1}|=j\\[1.50694pt]
(\bar{t},\,G)\neq(\bar{t}^{\prime},\,G^{\prime}),\;\tilde{\rho}^{N}_{(\bar{t},\,G),(\bar{t}^{\prime},\,G^{\prime})}=\rho_{q}\end{subarray}}\mathbb{P}\{\bar{Z}_{\rho_{q}}\geq u_{n}\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{4b_{k}}\}\Bigg)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O\left(\frac{(2n+1)^{d}}{\lambda_{n,k}^{2}(u_{n})}\sum_{q:\rho_{q}\leq\delta,\;q\leq Q_{n}}q^{d-1}\mathbb{P}\{\bar{Z}_{\rho_{q}}\geq u_{n}\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{4b_{k}}\}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O\left(\frac{(2n+1)^{d}}{\lambda_{n,k}^{2}(u_{n})}\sum_{q:\rho_{q}\leq\delta,\;q\leq Q_{n}}q^{d-1}\left[\mathbb{P}\{\bar{Z}_{\rho_{q}}\geq u_{n}\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{4b_{k}}\}-\left[\mathbb{E}[\mathds{1}^{{}^{N}}_{\bar{0},k}(u_{n})]\right]^{2}\right]\right)

\displaystyle+\;O\left(\frac{(2n+1)^{d}}{\lambda_{n,k}^{2}(u_{n})}\sum_{q:\rho_{q}\leq\delta,\;q\leq Q_{n}}q^{d-1}\left[\mathbb{E}[\mathds{1}^{{}^{N}}_{\bar{0},k}(u_{n})]\right]^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O\left(\frac{(2n+1)^{d}}{\lambda_{n,k}^{2}(u_{n})}u_{n}^{-(4b_{k}-2)}\sum_{q:\rho_{q}\leq\delta,\;q\leq Q_{n}}\rho_{q}q^{d-1}\exp\left[\frac{-2\varrho_{k}u_{n}^{2}}{1+2\rho_{q}\varrho_{k}}\right]\right)

\displaystyle+\;O\left(\frac{(2n+1)^{d}}{\lambda_{n,k}^{2}(u_{n})}\left[\mathbb{E}[\mathds{1}^{{}^{N}}_{\bar{0},k}(u_{n})]\right]^{2}\sum_{q:\rho_{q}\leq\delta,\;q\leq Q_{n}}q^{d-1}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O\left(\frac{(2n+1)^{d}}{\lambda_{n,k}^{2}(u_{n})}u_{n}^{-(4b_{k}-2)}\exp\left[\frac{-2\varrho_{k}u_{n}^{2}}{1+2\delta\varrho_{k}}\right]\right)

\displaystyle+\;O\left(\frac{(2n+1)^{d}}{\lambda_{n,k}^{2}(u_{n})}\left[\mathbb{E}[\mathds{1}^{{}^{N}}_{\bar{0},k}(u_{n})]\right]^{2}\sum_{q:\rho_{q}\leq\delta,q\leq Q_{n}}q^{d-1}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O\left(\frac{(2n+1)^{d}}{\lambda_{n,k}^{2}(u_{n})}u_{n}^{-(4b_{k}-2)}\exp\left[-\varrho_{k}u_{n}^{2}\right]\right)+O\left(\frac{(2n+1)^{2d}}{\lambda_{n,k}^{2}(u_{n})}\left[\mathbb{E}[\mathds{1}^{{}^{N}}_{\bar{0},k}(u_{n})]\right]^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O\left(u_{n}^{-2(b_{k}-1)}\dfrac{\exp[-(\varrho_{k}-a_{k})u_{n}^{2}]}{\lambda_{n,k}(u_{n})}\right)+O\left(\exp[-2(\varrho_{k}-a_{k})u_{n}^{2}]\right),

where the first relation follows from (
5.24
) and the fact that
\delta\leq\delta^{N};
the second one holds due to (
5.19
), and since
|\Gamma_{n}|=(2n+1)^{d}
and
|\mathcal{N}_{k}|
is finite; the third one follows by adding and subtracting
\left[\mathbb{E}[\mathds{1}^{{}^{N}}_{\bar{0},k}(u_{n})]\right]^{2};
the fourth one is true due to (
5.23
), the fifth one holds due to
\Cr{a:CLTG}
and since
\rho_{q}\leq\delta;
the sixth one follows because
2\delta\varrho_{k}\leq 1
and
\sum_{q\leq Q_{n}}q^{d-1}=O((2n+1)^{d});
while the last one is got by using Lemma
5.5
.
3
and then substituting (
1.4
).

Since
j
was arbitrary and
b_{k}\geq 1
for
1\leq k<d-1,
the desired result is now easy to see.
