case here as well.

This completes the proof.
∎

7
Discussion

Here we expand on some on the remarks given in Section
1
and also provide several directions for future research.

We begin by elaborating on Remark

R
1

. Recall that the indicator associated with the random vector given in (
3.5
) approximates the presence of minimal subcomplexes with non-trivial Betti numbers. The
\|\cdot\|_{1}-
isotropy assumption significantly simplified the covariance matrix associated with this vector. On the other hand, if we had assumed
\|\cdot\|_{2}-
isotropy, then instead of
W_{2b_{k}}(\rho_{2})
we would have ended with a matrix whose off-diagonal entries were made up of the two distinct entries
\rho_{2}
and
\rho_{\sqrt{2}}.
This would have made our subsequent computations slightly more involved.

Next, to understand the restriction mentioned in Remark

R
3

, let us consider a Gaussian field on the continuum with a smooth covariance function, say
\textnormal{Cov}[X_{\bar{t}},X_{\bar{t}^{\prime}}]=e^{-\|\bar{t}-\bar{t}^{\prime}\|_{2}}.
Then, note that fine sampling would result in
\rho_{1}
and
\rho_{2}
being close to each other and also being close to
1,
thereby reversing the inequality in
\Cr{a:ComC}(k).
In this sense, this latter covariance condition puts a lower bound on the sampling distances for our results to hold.

Moving on, recall that a key ingredient across our proofs are the covariance bounds given in Lemma
5.7
. These extend the bounds given in
[
18
, Lemma 3.4 (i), (ii)]
to the multivariate case. A multivariate bound similar to the one in Lemma
5.7
.
4
was also obtained in
[
7
, Lemma 1]
and plays an important role across
[
14
,
26
,
27
]
. While that bound is for more general functions of Gaussian vectors, it is weaker for the specific indicator function that we work with.

Under the independence assumptions mentioned in Remark

R
11

, note that the tighter covariance bound obtained in Lemma
5.7
.
4
would have been no longer necessary, thereby drastically simplifying our calculations. In fact, one could have then used a weaker Stein-Chen bound, as in
[
24
]
for example, to establish the distributional convergences. Another notable difference would have been that the events
\{\bar{X}_{\bar{t},\bar{\Omega}}\geq u\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{2b_{k}}\}
and
\{\bar{X}_{\bar{t},G}\geq u\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{2b_{k}}\}
for
G\in\mathcal{N}_{k}
would then have become equiprobable. Recall that these events are associated with
\|\cdot\|_{1}-
isometric and non-isometric minimal subcomplexes having non-trivial Betti numbers. Due to this, the leading constant in
\lambda_{n,k}(u_{n})
would have changed.

We now point to several interesting questions that arise from the present work. Since Assumption
\Cr{a:ComC}(k)
was central to all of our theorems, one of the first questions to ask is: ‘What happens when this condition is not satisfied?’. When
\Cr{a:ComC}(k)
holds, then it is easy to see from Lemmas
3.5
.
1
and
3.7
that
\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}[\check{S}_{n,k}(u_{n})]/\mathbb{E}[S_{n,k}(u_{n})]=1.
On the other hand, if this condition is not satisfied, the first consequence is that, whenever
u_{n}\to\infty,

\frac{\mathbb{E}[S^{\prime}_{n,k}(u_{n})]}{\mathbb{E}[S_{n,k}(u_{n})]}\to 0,

(7.1)

where

S^{\prime}_{n,k}(u_{n}):=\sum_{(\bar{t},\,\bar{\Omega})\in\Gamma_{n}\times\tbinom{\langle d\rangle}{b_{k}}}\mathds{1}[\bar{X}_{\bar{t},\bar{\Omega}}\geq u_{n}2b_{k}]\mathds{1}[X_{\bar{t}}<u_{n}].

This can be shown using the bounds for
\mathbb{P}\{\bar{X}_{\bar{t},\bar{\Omega}}\geq u_{n}\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{2b_{k}},X_{\bar{t}}\leq u_{n}\}
given in
[
13
, Theorem 2.2]
. Since
\Cr{a:ComC}(k)
does not hold anymore, note that the above result cannot be shown directly using Lemma
2.4
. This is because one cannot obtain bounds for
\mathbb{P}\{(\bar{X}_{\bar{t},\bar{\Omega}},X_{\bar{t}})\geq u_{n}\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{2b_{k}+1}\}
as the Savage condition associated with this expression no longer holds. Using (
7.1
), it is now easy to see that

\frac{\mathbb{E}[\check{S}_{n,k}(u_{n})]}{\mathbb{E}[S_{n,k}(u_{n})]}\leq\frac{\mathbb{E}[S^{\prime}_{n,k}(u_{n})]}{\mathbb{E}[S_{n,k}(u_{n})]}\to 0.

This implies that
S_{n,k}(u_{n})
can no longer be used to study
\check{S}_{n,k}(u_{n})
and, consequently,
\beta_{n,k}(u_{n}).
In other words, one would need to use better approximators for
\beta_{n,k}(u_{n}).
At present, we are unsure if
\check{S}_{n,k}(u_{n})
will once again dictate the behaviour of
\beta_{n,k}(u_{n}).
In particular, it is not clear if
\mathbb{E}[S^{\prime}_{n,k}(u_{n})]
will dominate the expected count of the more intricate subcomplexes having non-trivial Betti numbers. But if this behaviour indeed holds, then, perhaps,
\beta_{n,k}(u_{n})
can be studied using
S^{\prime}_{n,k}(u_{n}).

As we noted in Remark

R
13

, another obvious work for the future is to show the CLT for Betti numbers in the entire non-vanishing regime. One way to proceed could be to build upon the ideas from the CLT proof in
[
24
]
. There, the Betti number are approximated by the contribution from all those isolated components whose vertex support is bounded by some sufficiently large
m.
It is then shown that if the regime is so chosen that components of size
m+1
cannot occur, then a CLT for the approximated Betti number implies one for the actual Betti number itself. Presently, the difficulty in adopting this approach to our setup is in proving the CLT for the isolated components. It appears that the isolation condition, which requires dealing simultaneously with Gaussian random variables exceeding and being below some threshold, entails use of a modified Stein-Chen approach with negative covariances. It is not clear how to generalize Theorem
2.11
to handle this case. We expect the computations to be a bit involved. Nevertheless, this should be quite interesting since we would then be able to obtain a better estimate of the variance of
\beta_{n,k}(u_{n})
itself; see Remark

R
12

.

In a sense, this work studies the distribution of the number of holes for all sufficiently large but fixed excursion level
u
and window size
n.
An alternate way to look at this setup would be fix
n
and only vary
u,
and then ask questions about the statistics of the range of
u-
values over which each hole persists; note that holes can appear and disappear as
u
is varied. A formal way to record such birth and death times is via what is known as the ‘persistence diagram’. In studying the persistence diagram associated with Gaussian excursions, ideas from
[
38
]
should be of help.

Since
\mathbb{E}[\beta_{n,k}(u_{n})]/\mathbb{E}[\beta_{n,0}(u_{n})]\to 0
for each
k\geq 1
, and also since there are only finitely many different Betti numbers in any given dimension
d
, we believe that the EPC of
\mathcal{K}(n;u)
should also exhibit trivial, Poisson, and CLT behaviour with regimes being determined by those of
\beta_{n,0}(u_{n})
. In fact, one should also be able to easily prove limit theorems for the LKCs using the results already established in this paper.

Recall that our theorems are proved in the sparse regime. The next logical step would be to derive limiting results in the thermodynamic regime, but without assuming the fast decay conditions as in
[
35
]
. Here, we believe that the ideas from
[
42
,
43
]
may turn out be very useful. The resulting theorems would supplement those in
[
14
]
,
[
27
]
,
[
31
]
, etc.

As has been mentioned in
[
18
]
, and as is demonstrated by
[
19
]
, one could use similar techniques as in this paper to establish Poisson approximation theorems for number of exceedances of nonstationary Gaussian sequences as well. This suggests that our results on Betti numbers should also be generalizable to the nonstationary scenario.

Considering
m-
dependent stationary Gaussian sequences,
[
34
]
and
[
16
]
obtained compound Poisson approximations for the number of exceedances by suitably modifying the Stein-Chen method used in
[
18
]
. In such cases when there are strong local covariances, it is interesting to ask whether we can extend these results to Betti numbers of
m-
dependent Gaussian fields. Since the basic ideas involved are similar, we believe that this should be possible. This work assumes significance from the perspective of ARMA models.

Regarding extending our results to general fields,
[
33
]
suggests that our theorems can be also be shown for
\chi^{2}-
fields. For other fields, we first note that even though Theorem
2.9
is quite general, an analogue of Theorem
2.11
needs to be established. Also, since we relied on precise multivariate Gaussian tail estimates, we will be required to estimate the corresponding tail probabilities for the particular field under consideration.

Lastly, it would be interesting to extend the ideas in this paper and those discussed above to the dynamic setup where the random field of interest also evolves with time. In this direction, ideas from
[
39
]
should be of help.

Appendix A

Lemma A.1
.

Let
W_{m}(\rho)
be as defined above (
6.1
). Then, the eigenvalues of the matrix
W_{m}(\rho)
are
(1-\rho),
repeated
(m-1)
times, and
(1+(m-1)\rho).
The corresponding linearly independent eigenvectors are
\bar{w}_{1},\ldots,\bar{w}_{m},
where
\bar{w}_{m}=\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{m}
and, for
j=1,\ldots,m-1,

\bar{w}_{j}(\ell)=\begin{cases}1,&\text{ if $\ell=1,$}\\
-1,&\text{ if $\ell=j+1,$}\\
0,&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Proof.

This is immediate.
∎

Lemma A.2
.

Let
W_{m_{1},m_{2}}(\rho,\mu)
be as defined in (
6.1
) with
m_{1},m_{2}\geq 1.
Then,
m_{1}+m_{2}-2
eigenvalues of
W_{m_{1},m_{2}}(\rho,\mu)
are equal to
(1-\rho).
The corresponding linearly independent eigenvectors are
\bar{w}_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,\bar{w}_{m_{1}-1}^{\prime},

\bar{w}_{1}^{\prime\prime},\ldots,\bar{w}_{m_{2}-1}^{\prime\prime},
where

\bar{w}_{j}^{\prime}(\ell)=\begin{cases}1,&\text{ if $\ell=1,$}\\
-1,&\text{ if $\ell=j+1,$}\\
0,&\text{ otherwise;}\end{cases}

and

\bar{w}_{j}^{\prime\prime}(\ell)=\begin{cases}1,&\text{ if $\ell=m_{1}+1,$}\\
-1,&\text{ if $\ell=m_{1}+j+1,$}\\
0,&\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

Additionally, if
\rho\neq 1
and if at least one of
(1+(m_{1}-1)\rho)
and
(1+(m_{2}-1)\rho)
is non-zero, then

|W_{m_{1},m_{2}}(\rho,\mu)|=(1-\rho)^{m_{1}+m_{2}-2}[(1+(m_{1}-1)\rho)(1+(m_{2}-1)\rho)-m_{1}m_{2}\mu^{2}].

Lastly, the following statements hold when
m_{1},m_{2}
satisfy some special conditions:

1.

If
m_{1}=m_{2},
then the remaining two eigenvalues of
W_{m_{1},m_{2}}(\rho,\mu)
are
1+(m_{1}-1)\rho\pm m_{1}\mu
with the corresponding eigenvectors being
\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{2m_{1}}
and
(\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{m_{1}},-\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{m_{1}}).

2.

If
m_{2}=1,
then the remaining two eigenvalues are
[2+(m_{1}-1)\rho\pm\sqrt{(m_{1}-1)^{2}\rho^{2}+4m_{1}\mu^{2}}]/2.

Proof.

It is straightforward to verify that
\bar{w}_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,\bar{w}_{m_{1}-1}^{\prime},

\bar{w}_{1}^{\prime\prime},\ldots,\bar{w}_{m_{2}-1}^{\prime\prime}
are indeed independent eigenvectors with eigenvalue
1-\rho.

We now establish the determinant formula. Without loss of generality, let
(1+(m_{1}-1)\rho)
be non-zero. This, combined with the fact that
\rho\neq 1,
shows that all the eigenvalues of
W_{m_{1}}(\rho),
given by Lemma
A.1
, are non-zero; hence, it is invertible. Then, from matrix theory concerning determinant of block matrices,

|W_{m_{1},m_{2}}(\rho,\mu)|=|W_{m_{1}}(\rho)|\;|W_{m_{2}}(\rho)-\mu^{2}\mathbf{1}_{m_{2},m_{1}}\;W_{m_{1}}^{-1}(\rho)\;\mathbf{1}_{m_{1},m_{2}}|.

(A.1)

From Lemma
A.1
again, observe that

|W_{m_{1}}(\rho)|=(1-\rho)^{m_{1}-1}(1+(m_{1}-1)\rho),

(A.2)

and that
\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{m_{1}}
is an eigenvector of
W_{m_{1}}(\rho).
This latter fact and that
\mathbf{1}_{m_{2},m_{2}}=W_{m_{2}}(1)
show

\mathbf{1}_{m_{2},m_{1}}\;W_{m_{1}}^{-1}(\rho)\;\mathbf{1}_{m_{1},m_{2}}=\frac{m_{1}}{1+(m_{1}-1)\rho}W_{m_{2}}(1).

Using this calculation and Lemma
A.1
one last time, it is now easy to see that the eigenvectors of
W_{m_{2}}(\rho)-\mu^{2}\mathbf{1}_{m_{2},m_{1}}\;W_{m_{1}}^{-1}(\rho)\;\mathbf{1}_{m_{1},m_{2}}
are exactly as those of
W_{m_{2}}(\rho)
and the corresponding eigenvalues are
(1-\rho),
repeated
(m_{2}-1)
times, and
(1+(m_{2}-1)\rho)-m_{1}m_{2}\mu^{2}/(1+(m_{1}-1)\rho).
The last eigenvalue is well defined since
(1+(m_{1}-1)\rho)
is non-zero. Therefore,

|W_{m_{2}}(\rho)-\mu^{2}\mathbf{1}_{m_{2},m_{1}}\;W_{m_{1}}^{-1}(\rho)\;\mathbf{1}_{m_{1},m_{2}}|=(1-\rho)^{m_{2}-1}\left[1+(m_{2}-1)\rho)-\frac{m_{1}m_{2}\mu^{2}}{1+(m_{1}-1)\rho}\right].

(A.3)

Substituting (
A.2
) and (
A.3
) in (
A.1
), the desired result follows.

It remains to show the statements on the remaining eigenvalues. The first one is trivially true. So, consider the case that
m_{2}=1.
From our earlier calculations, we have

|W_{m_{1},1}(\rho,\mu)|=(1-\rho)^{m_{1}-1}(1+(m_{1}-1)\rho-m_{1}\mu^{2})

and that
m_{1}-1
eigenvalues of
W_{m_{1},1}(\rho,\mu)
are
1-\rho.
Separately, the trace of
W_{m_{1},1}(\rho,\mu)
is
m_{1}+1.
If we let
\kappa_{1}
and
\kappa_{2}
be the remaining two eigenvalues, it then follows that

\kappa_{1}+\kappa_{2}=2+(m_{1}-1)\rho\text{ and }\kappa_{1}\kappa_{2}=1+(m_{1}-1)\rho-m_{1}\mu^{2}.

From this, the desired result is easy to see.
∎

Lemma A.3
.

Fix
m_{1},m_{2}\geq 1.
Let
\rho,\mu\in[0,1)
be such that both
1+(m_{1}-1)\rho-m_{1}\mu
and
1+(m_{2}-1)\rho-m_{2}\mu
are positive. Then,
W_{m_{1},m_{2}}(\rho,\mu)
from (
6.1
) is positive definite.

Proof.

From Lemma
A.2
,
m_{1}+m_{2}-2
eigenvalues of
W_{m_{1},m_{2}}(\rho,\mu)
are
1-\rho;
these are already positive as
\rho<1.
Let
\kappa_{1}
and
\kappa_{2}
be the remaining two eigenvalues. Clearly, the trace of
W_{m_{1},m_{2}}(\rho,\mu)
is
m_{1}+m_{2}.
Consequently, we have
\kappa_{1}+\kappa_{2}=2(1-\rho)+(m_{1}+m_{2})\rho;
this is positive since
\rho\in[0,1).
Thus, to prove that both
\kappa_{1}
and
\kappa_{2}
are positive and hence show that
W_{m_{1},m_{2}}(\rho,\mu)
is positive definite, it suffices to show that
\kappa_{1}\kappa_{2}>0.

Now observe that

\displaystyle\kappa_{1}\kappa_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(1+(m_{1}-1)\rho)(1+(m_{2}-1)\rho)-m_{1}m_{2}\mu^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(1+(m_{1}-1)\rho-m_{1}\mu)(1+(m_{2}-1)\rho-m_{2}\mu)

\displaystyle+\;m_{1}\mu(1+(m_{2}-1)\rho-m_{2}\mu)+m_{2}\mu(1+(m_{1}-1)\rho-m_{1}\mu)

\displaystyle>

\displaystyle 0,

where the first relation follows from the determinant formula given in Lemma
A.2
and the fact that the remaining
m_{1}+m_{2}-2
eigenvalues of
W_{m_{1},m_{2}}(\rho,\mu)
are
1-\rho,
while the last relation follows from the given conditions on
\rho
and
\mu.
The desired result is now easy to see.
∎

Lemma A.4
.

Fix
m\geq 1
and
u\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}.
Let
\rho,\rho^{\prime}\in[0,1)
be such that
1+(m-1)\rho-m\rho^{\prime}>0.
For
\mu\in[0,\rho^{\prime}],
let
\bar{Z}_{\mu}\sim N(\bar{0},W_{m,m}(\rho,\mu)),
where
W_{m,m}(\rho,\mu)
is as in (
6.1
). Let
f_{\bar{Z}_{\mu}}
denote the density of
\bar{Z}_{\mu}
and let
g(\mu):=\mathbb{P}\{\bar{Z}_{\mu}\geq u\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{2m}\}.
Then, the following statements are true.

1.

g(\mu)
is continuous in
[0,\rho^{\prime}].

2.

g(\mu)
is differentiable on
(0,\rho^{\prime}).
Further, for any
\mu\in(0,\rho^{\prime}),

\left|\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu}[g(\mu)]\right|\leq u^{-2(m-1)}\exp\left[\frac{-mu^{2}}{1+(m-1)\rho+m\mu}\right]

(A.4)

for some constant
\Cr{c:IB}\geq 0
which depends on
m.

Remark A.5
.

Because of Lemma
A.3
,
W_{m,m}(\rho,\mu)
is a symmetric positive definite matrix and, hence,
\bar{Z}_{\mu}
is well defined.

Proof of Lemma
A.4
.

We provide arguments for each statement separately.

•

Arguments for Statement
1
: By definition, for any
\bar{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{2m}
and
\mu\in[0,\rho^{\prime}],
we have

f_{\bar{Z}_{\mu}}(\bar{z})=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{m}\sqrt{|W_{m,m}(\rho,\mu)|}}\exp\left[\frac{-\bar{z}\,W^{-1}_{m,m}(\rho,\mu)\,\bar{z}^{\top}}{2}\right].

(A.5)

For each
\mu\in[0,\rho^{\prime}],
observe that

\displaystyle|W_{m,m}(\rho,\mu)|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(1-\rho)^{2m-2}[(1+(m-1)\rho)^{2}-m^{2}\mu^{2}]

(A.6)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle(1-\rho)^{2m-2}[(1+(m-1)\rho)^{2}-(m\rho^{\prime})^{2}]

(A.7)

\displaystyle>

\displaystyle 0,

where the first relation follows from Lemma
A.2
, the second holds since
\mu\in[0,\rho^{\prime}],
while the last one holds because
\rho,\rho^{\prime}\in[0,1)
and
1+(m-1)\rho-\rho^{\prime}>0.
Again, from Lemma
A.2
and because
\rho,\mu\geq 0,
note that the largest eigenvalue of
W_{m,m}(\rho,\mu)
is
1+(m-1)\rho+m\mu;
since
\mu\leq\rho^{\prime},
it is bounded from above by
1+(m-1)\rho+m\rho^{\prime}.

Using these calculations, it then follows that

|f_{\bar{Z}_{\mu}}(\bar{z})|\\
\leq\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{m}(1-\rho)^{m-1}\sqrt{[1+(m-1)\rho]^{2}-[m\rho^{\prime}]^{2}}}\exp\left[\frac{-\bar{z}\bar{z}^{\top}}{2(1+(m-1)\rho+m\rho^{\prime})}\right].

(A.8)

Note that the RHS is integrable and does not depend on
\mu.

Now, consider any sequence
\{\mu_{n}\}\subset[0,\rho^{\prime}]
with
\lim_{n\to\infty}\mu_{n}=\mu^{*}.
As
f_{\bar{Z}_{\mu}}
is continuous in
\mu
on
[0,\rho^{\prime}],
we have
\lim_{n\to\infty}f_{\bar{Z}_{\mu_{n}}}(\bar{z})=f_{\bar{Z}_{\mu^{*}}}(\bar{z}).
Using this and the fact that the RHS of (
A.8
) is integrable, it follows from the dominated convergence theorem that
\lim_{n\to\infty}g(\mu_{n})=g(\mu^{*}).
This shows that
g(\mu)
is continuous on
[0,\rho^{\prime}],
as desired.

•

Arguments for Statement
2
: For
\hat{h}_{ij}(\mu)=h_{ij}(\mu)/|W_{m,m}(\rho,\mu)|,
where
\{h_{ij}(\mu)\}
are some suitable polynomials in
\mu,
it is easy to see that (
A.5
) can be rewritten as

f_{\bar{Z}_{\mu}}(\bar{z})=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{m}\sqrt{|W_{m,m}(\rho,\mu)|}}\exp\left[\frac{-\sum_{i,j=1}^{2m}z(i)\;z(j)\;\hat{h}_{ij}(\mu)}{2}\right].

Hence, for
\mu\in(0,\rho^{\prime}),

\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu}\left[f_{\bar{Z}_{\mu}}(\bar{z})\right]=-\frac{f_{\bar{Z}_{\mu}}(\bar{z})}{2}\left[\frac{1}{|W_{m,m}(\rho,\mu)|}\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu}[|W_{m,m}(\rho,\mu)|]+\sum_{i,j}z(i)\;z(j)\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu}\hat{h}_{ij}(\mu)\right].

Like
h_{ij}(\mu),
note from (
A.6
) that
|W_{m,m}(\rho,\mu)|
is also a polynomial in
\mu.
Consequently, we have that
\frac{1}{|W_{m,m}(\rho,\mu)|}\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu}[|W_{m,m}(\rho,\mu)|]
and
\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu}\hat{h}_{ij}(\mu)
are rational functions in
\mu,
whose denominators are
|W_{m,m}(\rho,\mu)|
and
|W_{m,m}(\rho,\mu)|^{2},
respectively. Because
[0,\rho^{\prime}]
is a compact set and since (
A.7
) holds, it then follows that

\sup_{\mu\in(0,\rho^{\prime})}\left|\frac{1}{|W_{m,m}(\rho,\mu)|}\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu}[|W_{m,m}(\rho,\mu)|]\right|=\sup_{\mu\in[0,\rho^{\prime}]}\left|\frac{1}{|W_{m,m}(\rho,\mu)|}\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu}[|W_{m,m}(\rho,\mu)|]\right|<\infty;

similarly,

\sup_{\mu\in(0,\rho^{\prime})}\left|\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu}\hat{h}_{ij}(\mu)\right|<\infty.

Combining these observations with (
A.8
), it then follows that there exists a constant
\Cr{c:IB}^{\prime}\geq 0
depending on
m
such that

\left|\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu}\left[f_{\bar{Z}_{\mu}}(\bar{z})\right]\right|\leq\Cr{c:IB}^{\prime}\left[1+\bar{z}\bar{z}^{\top}\right]\exp\left[\frac{-\bar{z}\bar{z}^{\top}}{2(1+(m-1)\rho+m\rho^{\prime})}\right]

(A.9)

for each
\mu\in(0,\rho^{\prime}).
As the RHS is independent of
\mu
and integrable and since

\mathbb{P}\{\bar{Z}_{\mu}\geq u\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{2m}\}=\int_{[u,\infty)^{2m}}f_{\bar{Z}_{\mu}}(\bar{z})\mathrm{d}\bar{z}=\int_{(u,\infty)^{2m}}f_{\bar{Z}_{\mu}}(\bar{z})\mathrm{d}\bar{z},

the dominated convergence theorem shows that, for each
\mu\in(0,\rho^{\prime}),

\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu}\mathbb{P}\{\bar{Z}_{\mu}\geq u\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{2m}\}=\int_{(u,\infty)^{2m}}\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu}\left[f_{\bar{Z}_{\mu}}(\bar{z})\right]\mathrm{d\bar{z}}.

(A.10)

This shows that
g(\mu)
is differentiable over
(0,\rho^{\prime}),
as desired.

It remains to prove (
A.4
). Let
\psi:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
be a monotonically increasing
C^{\infty}-
function satisfying

\psi(z)=\begin{cases}0&\text{ if }z\leq 0,\\
1&\text{ if }z\geq 1,\\
\end{cases}

and
|\frac{d\psi(z)}{dz}|<\infty.
Let
\Cr{c:IB}^{\prime\prime}:=\sup_{z}|\frac{d\psi(z)}{dz}|.
For
\epsilon\in(0,1],
let
\psi_{\epsilon}(z;u):=\psi\left(\frac{z-u}{\epsilon}\right).
Clearly, if
\epsilon_{1}<\epsilon_{2},
then
\psi_{\epsilon_{1}}(z;u)\geq\psi_{\epsilon_{2}}(z;u)
for all
z.
Also,
\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\psi_{\epsilon}(z;u)=\mathds{1}[z>u].
Thus, it follows that
\psi_{\epsilon}(z;u)
monotonically increases to
\mathds{1}[z>u]
as
\epsilon\to 0.
For
\bar{z}\equiv(z(1),\ldots,z(2m))\in\mathbb{R}^{2m}
and
\epsilon\in(0,1],
let

\Psi_{\epsilon}(\bar{z};u):=\prod_{i=1}^{2m}\psi_{\epsilon}(z(i);u).

Then, from (
A.10
), we have

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu}\mathbb{P}\{\bar{Z}_{\mu}\geq u\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{2m}\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2m}}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{2m}\mathds{1}[\bar{z}(i)>u]\right]\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu}\left[f_{\bar{Z}_{\mu}}(\bar{z})\right]\mathrm{d\bar{z}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2m}}\left[\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\Psi_{\epsilon}(\bar{z};u)\right]\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu}\left[f_{\bar{Z}_{\mu}}(\bar{z})\right]\mathrm{d\bar{z}}.

As
|\Psi_{\epsilon}(\bar{z};u)|\leq 1,
we have, for each
\epsilon>0,

\left|\Psi_{\epsilon}(\bar{z};u)\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu}\left[f_{\bar{Z}_{\mu}}(\bar{z})\right]\right|\leq\left|\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu}\left[f_{\bar{Z}_{\mu}}(\bar{z})\right]\right|.

The RHS is integrable for each
\mu\in(0,\rho^{\prime}),
as discussed above. Hence, by dominated convergence theorem, it follows that

\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu}\mathbb{P}\{\bar{Z}_{\mu}\geq u\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{2m}\}=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2m}}\Psi_{\epsilon}(\bar{z};u)\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu}\left[f_{\bar{Z}_{\mu}}(\bar{z})\right]\mathrm{d\bar{z}}.

(A.11)

Let
\sigma_{ij}
denote the
(i,j)-
th entry of
W_{m,m}(\rho,\mu).
Since
\sigma_{ij}=\mu
only if either
i\in\{1,\ldots,m\}
and
j\in\{m+1,\ldots,2m\},
or
i\in\{m+1,\ldots,2m\}
and
j\in\{1,\ldots,m\},
we have

\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu}\left[f_{\bar{Z}_{\mu}}(\bar{z})\right]=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=m+1}^{2m}\frac{\partial}{\partial\sigma_{ij}}\left[f_{\bar{Z}_{\mu}}(\bar{z})\right]+\sum_{i=m+1}^{2m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\frac{\partial}{\partial\sigma_{ij}}\left[f_{\bar{Z}_{\mu}}(\bar{z})\right].

(A.12)

Pick
(i,j)
where
\sigma_{ij}=\mu.
Then, by some standard algebra, it is easy to see that

\frac{\partial f_{\bar{Z}_{\mu}}(\bar{z})}{\partial\sigma_{ij}}=\frac{\partial^{2}f_{\bar{Z}_{\mu}}(\bar{z})}{\partial z(i)\;\partial z(j)}.

From this, using integration by parts twice, it follows that

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2m}}\Psi_{\epsilon}(\bar{z};u)\frac{\partial}{\partial\sigma_{ij}}\left[f_{\bar{Z}_{\mu}}(\bar{z})\right]\mathrm{d\bar{z}}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2m}}\frac{\partial^{2}\left[\Psi_{\epsilon}(\bar{z};u)\right]}{\partial z(i)\;\partial z(j)}f_{\bar{Z}_{\mu}}(\bar{z})\mathrm{d\bar{z}}.

For some references on the above two steps, see the proof of
[
2
, Theorem 2.3]
.

Now, as
\left|\frac{\partial\psi_{\epsilon}(z;u)}{\partial z}\right|\leq\frac{\Cr{c:IB}^{\prime\prime}}{\epsilon}
for
z\in(u,u+\epsilon)
and
0
otherwise and, since,
\psi_{\epsilon}(z;u)\leq 1
for
z\in(u,\infty)
and
0
otherwise, the above relation shows that

\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2m}}\Psi_{\epsilon}(\bar{z};u)\frac{\partial}{\partial\sigma_{ij}}\left[f_{\bar{Z}_{\mu}}(\bar{z})\right]\mathrm{d\bar{z}}\right|\\
\leq[\Cr{c:IB}^{\prime\prime}]^{2}\max_{\bar{z}_{ij}\in(u,u+\epsilon)^{2}}\left[f_{ij}(\bar{z}_{ij})\;\mathbb{P}\{\bar{Z}_{\hat{i}\hat{j}}\geq u\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{2m-2}|\bar{Z}_{ij}=\bar{z}_{ij}\}\right],

(A.13)

where
\bar{Z}_{ij}
is a
2-
dimensional vector made up of the
i-
th and
j-
th component of
\bar{Z}_{\mu},

\bar{Z}_{\hat{i}\hat{j}}
is
\bar{Z}_{\mu}
with
i-
th and
j-
th components deleted,
f_{ij}
is the density of
\bar{Z}_{ij},
and
\bar{z}_{ij}
is the
2-
dimensional vector made up of
z(i)
and
z(j).

Let
f_{\hat{i}\hat{j}}(\cdot|\bar{Z}_{ij}=\bar{z}_{ij})
denote the conditional density of
\bar{Z}_{\hat{i}\hat{j}}.
Clearly,
\bar{Z}_{\hat{i}\hat{j}}|\bar{Z}_{ij}=\bar{z}_{ij}
is Gaussian with mean
\mathscr{M}(\bar{z}_{ij}):=\bar{z}_{ij}\left[\textnormal{Var}[\bar{Z}_{ij}]\right]^{-1}\textnormal{Cov}[\bar{Z}_{ij},\bar{Z}_{\hat{i}\hat{j}}]
and covariance matrix

\textnormal{Var}[\bar{Z}_{\hat{i}\hat{j}}|\bar{Z}_{ij}]=\textnormal{Var}[\bar{Z}_{\hat{i}\hat{j}}]-\textnormal{Cov}[\bar{Z}_{\hat{i}\hat{j}},\bar{Z}_{ij}]\left[\textnormal{Var}[\bar{Z}_{ij}]\right]^{-1}\textnormal{Cov}[\bar{Z}_{ij},\bar{Z}_{\hat{i}\hat{j}}].

(A.14)

Since
\textnormal{Var}[\bar{Z}_{\hat{i}\hat{j}}|\bar{Z}_{ij}]
is the Schur-complement of
\textnormal{Var}[\bar{Z}_{ij}],
and since
\textnormal{Var}[\bar{Z}_{\mu}]
is positive definite for each
\mu\in[0,\rho^{\prime}],
it follows that
\textnormal{Var}[\bar{Z}_{\hat{i}\hat{j}}|\bar{Z}_{ij}]
itself is positive definite for each
\mu\in[0,\rho^{\prime}].

From Lemma
A.2
and, since
\rho,\mu>0,
note that

\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{2m}\,[W_{m,m}(\rho,\mu)]^{-1}=\frac{1}{1+(m-1)\rho+m\mu}\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{2m}>0.

(A.15)

Hence, it follows that, for any permutation matrix
P,

\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{2m}\,[P\,W_{m,m}(\rho,\mu)\,P^{\top}]^{-1}=\frac{1}{1+(m-1)\rho+m\mu}\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{2m}>0.

(A.16)

Now, pick a permutation matrix
P
so that

P\,W_{m,m}(\rho,\mu)\,P^{\top}=\begin{bmatrix}\textnormal{Var}[\bar{Z}_{\hat{i}\hat{j}}]&\textnormal{Cov}[\bar{Z}_{\hat{i}\hat{j}},\bar{Z}_{ij}]\\
\textnormal{Cov}[\bar{Z}_{ij},\bar{Z}_{\hat{i}\hat{j}}]&\textnormal{Var}[\bar{Z}_{ij}]\end{bmatrix}.

From (
A.14
), (
A.16
), and the block matrix inversion formula, we then have

\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{2m}\begin{bmatrix}[\textnormal{Var}[\bar{Z}_{\hat{i}\hat{j}}|\bar{Z}_{ij}]]^{-1}\\[2.15277pt]
-\left[\textnormal{Var}[\bar{Z}_{ij}]\right]^{-1}\textnormal{Cov}[\bar{Z}_{ij},\bar{Z}_{\hat{i}\hat{j}}][\textnormal{Var}[\bar{Z}_{\hat{i}\hat{j}}|\bar{Z}_{ij}]]^{-1}\end{bmatrix}=\frac{1}{1+(m-1)\rho+m\mu}\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{2m-2}>0.

Clearly, the above relation also holds if the vector
\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{2m}
is replaced by
u\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{2m}.
Hence,

[u\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{2m-2}-\mathscr{M}(u\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{2})]\,[\textnormal{Var}[\bar{Z}_{\hat{i}\hat{j}}|\bar{Z}_{ij}]]^{-1}=\frac{1}{1+(m-1)\rho+m\mu}\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{2m-2}>0.

(A.17)

Consequently, by continuity of affine functions, for sufficiently small
\epsilon>0
and each
\bar{z}_{ij}\in(u,u+\epsilon)^{2},
the Savage condition

\bar{\Delta}_{\bar{z}_{ij}}:=[u\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{2m-2}-\mathscr{M}(\bar{z}_{ij})]\,[\textnormal{Var}[\bar{Z}_{\hat{i}\hat{j}}|\bar{Z}_{ij}]]^{-1}>0

(A.18)

holds. Therefore, from Lemma
2.4
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\{\bar{Z}_{\hat{i}\hat{j}}\geq u\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{2m-2}|\bar{Z}_{ij}=\bar{z}_{ij}\}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\{\bar{Z}_{\hat{i}\hat{j}}-\mathscr{M}(\bar{z}_{ij})\geq u\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{2m-2}-\mathscr{M}(\bar{z}_{ij})|\bar{Z}_{ij}=\bar{z}_{ij}\}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\prod_{\ell=1}^{2m-2}\bar{\Delta}_{\bar{z}_{ij}}(\ell)}f_{\hat{i}\hat{j}}(u\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{2m-2}|Z_{ij}=z_{ij}).

Substituting this in (
A.13
), we get

\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2m}}\Psi_{\epsilon}(\bar{z};u)\frac{\partial}{\partial\sigma_{ij}}\left[f_{\bar{Z}_{\mu}}(\bar{z})\right]\mathrm{d\bar{z}}\right|\leq[\Cr{c:IB}^{\prime\prime}]^{2}\max_{\bar{z}_{ij}\in(u,u+\epsilon)^{2}}\left[\frac{f_{\bar{Z}_{\mu}}((\bar{z}_{ij},u\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{2m-2})P)}{\prod_{\ell=1}^{2m-2}\bar{\Delta}_{\bar{z}_{ij}}(\ell)}\right].

Separately, from (
A.17
) and the fact that
\mu\leq\rho^{\prime},
observe that

\prod_{\ell=1}^{2m-2}\bar{\Delta}_{u\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{2m-2}}(\ell)=\frac{u^{2m-2}}{(1+(m-1)\rho+m\mu)^{2m-2}}\geq\frac{u^{2m-2}}{(1+(m-1)\rho+m\rho^{\prime})^{2m-2}}.

(A.19)

The above two relations and the fact that both
f_{\bar{Z}_{\mu}}
and
\bar{\Delta}
are continuous functions now show

\limsup_{\epsilon\to 0}\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2m}}\Psi_{\epsilon}(\bar{z};u)\frac{\partial}{\partial\sigma_{ij}}\left[f_{\bar{Z}_{\mu}}(\bar{z})\right]\mathrm{d\bar{z}}\right|\\
\leq[\Cr{c:IB}^{\prime\prime}]^{2}\left[\frac{(1+(m-1)\rho+m\rho^{\prime})^{2m-2}}{u^{2m-2}}f_{\bar{Z}_{\mu}}(u\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{2m})\right].

(A.20)

Therefore, by substituting (
A.5
) in (
A.20
) and then making use of the determinant bound given in (
A.7
) and the fact that

u\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{2m}W^{-1}_{m,m}(\rho,\mu)u\bar{\mathbf{1}}^{\top}_{2m}=2u^{2}m/(1+(m-1)\rho+m\mu),

(A.21)

we have

\displaystyle\limsup_{\epsilon\to 0}\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2m}}\Psi_{\epsilon}(\bar{z};u)\frac{\partial}{\partial\sigma_{ij}}\left[f_{\bar{Z}_{\mu}}(\bar{z})\right]\mathrm{d\bar{z}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle[\Cr{c:IB}^{\prime\prime}]^{2}\frac{[1+(m-1)\rho+m\rho^{\prime}]^{2m-2}}{(2\pi)^{m}(1-\rho)^{m-1}\sqrt{[1+(m-1)\rho]^{2}-[m\rho^{\prime}]^{2}}}u^{-2(m-1)}\exp\left[\frac{-mu^{2}}{1+(m-1)\rho+m\mu}\right].

The desired result now follows from (
A.11
) and (
A.12
).

This completes the proof.
∎

Lemma A.6
.

Let
m\geq 2,

1\leq m_{1},m_{2}<m,
and
Q_{m,m_{1},m_{2}}(\rho,\mu^{\prime},\mu)
be as in (
6.2
). Let
\rho,\mu^{\prime}\in[0,1)
be such that one of the following holds:

1.

\rho>\mu^{\prime},
or

2.

m_{1}=m_{2}=1
and
1+(m-2)\rho-(m-1)\mu^{\prime}>0.

Then,

|Q_{m,m_{1},m_{2}}(\rho,\mu^{\prime},\mu)|=|W_{m_{1},m-m_{1}}(\rho,\mu^{\prime})||W_{m_{2},m-m_{2}}(\rho,\mu^{\prime})|\Big[1-\\
\mu^{2}[\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{m}\,W^{-1}_{m_{1},m-m_{1}}(\rho,\mu^{\prime})\,\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{m}^{\top}]\,[\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{m}\,W^{-1}_{m_{2},m-m_{2}}(\rho,\mu^{\prime})\,\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{m}^{\top}]\Big].

Proof.

Due to the conditions on
\rho,\mu^{\prime},
it follows from Lemma
A.3
that both the matrices
W_{m_{1},m-m_{1}}(\rho,\mu^{\prime})
and
W_{m_{2},m-m_{2}}(\rho,\mu^{\prime})
are positive definite and, hence, invertible.

Now, observe that

\displaystyle|Q_{m,m_{1},m_{2}}(\rho,\mu^{\prime},\mu)|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|W_{m_{1},m-m_{1}}(\rho,\mu^{\prime})|\,|W_{m_{2},m-m_{2}}(\rho,\mu^{\prime})-\mu^{2}\mathbf{1}_{m,m}W^{-1}_{m_{1},m-m_{1}}(\rho,\mu^{\prime})\mathbf{1}_{m,m}|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|W_{m_{1},m-m_{1}}(\rho,\mu^{\prime})|\,|W_{m_{2},m-m_{2}}(\rho,\mu^{\prime})-\mu^{2}\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{m}^{\top}\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{m}W^{-1}_{m_{1},m-m_{1}}(\rho,\mu^{\prime})\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{m}^{\top}\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{m}|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|W_{m_{1},m-m_{1}}(\rho,\mu^{\prime})|\,|W_{m_{2},m-m_{2}}(\rho,\mu^{\prime})-\mu^{2}[\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{m}W^{-1}_{m_{1},m-m_{1}}(\rho,\mu^{\prime})\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{m}^{\top}]\,\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{m}^{\top}\,\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{m}|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|W_{m_{1},m-m_{1}}(\rho,\mu^{\prime})|\,|W_{m_{2},m-m_{2}}(\rho,\mu^{\prime})|\,\Big|\mathbb{I}_{m}-

\displaystyle\mu^{2}[\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{m}W^{-1}_{m_{1},m-m_{1}}(\rho,\mu^{\prime})\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{m}^{\top}]\,\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{m}^{\top}\,\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{m}W^{-1}_{m_{2},m-m_{2}}(\rho,\mu^{\prime})\Big|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|W_{m_{1},m-m_{1}}(\rho,\mu^{\prime})||W_{m_{2},m-m_{2}}(\rho,\mu^{\prime})|\Big[1-

\displaystyle\mu^{2}[\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{m}\,W^{-1}_{m_{1},m-m_{1}}(\rho,\mu^{\prime})\,\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{m}^{\top}]\,[\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{m}\,W^{-1}_{m_{2},m-m_{2}}(\rho,\mu^{\prime})\,\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{m}^{\top}]\Big],

where the first relation follows from the determinant formula for block matrices, the second relation holds since
\mathbf{1}_{m,m}=\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{m}^{\top}\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{m},
the fourth relation follows by taking
W_{m_{2},m-m_{2}}(\rho,\mu^{\prime})
common and then applying the product rule for determinants (here,
\mathbb{I}_{m}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times m}
is the identity matrix), while the final relation follows from Sylvester’s determinant identity. This gives the desired result.
∎

For
m\geq 1,
let
\hat{Q}_{m}(\mu)
be the
2m\times 2m-
dimensional matrix given by

\hat{Q}_{m}(\mu)=\begin{bmatrix}0&\mu\mathbf{1}_{m,m}\\
\mu\mathbf{1}_{m,m}&0\end{bmatrix}.

(A.22)

Lemma A.7
.

The eigenvalues of
\hat{Q}_{m}(\mu)
are
m\mu,

-m\mu,
and
0
repeated
2(m-1)
times. The corresponding eigenvectors are
\bar{v}_{1}=\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{2m},

\bar{v}_{2}=(\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{m},-\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{m}),
and
w^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,w^{\prime}_{m-1},w^{\prime\prime}_{1},\ldots,w^{\prime\prime}_{m-1},
with the latter defined as in Lemma
A.2
(one needs to set
m_{1}=m_{2}=m
there).

Proof.

This is straightforward to see.
∎

Lemma A.8
.

Let
m\geq 2
and
\rho,\mu^{\prime}\in[0,1)
be such that
\rho>\mu^{\prime}.
For
1\leq j<m,
let
Q_{m,j,j}(\rho,\mu^{\prime},\mu)
be defined as in (
6.2
). Then, there exists
\delta\equiv\delta(m,\rho,\mu^{\prime})
and
\kappa\equiv\kappa(m,\rho,\mu^{\prime})
such that, for any
\mu\in[0,\delta]
and
1\leq j<m,
the matrix
Q_{m,j,j}(\rho,\mu^{\prime},\mu)
is positive definite and its maximum eigenvalue is bounded from above by
\kappa.

Proof.

On account of the conditions on
\rho,\mu^{\prime},
it follows from Lemma
A.3
that
W_{j,m-j}(\rho,\mu^{\prime})
is positive definite for each
1\leq j<m.
Let
\kappa^{\min}_{j}\equiv\kappa^{\min}_{j}(m,j,\rho,\mu^{\prime})
be the smallest eigenvalue of
W_{j,m-j}(\rho,\mu^{\prime})
and let

\kappa^{\min}\equiv\kappa^{\min}(m,\rho,\mu^{\prime})=\min_{1\leq j<m}\kappa^{\min}_{j}.

Similarly, define
\kappa^{\max}
by considering the largest eigenvalue of
W_{j,m-j}(\rho,\mu^{\prime})
for each
j.
From positive definiteness and since
m
is finite, we have
0<\kappa^{\min}\leq\kappa^{\max}.

Clearly, for each
1\leq j<m,
the eigenvalues of
Q_{m,j,j}(\rho,\mu^{\prime},0)
lie between
\kappa^{\min}
and
\kappa^{\max}.
Also, as
\mu\geq 0,
Lemma
A.7
shows that the eigenvalues of
\hat{Q}_{m}(\mu)
lie between
-m\mu
and
m\mu.
Finally, observe that
Q_{m,j,j}(\rho,\mu^{\prime},\mu)=Q_{m,j,j}(\rho,\mu^{\prime},0)+\hat{Q}_{m}(\mu)
and that all the three matrices in this relation are symmetric. Therefore, by Weyl’s inequality, it follows that all eigenvalues of
Q_{m,j,j}(\rho,\mu,\mu^{\prime})
lie between
\kappa^{\min}-m\mu
and
\kappa^{\max}+m\mu
for all
j.

Now, set
\delta=\kappa^{\min}/(2m)
and
\kappa=\kappa^{\max}+\kappa^{\min}/2.
The desired result is then easy to see.
∎

Lemma A.9
.

Let
m\geq 2
and
\rho,\mu^{\prime}\in[0,1)
be such that
1+(m-2)\rho-(m-1)\mu^{\prime}>0.
Then, there exists
\delta\equiv\delta(m,\rho,\mu^{\prime})>0
and
\kappa\equiv\kappa(m,\rho,\mu^{\prime})>0
such that, for any
\mu\in[0,\delta],
the matrix
Q_{m,1,1}(\rho,\mu^{\prime},\mu),
defined as in (
6.2
), is positive definite and its maximum eigenvalue is bounded from above by
\kappa.

Proof.

Let
m_{2}=m-1
and
W_{1,m_{2}}(\rho,\mu^{\prime})
be defined as in (
6.1
). Let
\kappa^{\min}\equiv\kappa^{\min}(m,\rho,\mu^{\prime})
and
\kappa^{\max}\equiv\kappa^{\max}(m,\rho,\mu^{\prime})
be the smallest and largest eigenvalues of
W_{1,m_{2}}(\rho,\mu^{\prime}).
From the given conditions on
\rho,\mu^{\prime},
it is easy to see from Lemma
A.3
that the matrix
W_{1,m_{2}}(\rho,\mu^{\prime})
is positive definite; hence,
0<\kappa^{\min}\leq\kappa^{\max}.

Set
\delta=\kappa^{\min}/(2m)
and
\kappa=\kappa^{\max}+\kappa^{\min}/2.
By an application of Weyl’s inequality as in the proof of Lemma
A.8
, the desired result is easy to see.
∎

Lemma A.10
.

Fix
m\geq 2
and
u\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}.
Suppose one of the following conditions is true:

1.

\rho,\mu^{\prime}\in[0,1)
are such that
\rho>\mu^{\prime};
further,
\delta,\kappa>0
are such that the implications of Lemma
A.8
hold.

2.

\rho,\mu^{\prime}\in[0,1)
are such that
1+(m-2)\rho-(m-1)\mu^{\prime}>0;
further,
\delta,\kappa>0
are such that the implications of Lemma
A.9
hold.

For
\mu\in[0,\delta],
let
\bar{Z}_{\mu}\sim N(\bar{0},Q_{m,1,1}(\rho,\mu^{\prime},\mu)),
where
Q_{m,1,1}(\rho,\mu^{\prime},\mu)
is as defined in (
6.2
). Let
f_{\bar{Z}_{\mu}}
be the density of
\bar{Z}_{\mu}
and
g(\mu):=\mathbb{P}\{\bar{Z}_{\mu}\geq u\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{2m}\}.
Then, the following statements are true.

1.

g(\mu)
is continuous in
[0,\delta].

2.

g(\mu)
is differentiable on
(0,\delta).
Further, for any
\mu\in(0,\delta),

\left|\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu}[g(\mu)]\right|\leq u^{-2(m-1)}\exp\left[\frac{-2\phi_{m-1}(\rho,\mu^{\prime})u^{2}}{1+2\phi_{m-1}(\rho,\mu^{\prime})\mu}\right],

where
\Cr{c:GenB}\geq 0
is some constant which depends on
m
and
\phi_{m-1}(\rho,\mu^{\prime})
is defined as in (
3.10
).

Remark A.11
.

Since
\delta>0
is such that the implications of Lemmas
A.8
or
A.9
hold, the matrix
Q_{m,1,1}(\rho,\mu^{\prime},\mu)
is symmetric and positive definite and, hence,
\bar{Z}_{\mu}
is well defined for all
\mu\in[0,\delta].

Proof of Lemma
A.10
.

As the line of reasoning is similar to that in the proof of Lemma
A.4
, our arguments here are brief. We first recall the key intermediate steps from the proof of Lemma
A.4
and then provide their corresponding variants here.

•

There, the continuity and differentiability of
g(\mu)
were a simple consequence of the dominated convergence theorem once we obtained functions that dominated
|f_{\bar{Z}_{\mu}}|
and
\left|\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu}\left[f_{\bar{Z}_{\mu}}(\bar{z})\right]\right|
(see (
A.8
) and (
A.9
), respectively). These dominating functions were derived there by making use of a lower bound on the determinant and an upper bound on the maximum eigenvalue of
\textnormal{Var}[\bar{Z}_{\mu}]
(see (
A.7
) and the discussion below it).

•

Separately, after some algebra, the bound on
\left|\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu}[g(\mu)]\right|
there followed by using the formula for
u\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{2m}W^{-1}_{m,m}(\rho,\mu)u\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{2m}
given in (
A.21
), the fact that (
A.15
) holds, and finally bounds on
\bar{\Delta}_{u\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{2m-2}}
and the determinant of
\textnormal{Var}[\bar{Z}_{\mu}]
given in (
A.19
) and (
A.7
), respectively.

We now give the equivalent statements here. For brevity, let
Q_{m}(\rho,\mu^{\prime},\mu)\equiv Q_{m,1,1}(\rho,\mu^{\prime},\mu).
First, for each
\mu\in[0,\delta],
we have

\displaystyle|Q_{m}(\rho,\mu^{\prime},\mu)|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|W_{1,m-1}(\rho,\mu^{\prime})|^{2}\Big[1-\mu^{2}[\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{m}\,W^{-1}_{1,m-1}(\rho,\mu^{\prime})\,\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{m}^{\top}]^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle|W_{1,m-1}(\rho,\mu^{\prime})|^{2}\Big[1-\delta^{2}[\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{m}\,W^{-1}_{1,m-1}(\rho,\mu^{\prime})\,\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{m}^{\top}]^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|Q_{m}(\rho,\mu^{\prime},\delta)|

\displaystyle>

\displaystyle 0,

where the first relation follows from Lemma
A.6
, the second one holds due to the fact that
\mu\leq\delta,
the third one holds due to Lemma
A.6
again, while the last one follows since
Q_{m}(\rho,\mu^{\prime},\delta)
is positive definite, which itself holds due to Lemma
A.8
or
A.9
.

Separately, for any
\mu\in[0,\delta],
note that the largest eigenvalue of
Q_{m}(\rho,\mu^{\prime},\mu)
is bounded from above by some
\kappa>0,
independent of
\mu.

Lastly, by brute force,

(r_{1},r_{m-1}\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{m-1},r_{1},r_{m-1}\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{m-1})\,Q_{m}(\rho,\mu^{\prime},\mu)=\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{2m},

where

r_{j}=\frac{1+(m-1-j)\rho-(m-j)\mu^{\prime}}{1+(m-2)\rho-(m-1)[\mu^{\prime}]^{2}+\mu[m+(m-2)\rho-2(m-1)\mu^{\prime}]},

for
j=1,m-1
and
(r_{1},r_{m-1}\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{m-1},r_{1},r_{m-1}\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{m-1})
is the vector whose first entry is
r_{1},
the next
m-1
entries are
r_{m-1}
and so on. Note that, since
m+(m-2)\rho-2(m-1)\mu^{\prime}=(m-1)(1-\mu^{\prime})+[1+(m-2)\rho-(m-1)\mu^{\prime}],
the given conditions on
\rho,\mu^{\prime}
ensure that both the numerators and denominators of
r_{1}
and
r_{m-1}
are positive. Therefore,

\displaystyle\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{2m}Q^{-1}_{m}(\rho,\mu^{\prime},\mu)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{2m}\frac{\min\{1-\mu^{\prime},1+(m-2)\rho-(m-1)\mu^{\prime}\}}{1+(m-2)\rho-(m-1)[\mu^{\prime}]^{2}+\mu[m+(m-2)\rho-2(m-1)\mu^{\prime}]}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{2m}\frac{\min\{1-\mu^{\prime},1+(m-2)\rho-(m-1)\mu^{\prime}\}}{1+(m-2)\rho-(m-1)[\mu^{\prime}]^{2}+\delta[m+(m-2)\rho-2(m-1)\mu^{\prime}]}

\displaystyle>

\displaystyle 0,

where the second relation holds because
\mu\in[0,\delta],
while the last one follows from the given conditions on
\rho
and
\mu^{\prime}
and since
\delta>0.
This shows that a condition equivalent to (
A.15
) and, consequently, to (
A.17
) holds here. Moving on, the above bound also shows that

\prod_{\ell=1}^{2m-2}\bar{\Delta}_{u\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{2m-2}}(\ell)\geq u^{2m-2}\left[\frac{\min\{1-\mu^{\prime},1+(m-2)\rho-(m-1)\mu^{\prime}\}}{1+(m-2)\rho-(m-1)[\mu^{\prime}]^{2}+\delta[m+(m-2)\rho-2(m-1)\mu^{\prime}]}\right]^{2m-2},

where
\bar{\Delta}_{u\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{2m-2}}
is the term equivalent to the one defined in (
A.18
). From this bound, it follows that a condition equivalent to (
A.19
) is true here. From the values of
r_{1}
and
r_{m-1}
given above, one can also see that

\displaystyle u\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{2m}\,Q_{m}^{-1}(\rho,\mu^{\prime},\mu)\,u\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{2m}^{\top}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2u^{2}[r_{1}+(m-1)r_{m-1}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2u^{2}[m+(m-2)\rho-2(m-1)\mu^{\prime}]}{1+(m-2)\rho-(m-1)[\mu^{\prime}]^{2}+\mu[m+(m-2)\rho-2(m-1)\mu^{\prime}]},

which gives a formula similar to (
A.21
); in fact, they match when
\rho=\mu^{\prime}.

From these relations, the desired bound is now easy to see. This completes the proof.
∎
